It also embraces in the prayer the doctrine of repentance, it censures the confidence of one's own justice, and it shows the justice of faith. Wherefore this chapter contains most useful doctrine, for instructing and confirming the pious concerning many articles.

To these things it clearly refutes the madness of the Jews, who deny that the Messiah has come, deny that it was necessary for the Messiah to suffer, contend that the Jews were going to have a worldly kingdom, etc. This passage most greatly convicts the Jews, and shows that they are stubborn against their own conscience.

But first let us consider the historical matters.Jeremiah ch. 25.&29.predicts that the exile of the Jewish people in Babylon is going to last for 70 years. This prophecy was dissolving the arguments of the false prophets, who were objecting the promises. God promised that He was going to preserve this worship and this kingdom, until the Messiah comes. Therefore they were judging that the temple could not be destroyed, nor the people to be led away, nor the polity of this people to be reduced into desolation. Jeremiah dilutes the argument, God has promised and will perform it, but otherwise than we imagine. Meanwhile he will also chastise and will miraculously restore those who have been chastised.

And indeed, this very reduction from Babylon was no less a miracle than the leading out from Egypt. And this return and the restoration of the temple proceeded slowly. For little by little others returned after others, and the building was hindered during the whole time of Cyrus, and thereafter for 42 years until the second year of Longimanus.

Reading these things, we ought to consider how horrible is the wrath of God against sins, since he has exacted such great punishments from this his own people. It must also be considered how small the church is.

Again, consolation is proposed to the pious. They see that in such great dangers the church can nevertheless be oppressed by no power of the world, but is ruled and preserved divinely. We see this also, that the pious counsels of good princes are hindered in many ways, just as the kindness of Cyrus was hindered in the time of Cyrus, and afterwards in the time of Darius, whom the Greeks call so.

WHEN therefore the seventieth year of the exile was now being spent, in the first year of Cyrus, after the capture of Babylon, mindful of the promises and hoping for the restoration of the people, Daniel remembers the prophecy of Jeremiah, and is moved with great force to pray.

This passage signifies that the prophets sought consolation from reading, and made progress.

It also warns that, although God had promised the return, he nevertheless willed that this benefit be sought by praying, and that the faith of the saints be exercised by invocation. As Christ says, ask, and you shall receive. Likewise, call upon me in the day of tribulation, and I will deliver you, etc.

Even though God has promised bodily or spiritual benefits, he nevertheless wills that faith be exercised by prayers, and he wills that repentance grow. As Zechariah says, Turn to me, and I will turn to you, etc. And Daniel prays concerning the restoring of the church. Thus let us also be affected with true sorrow on account of the calamities of the church, and let us pray that God may increase, govern, and preserve it.

The security of those who are not at all affected by the calamities of the church is most displeasing to God. As it is complained Amos cap.6. Woe to the wealthy, etc. who suffer nothing over the contrition of Joseph. And Daniel understands the magnitude of the calamities, Therefore he recites such a sad complaint about the sins of the people and about the punishment.

I said, however, that the doctrine of repentance is delivered here. Daniel confesses the sins of the people, and attributes to God the praise of justice, because He has justly punished the people. Then he seeks the remission of sins, and the restoration of the people. It is therefore true contrition, to acknowledge the wrath of God against our sins, to be terrified on account of the wrath of God, to grieve that we have offended God, to attribute to Him the praise, because He justly punishes us, and to be obedient in punishments.

He recites this confession here, To you O Lord justice, but to us confusion of face. This is the voice of true contrition. Just as Psalm 32.also teaches, I said I will confess against myself my injustice to the Lord, etc.

And Psalm 51.To you alone I have sinned, and done evil before you, that you may be justified in your words, and overcome when you are judged. That is, I acknowledge that I am only a sinner, and guilty before you. That you may be justified, that is, that I may attribute to you the praise of one who justly punishes and chastises. And you overcome when hypocrites judge you, namely those who think God is too harsh, too hard against this miserable nature, they accuse the judgment of God, not they can so submit themselves to him, that they might acknowledge that they are justly punished.

This is a huge roaring and a secret evil in the souls of men. Therefore Paul several times inculcates these two things, that God is just and justifying, that is, let us attribute to him the praise of justly punishing, let us not arrogate justice to ourselves, but let us believe that God is the justifier, that is, that we are pronounced just in such a way, when he himself has mercy on us, he justifies us. It is easy to attribute the praise of justice to God in words, but to acknowledge from the heart the magnitude of our sins, and to feel that we are justly punished, and to seek pardon, these are arduous and difficult.

It is necessary, however, that this doctrine of contrition be diligently inculcated in the churches, so that they may truly acknowledge sins, and truly know that punishments and calamities are inflicted upon us on account of sins. Just as Jeremiah says cap. 5. Your sins have withheld good from you. And in this place, The Lord our God is just in all his works which he has done, namely, although he has exacted the most grievous punishments from us, etc.

Nor yet is it enough to acknowledge sins, to gaze upon punishments, but consolation must also be added. Therefore Daniel not only proposes the doctrine of contrition, but adds another part. He teaches by his own example to seek and to expect pardon, on account of mercy and the promises. As it is said elsewhere, Repent, and believe the Evan gel. Let faith approach contrition and contemplate the promises. As here Daniel does not only say, To thee O Lord righteousness, but adds, To thee mercy and propitiation, Thou hast promised that thou art willing to show mercy and to forgive. And he repeats this sentiment several times, expressly excluding merits, We do not ask in our righteousness, but on account of thy great mercy.

And he says by name, Hear O God the prayer of thy servant, and look upon thy desolate temple, for the Lord's sake, that is, hear, look upon for the Lord's sake. For among the Hebrews this particle is inserted in such a way that Daniel, speaking to God in the second person, Hear O God, look upon, etc., afterwards adds the third person at the end, For the Lord's sake. And therefore the Jews have interpreted it, for Abraham's sake.

But this interpretation is foreign and to be rejected. For Daniel knew that Abraham was not the Lord of the people, but the promised deliverer, as it is said in the Psalm, The Lord said to my Lord, sit at my right hand, &c.

Nor is there any doubt that here Daniel understood Christ saying, For the Lord's sake, that is, for Christ's sake. And similar discourses concerning contrition & faith exist everywhere in the prophets, asJer. chap. 14.If our iniquities testify against us, O Lord, do it for thy name's sake, we have sinned against thee, our Saviour in the time of trouble not leaving us, &c.

Let us also learn, and let us practice both doctrines, let us acknowledge our sins, & let us seek pardon, let public & private calamities stir us up, because these examples are set forth in the prophets, so that we may understand & consider the calamities of the church, & seek deliverance. For especially in the last times there will be no less calamity for the Church, than was that Babylonian exile of that people. And this prayer of Daniel is to be recited as a common prayer for the church in these times.

Here readers are to be admonished, that they may observe this discourse of Daniel, for the sake of confirming the opinion, which is handed down by Paul concerning the righteousness of faith. For here is set forth a certain & clear The testimony of Paul's doctrine. For certain crafty men attempt to bend Paul's opinion to the human judgment concerning the justice of works, and they shout that it is accepted by us too harshly. They deny those propositions, that all men are under sin, that our works do not merit reconciliation, that the nature of man is vicious, that it cannot satisfy the law, likewise that men are accounted just freely through mercy on account of Christ, not on account of works.

These paradoxes, they say, are foreign to common sense. Therefore they seek cavils to elude Paul's sayings, and thus they mitigate them. They confess that justice is taken away from ceremonies, but they say that on account of moral works men are just. Just as philosophers say that a man is just on account of civil morals. For because they have secure and idle minds, they admire that civil justice, and they think it satisfies the law of God, they think themselves to be without sin. Therefore they neither seek nor understand other consolations.

But in true terrors we experience that there is need of the doctrine concerning gratuitous mercy. And unless we are sustained by this faith, minds collapse into despairs and blasphemies, they cannot call upon God, they cannot expect or seek help.

Wherefore let not the pious allow themselves to be deceived by those secure ones, who overwhelm the doctrine of Paul concerning faith. Let us confess ourselves to be hearers of Paul, let us learn from him himself, let us not arrogate to ourselves judgment against his sayings.

Then let us consider this, When Paul preaches concerning righteousness before God, it is necessary that his opinion agree with the prophets and fathers. And so it is indeed, In order all the prophets teach the same thing. They all testify that men are under sin, they accuse human righteousness. And again they propose gratuitous mercy revealed in the promises by God on account of the promised deliverer. And they command this mercy to be received by faith, thus they affirm that we are forgiven.

This is the one & consenting voice of all the prophets. Therefore Peter says, To this all the prophets bear witness, that through his name everyone who believes in him receives remission of sins. And this is the liberation, promised by the Messiah, to be liberated from sin, & to have complete righteousness restored, & eternal life. But the Jews dream that they are righteous by the law, & they expect liberation not from sin, and from death, but from exile and poverty. The prophets and apostles censured this error.

That Paul agrees here with Daniel, however, is manifest. Daniel says, To thee O Lord righteousness, but to us confusion of face. He acknowledges the whole church to have sins. And he says by name, We do not pray in our righteousness, etc. Likewise, lest anyone invent a Synecdoche, that he speaks of many, not of all (as Origen corrupts the saying of Daniel) he says by name, When I was confessing my sins, and the sins of my people. But so it is, the pious alone feel the burden of sin, others secure despise it.

As therefore Paul removes merit, and contends that all are under sin, likewise, he inculcates the particle freely, so here Daniel teaches, saying, Not in our righteousness, etc. Then, as Paul says, that sins are remitted through grace, so here he cries out (we flee to the mercy which is great) and he names the mediator, saying, For the Lord's sake. Furthermore, as often as mention is made of mercy, faith is to be understood correlatively, by which we determine that mercy happens to us. Altogether therefore Daniel agrees with Paul.

Thus it is profitable in the other prophets to consider the consenting opinions, as in the Psalms. That all are guilty, these sayings testify, There shall not be justified in thy sight every living man. Item, Who understands faults. Item, If thou shalt observe iniquities O Lord, O Lord who shall sustain?

Again concerning mercy and gratuitous remission, I said I will confess against myself my iniquity . Thus Isaiah, having embraced both parts in one verse, accuses all, and proposes mercy, All we like sheep have gone astray, every one to his own way, and the Lord has laid on him the iniquities of us all.

Thus the prophets acknowledged the coming deliverer, that he would bring a far greater benefit than pleasures and riches, namely that he would destroy the works of the devil, that is sin, by which human nature is oppressed, the tyranny of the devil, and eternal death.

Wherefore reading Paul, let us look with our mind at all the sermons of the fathers and prophets, from the very beginning, and let us think about the magnitude of sin, and let us know that the benefits which the son of God brought are not light. I have added these things briefly, so that students may be strengthened against Sophistry, by which some cavil and corrupt the sayings of Paul concerning the righteousness of faith.

THE PROPHECY CONCERNING THE 70. weeks.

THERE FOLLOWS the prophecy concerning the seventy weeks delivered to Daniel, which is a clear testimony against the Jews, concerning the time of the coming of Christ, and concerning the end of the Jewish polity. And Daniel has embraced many things briefly, as happens in prophecies. When he speaks of the coming of Christ, he simultaneously warns of the benefits which he is about to bring. He interprets the promises, namely, that it will come to pass that sin is abolished, and perpetual righteousness is begun, and a new and eternal life. He adds also that Christ is to die, and yet that he will give his benefits to many of the people, and afterwards there will be an end of this Jewish polity. This is the sum of the prophecy, in which it sufficiently appears that testimonies concerning many articles are proposed against the Jews.

First, this passage refutes the error concerning the law being retained, and concerning the political kingdom of Christ. If there will be perpetual righteousness, likewise, if Christ is to be killed, it follows that the Mosaic law is not to be retained, nor will there be a worldly kingdom.

SECONDLY, he delivers testimony concerning the passion of Christ.

THIRDLY, since the polity has now ceased, so that it has no leaders, no prophets, no distinctions of tribes, it is established that the saying of Jacob has been fulfilled, The scepter shall not be taken away from Judah, until the Savior has come. It is necessary, therefore, that the Savior has come.

And the length of time after the destruction of Jerusalem must be diligently considered, and compared with the duration of the law before the destruction. Nor is it consistent to wish that God would let the polity lie destroyed longer than it flourished, if He still approved of it. Now there are years from the given law until the destruction of Jerusalem, with the city captured by Titus, one thousand five hundred sixty-four. Now on the contrary the polity lies destroyed for 1467 years, up to this year, in which we now count from the birth of Christ 1542. Since therefore the Jewish polity lies destroyed for so long, it is necessary that the prophecy of Jacob has been fulfilled, and that Christ has come.

And God willed this polity to be destroyed, both so that it might be an example of wrath against the impious, and because the persuasion could not have been removed from men concerning the justice of the law, if that polity had endured. He willed, however, God to show that those rites are not righteousness. Item, that the righteousness of the law is not the righteousness of which the promises preach. Item, he wished to make the Gentiles equal to the Jews, so that we might understand clearly the gratuitous reconciliation.

Furthermore, the number of years was also revealed to Daniel, up to the preaching and resurrection of Christ. For afterwards, after the mission of the Holy Spirit and the begun preaching of the Gospel, the Jewish people cease to be the people of God. Therefore he does not speak of the year of the destruction, but of the prior ones up to the resurrection of Christ and thereabouts.

He names, however, seventy weeks, which make four hundred and ninety years. For they are to be understood as weeks of years. For in the scriptures there are only weeks of years or of days. It is manifest, however, that the weeks of days cannot be numbered here. For seventy weeks of days make only one year, four months, and 14 days. At that time it is sufficiently certain that neither had the building of Jerusalem been completed, nor had the whole people been brought back yet. It is necessary, therefore, that weeks of years be numbered here, as Leviticus 25.mention is made of weeks of years, There are therefore 70. weeks of years to be understood, namely 490. years.

SUBTRACTION OF WEEKS.

It is manifest that the angel wishes to reveal the time, and to predetermine it after the Babylonian exile up to Christ, either being born or preaching, so that both that age might be admonished concerning the coming of the Messiah, and we might be confirmed, and might know for certain, with the Jewish polity having been utterly destroyed, that the Messiah had already appeared.

And for the Jews indeed after Daniel the observation of the years was easy, especially since among that people the priests diligently noted the times, and many were long-lived. Nehemiah, who had seen Daniel as an old man when he was a youth, saw Alexander as an old man. Afterwards in the time of Antiochus many old men were killed. Yet God preserved some posterity. Simeon, who carried the infant Christ in his bosom, saw as a youth old men who had seen Maccabeus. Such men at the time when Christ was born understood the years here predetermined to square exactly with the advent of Christ.

But the accommodation would be easier for us also, if the years of the Persian reign were more certainly noted. For backward from the birth of Christ to Alexander the series of years has been observed and written down with moderate diligence. For there exists in Ptolemy the time noted from the death of Alexander to the first year of the reign of Augustus after the death of Antony. Namely 294 years. The place is at the end of the third book, ἀπὸ δὲ τῆς ἀλεξάνδρου τελευτῆς μέχρι τῆς Αύγούστου βασιλείας 294.

Therefore if you wish to have the beginning of Augustus after Caesar was killed, 12 years will have to be subtracted. And there will be from the death of Alexander to the killing of Caesar, 282. To these add the 42 years of Augustus, and there will be to the birth of Christ from the death of Alexander, according to this computation, 324 years. or thereabouts. Add the years of Christ up to his baptism, thus there will be 354 years. Add the years collected backward from the death of Alexander, up to the second year of Longimanus according to the Greek calculation, 136 years. Thus there will be 490 years from the second year of Longimanus up to the baptism of Christ. So many years are the seventy weeks.

But why we begin this computation from the second year of Longimanus, we shall say later. But if it does not please anyone to begin from Longimanus, but rather from the Monarchy of Cyrus after Babylon was captured, the weeks of Daniel correspond to the time when Christ is born, not when He is baptized.

And so that the reader may understand that this computation, which I have mentioned, is probable, let another annotation of the times be compared. It is certain that Alexander began to reign in the one hundred and eleventh Olympiad. It is handed down by great consensus, and it is consistent, on account of the magnitude of the deeds performed by Alexander, that the time was diligently annotated.

Then Josephus writes that in the 184. Olympiad Herod was created king. There will therefore be, according to this computation, between Alexander and Herod 292. years. That time correctly corresponds to the years which I took above from Ptolemy. For here the beginning of Alexander is added. Furthermore, from Alexander until the destruction of the temple made by King Nabogdonosor, there are 256. years. If, however, as the ancients wrote, the destruction of the temple was made in the 47. Olympiad. But if you again subtract the time of the exile, namely seventy years, there will be from the 70. year after the destruction of the temple, until Herod was created king 478. Add moreover the years To the birth of Christ 30. Thus you will have years of weeks 490. & a little more. But for good minds this suffices, that they see the time which is between the return of the people from Babylon and Christ to agree with the prophecy of Daniel, even if they do not have the most exact calculation.

However, how little is lacking in these calculations? The angel testifies that after 490 years he will show the Messiah to this people. It is certain, however, that from the liberation of the people to the birth of Christ there are so many years, and not many more, or fewer. For the time from Augustus to Alexander is certain. This also is certain, that the Persian Monarchy did not last more than two hundred years. Therefore the Messiah, the son of God, came, and the event corresponded to the prophecy of Daniel.

But since the angel expressly says that Christ will confirm the covenant with many for one and a half weeks, and then the sacrifices will cease. If we wish the weeks to be continuous, the weeks will have to be counted to the baptism of Christ, which will happen if they are counted from the second year of Longimanus, in which the prophecies of Zechariah and Haggai were published concerning the return of the people, which had been Previously hindered, now with the impediments removed, the return was granted. For the angel commands to count from the going forth of the word concerning the return of the people, and the restoration of Jerusalem.

Furthermore, others have accepted these words in different ways. Some interpret them concerning the edicts of kings, the first of which was that of Cyrus, which, in his absence, was hindered in the Scythian war. The second was issued after 42 years by Longimanus, who in Ezra is called both Artaxerxes and Darius. For these were common names of kings, as at Rome, Caesar, Augustus. And Herodotus in the sixth book says that by the word Darius is signified the same thing that is signified by the word for one who restrains. He says Xerxes is a warrior, Artaxerxes, a great warrior.

Furthermore, at that time when Longimanus renewed the decree concerning the granting of the return, at the same time a revelation was made to Haggai and Zechariah concerning the liberation and building that would proceed thereafter. Wherefore from here, from the second year of Longimanus, the computation is begun by some, for the reason that then the word went forth both from God and from Longimanus, so that the liberation and building would not be hindered thereafter. And if we take the beginning from here, there will be continuous weeks up to Christ preaching.

But if anyone prefers to begin from the decree of Cyrus, he will arrive at the birth of Christ.

Both opinions or calculations are harmonious, and as far as the sum of the matter is concerned, each calculation confirms the same thing, it shows that between the liberation of the people and Christ there are 490. years. When this is declared, the pious know that afterwards, with the Jewish polity destroyed, no other Messiah is to be expected. For this is the principal point treated in this prophecy, that there should exist a testimony that it was necessary for the Messiah to be born and seen while the Mosaic polity was still enduring. Just as the same verse teaches, And you Bethlehem, little among the thousands of Judah, out of you shall come forth a ruler in Israel. It was necessary for the Messiah to be born in Bethlehem while the Mosaic polity was standing.

It is evident, however, that now the entire Mosaic polity lies destroyed, and that Jerusalem and Bethlehem are not so much towns as huts or dens of Arabian brigands, for one thousand four hundred and sixty-seven years. It is necessary, therefore, that the Messiah has been born.

What can the Jews say against these things, if only they do not judge the writings of the prophets to be fabulous? But because the Jewish nation loves its own darkness, let us read for ourselves, and weigh the sayings of the prophets, that we may kindle and confirm our faith. Meanwhile let us also give thanks to God, because He cares for the church, because He has from time to time illustrated the doctrine, because He has from time to time instructed us with certain testimonies.

I COME therefore to the computation, but at the beginning the catalog of the Persian Kings must be reviewed.

CATALOG OF THE PERSIAN KINGS according to the Greeks.

Cyrus 29

Cambyses 7. years, months 5

Darius son of Hystaspes 36

Xerxes 20

Artaxerxes Longimanus 40

Darius Nothus 19

Artaxerxes Mnemon 40

Ochus 26

Arsames 3

Darius the last 6

This series is known to all the learned, who have read the Greek histories. And the ignorance of the recent Jews is to be blamed, who pretend that there were no more Persian kings than four, and on account of this ignorance, they lose one hundred years from the series of the years of the world.

There exists, however, another catalog in a fragment of Metasthenes, in which the appellations correspond more with Ezra. And although the authority of Metasthenes is slight among some, nevertheless because it agrees with Ezra and Philo, I do not despise it, especially since the first three about which the catalogs disagree are recounted and named in the same way by Ezra and Philo, both catalogs agree concerning the rest, and in the former ones a reconciliation is easy.

Darius the Mede and Cyrus reigned together for years 2

Cyrus alone 22

Artaxerxes Ahasuerus 20

Darius Artaxerxes Longimanus 37

Darius Nothus 19

Artaxerxes Mnemon 55

Ochus 26

Arsames 4

Darius 6

The first Darius, who is called the Mede, is unknown to the Greeks, and some think he is Cyaxares. It is certain, however, from Daniel, that he reigned at the beginning of this Darius the Mede with Cyrus. And indeed Daniel himself distinguished this Darius from Cyrus, he says that Darius is a Mede, but Cyrus a Persian. Therefore these things do not conflict with the Greeks, who, omitting the Medes, count the Persians.

Metasthenes passes over Cambyses, as I think, because he did not reign after the death of his father. But when his father Cyrus was leading an army into Scythia, he created him king at home according to the usual custom, so that in his absence, struggles concerning the succession might not arise.

Artaxerxes is therefore Ahasuerus, he whom the Greeks name Darius the son of Hystaspes, which is understood to be so from history. Because Philo narrates that this Ahasuerus recovered Assyria and Babylon, which had revolted. That is narrated in Herodotus concerning Darius the son of Hystaspes. And Herodotus writes that Darius had a concubine most dear of all, Aristona, which appellation agrees with the appellation Esther.

From Ezra it is manifest that Artaxerxes succeeded Ahasuerus, to him is added the surname by Metasthenes and Philo, Longimanus, or μακροχειρ. Nor is there any doubt that this is the same Longimanus, whom the Greeks name thus. But why is Xerxes omitted here? I judge that this happens again for this reason, because with Xerxes leading an army far into Europe, it was necessary for a colleague king to be co-opted at home, lest seditions should arise in the east if no one were in charge with royal authority after the departure of Xerxes. Thereafter the series agrees with the Greeks.

Now I return to the weeks. Since the building had been hindered in the time of Cyrus and Ahasuerus, as is evident from Ezra, afterwards under the third king, namely Longimanus, the building was permitted again. For as Cyrus, so here Longimanus was more kind toward the Jews than Ahasuerus, although Esther also swayed him, who perhaps accustomed Longimanus to true piety by domestic discipline. And that Longimanus had embraced the doctrine of the people of Israel concerning God is evident from the fact that the principal governors in the court were two men who truly worshipped God and were leaders of the Israelites, Ezra and Nehemiah.

I have already said often, however, that at the same time Haggai and Zechariah began to prophesy that the building of the temple would proceed. They think the angel speaks of this prophecy when he says, From the going forth of the word, that Jerusalem should be built again, etc.

Let us therefore establish the beginning of the weeks from the second year of Longimanus.

The event itself adds weighty testimony to this calculation. For before, the works had been interrupted. Now for the first time they proceeded, and in the sixth year of Longimanus the restoration of the temple was completed. Therefore some think it is said in John, that the temple was built in forty-six years. For there are from Cyrus until the second year of Longimanus, 42 years, during which the building was hindered, even though the temple had been begun. But afterwards under Longimanus, by a new decree, the building having been granted, the temple was completed within four years. Thus there will be 46 years from the first beginning.

There are, however, according to Metasthenes from the second year of Longimanus, until the beginning of Alexander, 145 years.

Alexander reigned for seven years after Darius was killed.

146. after the death of Alexander, until the beginning of the leader Judas Maccabaeus, as is clearly evident from the book of Maccabees.

127. from the beginning of Judas Maccabaeus, until the beginning of Herod.

30. years from the beginning of Herod until the birth of Christ.

In this calculation after Alexander, we have followed the book of the Maccabees, which counts the years from Alexander until the Maccabees. Nor indeed is there any doubt that there was singular diligence in the Jewish people in noting the series of years, wherefore even if this series is somewhat shorter than the other handed down above from the catalogue of the Olympiads, nevertheless I consider this one not to be spurned on account of the authority of the book of the Maccabees.

But let us collect the sum.

310. from the beginning of Alexander until the birth of Christ, add the years from Longimanus until Alexander 145. & the years from the birth of Christ until his baptism 30. With these years collected, you will have from the second year of Longimanus until Christ preaching, years 485. Now 69 weeks contain years 483. But the angel says expressly, until Christ the leader, that is, preaching, there are weeks 69. and then he adds a repetition, saying, After sixty-two weeks Christ shall be killed, at which place from the previous verse must also be repeated There shall be seven weeks, as he had said before, seven and sixty-two, which are sixty-nine. For in the last week, which is only halved, Christ performed his ministry, was crucified, and resurrected, sent the holy spirit, and publicly published the preaching of the Gospel, with the apostles sent to the nations. Therefore the angel says to Daniel concerning Christ, He shall confirm the covenant with many, that is, he shall begin his kingdom, he shall preach the Gospel, he shall become a victim for the human race, resurrected he shall give the holy spirit, he shall send the apostles, that they may teach the Gospel, he shall endow many with eternal salvation in that half-week. For before the last week was entirely completed after the baptism, Christ, namely in the fourth year after his baptism, was crucified and resurrected. In this calculation the weeks are not interrupted, up to the preaching and resurrected Christ. And the angel seems rather to have signified a continuous series.

Nor is the time of the destruction of Jerusalem to be included in the number of the weeks itself, because after Christ's resurrection and the promulgation of the Gospel with the holy spirit sent, when Since the Jewish polity was opposed to the Gospel, it was rejected. As it is said in the text, And there shall not be to him a people, namely that which was. Furthermore, the angel signifies that it will come to pass in the last week, that the covenant may be confirmed, that is, that Christ may be made a victim, and having been resurrected, may begin a new kingdom. Since these things are attributed to the last week, it is manifest that the angel does not include the remaining time up to Titus, or the destruction of Jerusalem. Already before this, this polity had ceased to be the polity of God, when it cast away the son of God.

You have, reader, not intricate calculations, which have been taken from testimonies not to be despised. But perhaps contentious men demand such a deduction, in which the boundaries of the weeks might coincide at the very joints of the times, to the birthday of Christ, or the day of baptism. Although I judge that the prophecy coincides exactly, yet those who require this scrupulous diligence do not do this to explain the matter, but to elude those things which have been moderately explained. Wherefore let them either hand down closer ones, or cease to cavil at these, if they have any candor. But this suffices for the pious, that they see that the time of the coming of the Messiah has been predetermined, namely after the return from Babylon About the years 490. And that it was necessary for the Messiah to be born, while the Jewish polity was still enduring. These testimonies are to be observed by us here especially. And although it is difficult to establish the joints of the times, yet this appears, that these years agree 490. either from Cyrus to the birth of Christ, or from Longimanus to the baptism of Christ. For the series of years after Alexander is better known. From Alexander, if the years are sought backwards according to the Greeks to Longimanus, the computation will square even more. It is agreed that Alexander began to reign in Olympiad 111.

Now if you count the years backwards, it is certain from Polybius, a most diligent and faithful writer, that Athens was captured by Lysander in Olympiad 92. you will therefore have years 76. or thereabouts, for it is difficult to establish the joint itself. But that war moved in the time of Pericles, a little before Darius Nothus, lasted years 27. If therefore you join the years from Alexander to the beginning of the Peloponnesian war, you will have years 103. These things are handed down by the Greeks with certain faith. The remaining time is that of the reign of Longimanus, who reigned about forty years. It is consistent therefore, what we previously posited from Megasthenes, from Longimanus that there are 145 years to Alexander.

Thus the collation of histories shows that all these computations, which I have reviewed, agree, and do not wander far, even if the very joints or moments of time are not shown most exactly. The sum of the matter, however, is held, and I hope that learned and candid men will approve both our zeal in inquiring into the series of times and the collation of histories, and indeed the computations themselves.

Readers must be warned again to consider what utility this address of the angel and this testimony brings to the pious. Many ravings of the Jews are clearly refuted. For since the Messiah had to be born while the Jewish polity was enduring, as is said here, it is necessary that the Messiah has been born and exhibited to us. The dreams about a worldly empire of the Messiah, which the Jews still promise themselves, are also refuted. We are taught about the passion of Christ, and about the true benefits, about the abolishing of sin, the abolishing of death, about the restitution of righteousness and eternal life. Finally, the angel has encompassed the sum of the Gospel in a few verses. Therefore, this address of the angel is profitable to pious minds, for confirming faith, because they see the events which in the Gospel are described, to be in agreement with the preaching.

Now I shall briefly run through the text. Seventy weeks are cut off upon your people, and upon your holy city, that is, 490 years are predetermined or appointed. For that is without doubt the meaning of the word which Daniel uses. For so long, he says, this people and your city, that is, the city and the polity, will be preserved until that time when transgression will be finished, and sin will be closed, that is, when through the advent of the Messiah sin will be blotted out, when Christ will be the victim, and when the Gospel will be revealed, for it will be blotted out in those who are about to believe the Gospel, or who will afterwards be members of the people of God. For although in this mortal life there remains in those who have been reborn through the Gospel the weakness of the carnal nature resisting the law of God, and the death of the body, nevertheless remission is preached, and sin is, as is said here, closed, covered by pardon, and the kingdom of Christ is begun, a new knowledge of God, a new righteousness, and eternal life, in those who are converted to Christ.

And iniquity will be expiated. Here the angel explains the former parts. How will be finished? sin? Yes, of course, because an expiation will be made, the promised sacrifice of the Messiah will be made, by which alone the eternal Father will be appeased.

And everlasting righteousness shall come. It is customary for the prophets, when they speak of the kingdom of Christ, to embrace the entire restoration. With sin abolished, with death abolished, at that time a new and everlasting righteousness and life will be exhibited, which in this mortal life is begun through the Gospel and the giving of the Holy Spirit, but afterwards, after the resurrection, it will be complete. And prophecy and vision will be sealed, that is, it will be completed and finished, namely; because the promises in the prophecies concerning the Messiah will be exhibited.

And the holy of holies shall be anointed, that is, the high priest and king anointed by the eternal Father, that is, sent, so that he may perform the office of high priest and king, and filled with the Holy Spirit. This, I say, the high priest and king, the Messiah himself, will then be present, and will perform his office, he will teach the Gospel, he will offer his own sacrifice, he will show himself to be the Lord, through whom God wills sin and death to be abolished, he will sanctify the believers, that is, he will remit their sins, and will pour out the Holy Spirit upon them. He clearly affirms, therefore, that then will come the promised Messiah. But as high priests and kings were anointed, so also the son of God, when he is sent, is said to be anointed, that is, to be sent to the function of the priesthood and royal office, and to be imbued with the holy spirit, asJohn 6 he says, Him the father God has sealed, that is, he has peculiarly chosen this one alone for so great an office, and sent him. Huge and ineffable is the wrath of God against the sin of the human race, nor was it to be appeased by any other victim, nor in any other way, except by the intercession of the son, who humbled himself, that he might become a victim. This one victim the father sealed, chose, approved, and imbued with the holy spirit, so that from his fullness others might draw, as the Baptist says. Thus far, therefore, he said these things, The promised deliverer will be born and will be exhibited, and will perform his office, after 490 years, while the city of Jerusalem and your polity still endure. This sentiment most abundantly consoled the pious then in public dangers. For what would be more desirable to us than to know that the church will remain in these very places, in which our posterity will be?

And you shall know and understand, From the going forth of the word concerning the return and the building of Jerusalem, that is, from the second year of Longimanus, in which God through the prophecy Haggai and Zechariah promise that the building will proceed, and in what year Longimanus issued the decree, so that the building might not be hindered. Then, says the angel, you shall count 69 weeks from the return of the people until Christ the leader, whether born or preaching. For since he says by name, until Christ the leader, he signifies one already performing the ministry, which he exercised openly after his baptism. I have warned above, however, that the weeks are not interrupted if they are counted from Longimanus to the baptism of Christ.

But why does he command 69 weeks to be counted, that is 483 years? He does not add the last one here, for he describes the time that precedes the baptism of Christ, and confirms the pious, so that they may know in the meantime that the city is not to be destroyed, even though great commotions and great changes of kingdoms were to follow, and it was to happen that Judea would be harshly shaken amidst the tumults of neighboring nations.

Therefore the consolation is added, Until Christ the leader, seven weeks and 62 weeks Jerusalem shall be inhabited, and the street and wall shall be built. The emphasis of the words in this brevity of the prophecy must be weighed. He says expressly, Jerusalem shall be inhabited, and it shall be built, 2 c.

that is, the city and the Jewish polity among them will not be destroyed. But the text adds (In the straits of the times) that is, and yet Judea will be variously vexed. First, in the restoration of the city and the temple, the neighboring nations in Samaria and the Persian prefects were opposing. Afterwards, Ochus cruelly raged against the Jews. After Alexander, when there were constant wars between the successors, especially between the Egyptians and the Syrians, Judea, being situated in the middle, was a prey to both. Amidst these dangers and evils, yet here the divine voice promises that the city of Jerusalem and the Jewish polity will not perish. Here let the pious reader consider the image of the true church of God and the labors of the church. The reduction from Babylon and the restoration of the polity was no less a miracle than the leading out of the people from Egypt had once been. First, the return was granted by Cyrus; some time later, Cyrus, departing from the kingdom, is killed in war.

With this patron lost, the benefit granted to the Jews is snatched away again. Here, in how much pain was Daniel? He was mourning the friendly king, he saw that the enemies of true doctrine would congratulate themselves on the death of Cyrus, and would confirm the power of the impious faction again, as are the turns in courts.

These things did not lightly torment the mind of the most wise man Daniel. But he nevertheless sustained himself with this consolation, he knew that the promises of God are certain, true, and valid, even if the events are to be wondered at, and do not correspond to our conjectures. For by this doctrine we ought to be instructed in the church, that we may seek and await the events by faith. Finally, after the rebuilding was granted again, how many various miseries, how many various tumults intervened?

Thus the church is always exercised by unexpected and various terrors and difficulties. We now, in the restoration of the church of God, in the schools and in the temples, with what difficulties are we conflicted? Impediments are thrown in our way from every side at home and abroad, but let us console ourselves with that ancient example of the restoration of Jerusalem.

And after sixty-two weeks, Christ shall be cut off. Here repeat from the previous verse seven, just as he said before, After 67. & two, that is, after 69. weeks, namely after 483. years Christ shall be killed, and shall become a victim for the church. Before he said that the city and the polity were to remain until the leader Christ comes. Now he expressly preaches about the passion of Christ. And The testimony concerning the death of the Messiah is clear, such as those which are also in Isaiah ch. 53. He was cut off from the land of the living. Item, He shall lay down his soul as an offering for sin.

Since these things are clearly spoken, they must not be depraved by foreign interpretations, as the blind and wicked Jews do, who, because they dream that the Messiah will occupy the empires of the world and will reign in the manner of Alexander or Augustus, corrupt the sayings concerning the passion of the Messiah. For they do not understand what kind of liberation from sin and from death was promised to the first fathers. They imagine childishly that the Messiah is sent only so that in this mortal, miserable, and fragile life he may heap riches and pleasures upon the descendants of Abraham. Nor do they consider that a far greater liberation was promised to the first fathers, who understood what things human nature had lost, namely, the firm knowledge of God, perpetual justice, and life. Therefore Isaiah calls the Messiah the father of eternal life. And chapter 53 when he had preached concerning his death, he adds a sermon concerning his resurrection and eternal kingdom.

Therefore, having rejected the corruptions of the Jews, let us hear the angel himself, τὸ ρητὸν let us hold fast, let us be disciples of the divine voice, and let us understand it rightly, Let us not ourselves invent interpretations, with the meaning of the words omitted. He speaks of a person when he names the Messiah, and of that very one whom he named the holy of holies in the next verse, he says here, he shall be cut off. Just as Isaiah also says, He was cut off from the land of the living.

Let us remember, therefore, that a certain and clear testimony is delivered here concerning the death and punishment of Christ. But when we read or hear this, let us also think of the circumstances, taken from other places of the prophets, and let us think also of the resurrection. He is subjected to punishment for this reason, that he may become a victim for the sins of the human race. Isaiah declared this in more words in that chapter which we have already cited so often, Because he shall lay down his soul as a sacrifice for sin, he shall see a long-lived seed. In this statement, death, the reason for the victim, the resurrection, and the kingdom are comprehended.

The Law had victims before, but they did not appease the wrath of God. This one victim appeases it. This is truly the ineffable counsel of God, but it shows the magnitude of both the wrath of God and his mercy. For since the virtue or obedience of no man could appease the wrath of God, except the obedience and death of the one Son, it is necessary that the true, immense, and in enarrable wrath be. Again, the force of love is immense, because He gave the Son to us, because by such a pledge, He brings us back from eternal torments, and makes us certain that we are loved by Him. As the Son Himself says, God so loved the world, that He gave His only begotten Son, etc.

Finally, in this brief saying, let the reader consider the entire doctrine of the Gospel concerning the benefits which have been given to us on account of Christ's death. Let him also consider the doctrine concerning faith and invocation. For those benefits are to be received by faith, so the eternal God the Father is truly appeased and propitious to you, when by faith you determine on account of the Son's death that He is propitious to you, and by this faith you invoke Him. He wills that you bring this faith into your invocation, as Ro. 5. it is said, Through Him we have access. Also to the Hebrews 4. Having a high priest Jesus the Son of God, who can sympathize with our infirmity. Let us approach with confidence to the throne of grace. Let this be enough to have touched upon here briefly, which are explained more copiously in the Epistle to the Romans and elsewhere, as often as it is spoken of justification.

And there shall not be a people to him. In the text there is even greater brevity in this member. By these words (And not to him) or (And not unto him) it is necessary to understand that there is no glory for him, or people. And, so that we might rather understand it concerning the people, the following sentence concerning the destruction of the city and temple moved us. This people will soon cease to be the church of the Messiah and of God. Just as it is briefly said in John, And his own did not receive him. The Jewish nation was rejected on account of the contempt of the Gospel, And the Church was gathered again for God from the remnants of this people and from the nations, as is often predicted in the sermons of the Gospel, and afterwards Paul disputes concerning the calling of the nations.

And the people of the leader who is coming will destroy the city and the sanctuary, that is, the army of Titus, And its end is a flood, and even to the end of the war a defined or established desolation, or devastation, that is, Then the end of the Jewish people and of the Jewish polity will follow, from which it will not emerge again, as it returned before from the Babylonian exile, but as if overwhelmed by a flood, it will perish utterly.

And indeed the war will be sharp and difficult. For the Romans did not oppress the Jews without many and various struggles, but after it has been fought out, a perpetual devastation and solitude is to remain. Never the Levitical priesthood, never the kingdom or principality of Judah, never the polity The Mosaic city of Jerusalem will never be restored, but, as Hosea says, henceforth the nations will be called to the fellowship of the people of God, and all who embrace the Gospel of the Messiah will be the people of God, as is said in Rom. 9. & 10..

It is evident that the Jews, after Titus, attempted several times to restore their polity. Under Hadrian, a multitude of Jews had gathered, collected from everywhere, and were invading their fatherland with arms. But it was routed and destroyed by Hadrian. Afterwards, Julian, out of hatred for the Christian name, granted the Jews permission to build the city and temple again. The work was begun, a huge sum of money was collected, but the building was hindered by an earthquake, and a great multitude of men was crushed by the shattered stones and rubble. Nazianzenus also narrates that figures of the cross were prodigiously impressed everywhere on the garments of the people. Terrified, therefore, by heavenly signs, the Jews abandoned the work they had begun, because God has decreed that the Jewish polity should not be restored. He wishes an example of his wrath to be seen by all nations, because he punishes the contempt of his Son with horrible punishments. He also wishes the polity of Moses to be buried, so that the opinion concerning ceremonies, that men are righteous by the law or ceremonies, may not be confirmed.

And he shall confirm the covenant or testament with many. He adds a declaration to the previous discourse, because it is not enough to speak of the death of Christ and of the destruction of the people; testimonies must also be added concerning the resurrection, concerning the kingdom, and concerning the benefits of Christ, likewise concerning the remnant of the people who are delivered, just as the other prophets promised, as Isaiah says, that the remnant would be saved, that is, not the high priests, not the princes, not the strength of the people, but a small and despised assembly.

I shudder looking at this so sad example. Why do our high priests boast that they are the church, that their assembly cannot be cast off at all? Why do we trust that we are children of God because of our profession? If God did not spare the descendants of Abraham and of the prophets and of others, who performed the greatest deeds, what will become of our high priests, who despise the Gospel? What of us, if we indulge in depraved desires, ambition, envy, avarice, luxury, and only because of our profession arrogate to ourselves the august title of the church?

Thus, however, the angel says, The Messiah shall confirm the testament with many for one week, that is, in the final week Christ openly the Gospel. He will preach, he will add testimonies, he will call many to the Gospel, and to the fellowship of eternal life, he will become a victim, and having been resurrected, he will begin an eternal kingdom, he will show himself to be the giver of righteousness and eternal life, he will send the apostles to gather the church, he will endow them with the holy spirit.

These things will happen in a half-week, that is, with the seven years not completed. For from the baptism of Christ to the resurrection, there are three years and some months. There is also added the time of Pentecost, within this time, the covenant was confirmed, that is, the promises of the new testament were exhibited, the Gospel concerning the remission of sins was preached, the son of god was made a victim, resurrected, he began an eternal kingdom, he sent the apostles, he gave the holy spirit, he began to gather the church, which he sanctifies, governs, and is about to endow with life, light, righteousness, eternal glory. He calls these proper works and benefits of Christ the confirmation of the covenant, that is, the exhibition of the promises, which were made to the fathers and prophets. Just as it is said in Jeremiah,chapter 31.Behold the days shall come, and I will strike a new covenant with the house of Israel. I will give my law into their hearts. I will be their god, etc.

There is no doubt that the angel is speaking about this very covenant, which is described copiously in the prophets, when he says, He shall confirm the pact or covenant with many. Now let the pious reader consider the promise of the Gospel and of eternal life given from the very beginning, so that he may diligently ponder in his mind the benefits of the son of God, so that he may acknowledge, and truly call upon this Messiah, and enjoy the liberation granted through him.

And in the middle of the week, he shall cause the sacrifice and the oblation to cease. O sad prophecy, sad voice to the Jews who are ignorant of the Gospel, and who admire and love their ceremonies, and the most beautiful rites handed down by God, approved by the custom of so many prophets and leaders. For we love the customs of our fathers, and we bear it with difficulty that they are rejected and condemned. But to Daniel and the pious, who understand the Gospel, this voice of the angel is most welcome, which testifies that those ceremonies were formerly signs and types of the sacrifice to come, but with the Messiah himself exhibited, there would be an end to the signs, and with the things themselves exhibited, the shadows would cease. The Messiah began righteousness and eternal life, with the Gospel and the holy spirit having been given. Let us acknowledge and receive these huge benefits, and not the spectacles of the old ceremonies, in which these things were foreshadowed, let us inquire.

The opinion therefore is, In the middle of the week, after Christ has made his sacrifice, has risen again, and has given the holy spirit, afterwards the Levitical ceremonies were rejected. Therefore afterwards the apostles, as is narrated in the Acts,chapter 15by a clear decree abrogated circumcision, and the remaining Levitical rites. And Paul struggles most fiercely concerning the same matter, as is evident. Let this testimony therefore be observed in Daniel, which affirms that after the preaching of the Messiah the Levitical ceremonies were to cease.

And upon the wing of abominations of desolation, and even unto the determined consummation, it shall drip upon the devastation. This last part of the prophecy contains a truly sad opinion. The previous verses promised happy things, that the polity would endure until the coming of the Messiah, that the Messiah would come, that he would give the benefits promised to the fathers by an eternal covenant. But an opinion concerning the destruction of the city was added. Moreover, that which is said here concerning the temple is much sadder. This very temple, in which God once willed to be invoked, where he heard, which he defended, is now so condemned by dire execrations, that he says henceforth, after the preaching of Christ, it will be the seat of the execrable idol, laying waste the worship of God.

And if, however, they introduce the darkness of Jewish corruptions even to this passage, nevertheless there is no doubt that this angel is saying that there will be execrable idols in the temple, after which a perpetual desolation and solitude will follow. Christ himself interpreted this text in this way, Matt. 24. When you shall see the abomination of desolation, spoken of by Daniel the prophet, standing in the holy place (he that readeth, let him understand) then they that are in Judea, let them flee, &c. He says that the sign of the destruction of Jerusalem, and of the ruin of the Jewish nation, will be when the idol stands in the holy place, which he calls by Hebrew custom βδέλυγμα. Although, however, the angel understands that the whole worship will be an execrable idol, after the high priests have killed the Messiah, yet so that the wrath of God may be seen, God permitted the temple to be profaned by many foul examples after the resurrection.

And although the pious understood that the very acts of worship fighting against the Son of God were things condemned by God, or that the sacrifices of those who opposed the Gospel were displeasing to God, and were truly idols and βδελύγματα, because no invocation is pleasing God, except that which is in the true knowledge and trust of the Son the mediator, yet I think that external profanations also occurred, striking the eyes, such as the standards of Tiberius, and the slaughters committed in the temple itself, when the Jews were attempting to cast out the standards, and perhaps the statues of Caligula, although Josephus narrates that history somewhat differently. For that there were some foul signs striking the eyes, which warned the pious of the prediction of Daniel and of Christ, and signified that the time of destruction was at hand, is consistent, just as now to the depraved opinions of bishops, canons, and monks, there are added external marks, striking the eyes, which can remind ordinary men that the worship of the enemies of the Gospel is condemned. There is a gathering to certain statues, help and salvation is sought from dead men, just as formerly from Hercules, Castor, Pollux, and the like. The manifold profanation of the Lord's Supper is manifest, as I shall say below. Why should I mention the rest? The wandering lusts of the mass-priests are certainly βδελύγματα , and they testify that this kind of mass-priest, who lives in almost Epicurean contempt of God, displeases God. Thus there were notable external marks of impiety I believe this was among the Jews after the resurrection of Christ.

But let us weigh the words of the text. The thing itself speaks, that the Jews, on account of their ignorance, interpret certain members here most insipidly. But that I may flee idle prolixity, I shall omit the Jewish ravings, and I shall briefly recite the interpretation.

These words are in the text, And upon the wing shall be the abominations of desolation. Many wonder at this figure, when it says, Upon the wing, but the explanation is ready, simple, elegant, & clear, Upon the wing, that is, upon the Cherubim standing on the table of the mercy seat, in the holy of holies.

Thus Christ interprets, who says, βδέλυγμα ἐρημώσεως τὸ ρηθὲν διὰ δανιὴλ τοῦ προφήτου ἐστὸς ἐν τόπῳ ἅγιῳ, that is, Either a man-made idol shall stand in that very place of the Cherubim, which had been the seat of God, as is evident from25. cap. Exo.or the worship toward that place & the invocation, which is done with the insult of the son of God, shall be an idol and a condemned thing. Now, alas, it is not difficult to show examples in temples. In the invocation of dead men, and the propha nation of the Lord's Supper, idols are without doubt worshipped and invoked, which are placed in the principal place, that is, they are the principal objects of worship in the temples of the enemies of the Gospel.

This whole address of the angel will be more atrocious, if one considers the entire description concerning the mercy seat and the ark. Nothing could be said or thought more sorrowful among the pious than that this true seat of God, where God had promised that He would certainly hear the prayers of His people, is defiled.

Why does He name the abomination of desolation? It is a customary figure for the Hebrews to call an idol an abomination or βδέλυγμα,, that is, a thing which God execrates and condemns. And He execrates and condemns it for this reason, because honor owed to God is falsely attributed to it. As you might think of a statue of Mary, such as many have seen many of, to which people run, and the presence of either Mary or God is bound to it against the commandment of God. There it is thought that either God is more propitious, or Mary hears prayers more kindly; in whatever way these things are painted over, the worship is impious and condemned by God. Such an idol and such worship is βδέλυγμα,, that is, a thing condemned by God, and condemned for this reason, because the invocation is not rightly made to God. I have recited the example so that the use of the word might be better understood.

But why does he add βδέλυγμα ἐρημώσεως It is a Hebraism, and a signification of the effect. As if he were to say, The devastating idol, because the idol placed in the temple of Jerusalem after the resurrection was a sign of the devastation and overthrow of the whole temple and polity. The impious worship was -aussa of the destruction. External profanations were signs of destruction. Understand therefore βδέλυγμα ἐρημώσεως, a condemned thing, a devastator.

But devastation is twofold, the former of doctrine, of opinions, of invocation. The other is the latter, the punishment itself, namely the destruction of the people. As if you were to say, The statue of Mary is a devastator, first of true doctrine concerning Christ the mediator, concerning the invocation of Christ, afterwards it also draws down punishments, just as kingdoms which have the title of church, without a doubt, are overturned and fall on account of idol-manias.

The following particle repeats the sentiment, namely, that when after the birth of the Messiah the city of Jerusalem shall be overturned, there will never afterwards be an establishment or restoration of the city of Jerusalem, of the temple, of the Levitical priesthood, and of the Jewish polity. The event now for 1470 years confirms this prediction. That time is somewhat longer than the time from David, who took the citadel, and

he founded the city of Jerusalem, until the destruction brought about by Titus. For there are 1150 years from David to this destruction brought about by Titus.

I HAVE DELIVERED the exposition of this most useful chapter, and the calculation of the times, as best I could; now indeed not at all intricate, which however I willingly leave to the judgment of the learned, whom I ask to take my study in good part. This calculation, which I have set forth, does not differ much from that which the most illustrious man D. Luther recounts in his Chronicle, who distributed the years partly according to Philo, partly according to the book of the Maccabees. But when the reader shall see that the testimonies which we also have added from the most certain histories almost agree with that number, he will be moved by the consensus, so as not to reject these computations.

Moreover, this inquiry into the times is profitable to students, for the reading of the prophets becomes more pleasing and sweeter when they are moderately understood. This, however, contributes to the nourishing of piety. Next, it helps to consider this counsel of God concerning the number of years, and concerning the revealed destruction. For us, after the destruction of Jerusalem, a certain testimony is proposed, which teaches that the Messiah has been exhibited to be. Thus the number of years warned the pious before Christ was born, at about which time they were to see the Messiah.

Let us consider, however, that there are most weighty reasons why God, concerning this so great matter, the mission of the Son, wished the church to be certain. Let us be grateful that He exhibited the Son, that He confirmed the church with these testimonies. Let us proclaim the benefit, let us inquire into the testimonies, let us compare the histories, so that the testimonies may become more illustrious. This study certainly becomes the pious. And the series of the histories themselves and of the times incidentally warns of many things concerning divine providence, concerning the punishments of the impious, concerning the form of the church, its miseries, and its defense. God wishes these examples to be considered, so that they may warn us concerning many great things, as the Psalm says. Finally, not to read and contemplate in one's mind backwards the beginnings of things, the origin of the church, its changes, restorations, government, not to compare the times of depraved religion to the antiquity of true doctrine, is a negligence highly to be censured in a learned man.

AFTER we have completed the exposition of the whole chapter, now let the studious reader repeat in his mind the principal points, so that how these let him consider what utility the reading has brought. In the beginning, the doctrine concerning repentance, faith, and true invocation is handed down. This passage contains an illustrious testimony of the prophetic and apostolic consensus. For the opinion of Daniel agrees entirely with Paul, when Daniel says, We pray, not because of our righteousness, but because of your many mercies. And indeed he adds, For the Lord's sake, namely, for the sake of the promised deliverer.

These things agree entirely with the Pauline sermons, which say that we are received by faith for the sake of the Lord the mediator, not because of our own dignity. Nor does Paul teach anything other than what the example of Daniel shows. It is useful, however, for the pious to see the consensus of the prophetic sermons and of Paul, so that they may understand that there is one perpetual opinion of the catholic church of God, which the first fathers, the prophets, Christ, and the apostles have professed.

And the passage is worthy of memory, because he invokes by name here, for the Lord's sake, that is, for the sake of the promised deliverer. This is the true voice of the church, distinguishing our invocation from the heathen and Mahometan. For the nations They do not know the Son of God, they do not rightly call upon God, nor do they establish that their prayers are received, because they hold no promise. We, however, know that the eternal Father is appeased through the Son the mediator, and that a promise has been delivered, that for His sake He will receive our prayers. Therefore Christ says, Whatever you ask the Father in my name, He will give to you.

Let us learn, therefore, that this is true invocation: to ask for the sake of the Son the mediator, and to call upon the eternal Father with confidence in the mercy promised for His sake. On the other hand, however, let us execrate ethnic and Mahometan invocations, which either do not know the Son of God or treat Him with contumely. Invocation is also depraved among our adversaries, who overwhelm the doctrine of faith, and do not teach that confidence in the mediator is to be brought to God, but rather either confidence in one's own worthiness, or doubt, which differs nothing from ethnic invocation. You have, therefore, in this chapter a doctrine useful for the whole of life.

There follows the prophecy concerning the time of the coming of the Messiah, concerning the death of the Messiah, concerning the destruction of the Jewish polity. This passage contains a manifest testimony concerning two articles, about which dispute most greatly the Jews. He teaches that the Messiah has been exhibited, because he was to come while the Jewish polity was still enduring. He teaches also that the kingdom of the Messiah will not be a worldly empire or the Mosaic polity, because here it is said manifestly and in many words that the Mosaic polity is to be destroyed.

Therefore the resurrected Messiah reigns in such a way that, with the Gospel given, he calls to eternal life those who acknowledge him to be the propitiator, and begins in them through the Holy Spirit a new light and obedience, deletes sins, and is about to give eternal life to those in whom faith shines and new justice has been begun.

Latin:

CAPUT IX.

In anno primo Darii filii Assueri de semine Medorum, qui imperavit super regnum Chaldaeorum: Anno uno regni eius, ego Daniel intellexi in libris numerum annorum, de quo factus est sermo domini ad Ieremiam prophetam, ut complerentur desolationis Ierusalem septuaginta anni. Et posui faciem meam ad dominum deum meum rogare et deprecari in ieiuniis, sacco & cinere. Et oravi dominum deum meum, & confessus sum, & dixi, Obsecro domine deus magne & terribilis, custodiens pactum & misericordiam diligentibus te & custodientibus mandata tua. Peccavimus, iniquitatem fecimus, impiè egimus, & recessimus, & declinavimus à mandatis tuis ac iudiciis. Non obedivimus servis tuis prophetis, qui locuti sunt in nomine tuo regibus nostris, principibus nostris, patribus nostris, omnique populo terrae. Tibi dñe iustitia: nobis autem confusio faciei, sicut est hodie viro Iuda, & habitatoribus Ierusalem et omni Israel, his qui propè sunt, & his qui procul in universis terris, ad quas eiecisti eos propter iniquitates eorum, in quibus peccaverunt in te. Domine, nobis confusio faciei, regibus nostris, principibus nostris, & patribus nostris qui peccaverunt: Tibi autem domino deo nostro misericordia & propitiatio, quia recessimus à te, & non audivimus vocem domini dei nostri, ut ambularemus in lege eius quam posuit nobis per servos suos prophetas. Et omnis Israel praevaricati sunt legem tuam, & declinaverunt ne audirent vocem tuam: et stillavit super nos maledictio & detestatio quae scripta est in libro Moysi serui dei, quia peccavimus ei. Et statuit sermones suos quos locutus est super nos et super principes nostros qui iudicaverunt nos, ut superinduceret in nos magnum malum, quale nunquam fuit sub omni caelo, secundum quod factum est in Ierusalem. Sicut scriptum est in lege Moysi, omne malum hoc venit super nos: & non rogavimus faciem tuam domine deus noster, ut reverteremur ab iniquitatibus nostris, & cogitaremus veritatem tuam. Et vigilavit dominus super malitiam, & adduxit eam super nos: iustus dominus deus noster in omnibus operibus suis quae fecit: non enim audivimus vocem eius. Et nunc domine deus noster qui eduxisti populum tuum de terra Aegypti in manu forti, & fecisti tibi nomen secundum diem hanc, peccavimus, iniquitatem fecimus. Domine, in omnem iustitiam tuam avertatur obsecro ira tua, & furor tuus à civitate tua Ierusalem, & à monte sancto tuo. Propter peccata enim nostra, & iniquitates patrum nostrorum, Ierusalem & populus tuus in opprobrium sunt omnibus per circuitum nostrum. Nunc ergo exaudi deus noster orationem servi tui, & preces eius, & ostende faciem tuam super sanctuarium tuum quod desertum est propter * temetipsum. Inclina deus meus aurem tuam, & aud:aperi oculos tuos, & vide desolationem nostram, & civitatem super quam invocatum est nomen tuum: neque enim in iustificationibus nostris prosternimus preces ante faciem tuam, sed in miserationibus tuis multis. Exaudi domine, placare domine: attende & fac ne moreris propter temetipsum deus meus: quia nomen tuum invocatum est super civitatem & super populum tuum.

Cumque adhuc loquerer & orarem, & confiterer peccata mea & peccata populi mei Israel, ut prosternerem preces meas in conspectu dei mei, pro monte sancto dei mei: Adhuc me loquente in oratione mea, ecce vir Gabriel quem videram in visione à principio, cito volans tetigit me in tempore sacrificii vespertini Et docuit me, & locutus est mihi, dixitque: Daniel, nunc egressus sum ut docerem te, & intelligeres. Ab exordio precum tuarum egressus est sermo: ego autem veni ut indicarem tibi, quia vir desideriorum es: tu autem animadverte sermonem, & intellige visionem. Septuaginta hebdomades * abbreviatae sunt super populum tuum & super urbem sanctam tuam, ut consummetur praevaricatio, & finem accipiat peccatum, & deleatur iniquitas, & adducatur iustitia sempiterna, & impleatur visio & prophetia: & ungatur sanctus sanctorum. Scito ergo & animadverte: ab exitu sermonis, ut iterum aedificetur Ierusalem, usque ad Christum ducem, hebdomades septem et hebdomades sexagintaduae erunt: et rursum aedificabitur platea, & muri in angustia temporum. Et post hebdomades sexagintaduas occidẽ Christus, * & non erit ei (populus) Et civitatem & sanctuarium dissipabit populus cum duce venturo, & finis eius vastitas, & post finem belli statuta desolatio Confirmavit autem pactum multis hebdomada una: & in dimidio hebdomadis deficiet hostia & sacrificium, & erit * in templo abominatio desolationis: & usque ad consummationem & finem perseverabit desolatio.

Etsi singula capita uberrimam doctrinam continent, tamen hoc caput longè antecellit caeteris. Tradit enim illustre testimonium de tempore adventus Christi, de morte Christi, de iustitia novi testamenti, de excidio politiae Iudaicae. Complectitur & in precatione doctrinam paenitentiae, taxat fiduciam propriae iustitiae, & monstrat iustitiam fidei. Quare hoc caput doctrinam utilissimam continet, ad crudiendos & confirmandos pios de multis articulis.

Ad haec perspicuè refutat amentiam Iudaeorum, qui negant Messiam venisse, negant Messiam oportuisse pati, contendunt Iudaeos habituros regnum mundanum, &c. Hic locus maximè convincit Iudaeos, & ostendit eos contra conscientiam suam pervicaces esse.

Sed primum historica consideremus.Ieremias cap. 25.&29.praedicit exilium populi Iudaici in Babylone duraturum esse annos 70. Haec prophetia diluebat argumenta pseudoprophetarum, qui obiiciebant promissiones. Deus promisit se servaturum esse hunc cultum & hoc regnum, donec veniat Messias. Ergo iudicabant non posse deleri templum, nec populum abduci, nec politiam huius populi redigi in vastitatem. Ieremias diluit argumentum, Deus promisit & praestabit, sed aliter quam nos imaginamur. Interea etiam castigabit & castigatos mirabiliter restituet.

Ac profecto, haec ipsa reductio ex Babylone non fuit minus miraculum, quàm eductio ex Aegypto. Et tardè processit hic reditus & templi restitutio. Nam paulatim alii post alios redierunt, & aedificatio impedita fuit toto Cyri tempore, & deinceps annos 42. usque ad secundum annum Longimani.

Haec legentes, cogitare debemus, quàm sit horribilis ira dei adversus peccata, cum de hoc suo populo tantas poenas sumpserit. Cogitandum etiam quàm sit exigua ecclesia.

Rursus consolatio piis proponitur. Vident in tantis periculis tamen nulla mundi potentia opprimi ecclesiam posse, sed regi eam & servari divinitus. Videmus & hoc, pia consilia bonorum principum multis modis impediri, sicut Cyri beneficium impeditum fuit tempore Cyri, & postea tempore Darii, quem Graeci sic vocant.

CUM igitur iam ageretur annus septuagesimus exilii, primo anno Cyri, post captam Babylonem, Daniel memor promissionum, & sperans restitutionem populi, recordatur vaticinium Ieremiae, & ingenti impetu movetur ad precandum.

Hic locus significat prophetas ex lectione quaesivisse consolationem, & profecisse.

Monet item, quod, etsi promiserat deus reditum, tamen voluerit peti hoc beneficium precando, & invocatione exerceri fidem sanctorum. Sicut inquit Christus, petite, & accipietis. Item, invoca me in die tribulationus, & eripiam te, &c.

Etiamsi promisit deus beneficia corporalia vel spiritualia, tamen precibus vult exerceri fidem, & vult crescere paenitentiam Sicut inquit Zacharias, Convertimini ad me, & ego convertar ad vos, &c. Et orat Daniel de restituenda ecclesia. Ita nos quoque afficiamur vero dolore propter ecclesiae calamitates, & oremus, ut deus eam augeat, gubernet, & servet.

Maximè displicet deo securitas illorum, qui nihil afficiuntur calamitatibus ecclesiae. Sicut queritur Amos cap.6. Vae opulentis, &c. qui nihil patiuntur super contritione Ioseph. Ac Daniel intelligit magnitudinem calamitatum, ideo tam tristem querelam de peccatis populi & de poena recitat.

Dixi autem hîc tradi doctrinam paenitentiae. Daniel fatetur peccata populi, & tribuit deo laudem iustitiae, quod iustè puniverit populum. Deinde petit remissionem peccatorum, & reductionem populi. Est ergo vera contritio, agnoscere iram dei adversus nostra peccata, expavescere propter iram dei, dolere quòd deum offenderimus, tribuere ei laudem, quòd iustè nos puniat, & obedire in poenis.

Hanc confessiovem hîc recitat, Tibi domine iustitia, nobis autem confusio faciei. Haec est vox verae contritionis. Sicut &Psalmus 32.docet, Dixi confitebor adversum me iniustitiam meam domino, &c.

Et Psalmo 51.Tibi solum peccavi, & malum coram te feci, ut iustificeris in sermonibus tuis, & vincas cum iudicaris. Id est, Agnosco me tantum esse peccatorem, ac reum coram te. Ut iustificeris, id est, ut tibi tribuam laudem iustè punientis ac plectentis. Ac vincas cum te iudicant hypocritae, scilicet qui cogitant deum nimis asperum, nimis durum esse adversus hanc miseram naturam, accusant iudicium dei, non possunt se ei ita subiicere, ut agnoscerent se iuste plecti.

Hic fremitus est ingens & arcanum malum in animis hominum. Ideo Paulus aliquoties inculcat haec duo, ut sit deus iustus & iustificans, id est, tribuamus ei laudem iustè punientis, non arrogemus nobis iustitiam, sed credamus quod deus sit iustificator, id est, quòd ita iusti pronunciemur, cum ipse nostri miseretur, nos iustificat. Facile est verbus tribuere deo laudem iustitiae, sed ex corde agnoscere magnitudinem nostrorum peccatorum, ac sentire, quòd iustè plectamur, ac petere veniam, haec sunt ardua & difficilia.

Necesse est autem hanc doctrinam contritionis diligenter inculcari ecclesiis, ut verè agnoscant peccata, verè sciant poenas & calamitates nobis infligi propter peccata. Sicut Ieremias inquitcap. 5.Peccata vestra prohibuerunt bonum à vobis. Et hoc loco, Iustus est dominus deus noster in omnibus operibus quae fecit, videlicet, quamvis gravissimas poenas de nobis sumpserit,&c.

Nec tamen satis est peccata agnoscere, intueri poenas, sed accedat etiam consolatio. Ergo Daniel non solum doctrinam contritionis proponit, sed addit alteram partem. Docet suo exemplo petere, & expectare veniam, propter misericordiam & promissiones. Sicut alibi dicitur, Agite paenitentiam, & credite Evan gelio. Accedat fides ad contritionem & intueatur promissiones. Sicut hîc Daniel non tantum dicit, Tibi domine iustitia, Sed addit, Tibi misericordia & propitiatio, Tu promisisti te velle misereri & ignoscere. Et hanc sententiam aliquoties repetit, disertè excludens merita, Non in iustitia nostra petimus, sed propter magnum misericordiam tuam.
