Veni autem ut docerem te quae ventura sunt populo tuo in novissimis diebus: quoniam adhuc visio in dies. Cumque loqueretur mihi huiuscemodi verbis, deieci vultum meum ad terram, & tacui. Et ecce, quasi similitudo filii hominis tetigit labia mea: & aperiens os meum, locutus sum, & dixi ad eum qui stabat contra me: Domine mi, in visione tua dissolutae sunt compages meae, & nihil in me remansit virium. Et quomodo poterit servus domini mei loqui cum domino meo? nihil enim in me remansit virium, sed & halitus meus intercluditur. Rursum ergo tetigit me quasi visio hominis, & confortavit me,& dixit: Noli timere vir desideriorum, pax tibi, confortare, & esto robustus.Cumque loque retur mecum,convalui, & dixi: Loquere dominemi, quia confortasti me. Et ait: Nunquid scis quare venerim ad te? & nunc revertar, ut praelier adversum principem Persarum. Cum enim egrederer,apparuit princeps Graecorum veniens. Veruntamen annunciabo tibi, quod expressum est in scriptura veritatis: & nemo est adiutor meus in omnibus his, nisi Michael princeps vester.

REVELATIO quae describitur in superiori capite,facta est anno I. Cyri, quo aliquanto post hanc revelationem, Cyri edictum de restitutione Ierosolymae propositum est,& redire aliqua Iudaeorum agmina coeperunt ac inchoârunt aedificationem. Omnia tunc laeticiae, gratulationum, ac bonae spei plena erant. Pii gratias deo agebant, quòd ecclesiae reddebatur vetus sedes, templum, cultus,celebrabant vaticinium Ieremiae,laudabant deum,qui voce Ieremiae mirabiliter servaverat gentis reliquias. Erat iunioribus dulce, videre maiorum patriam, templum, cultus divinitus institutos,frui libertate,studiis piis. Agnoscebant etiam hoc suum exilium profuisse ad doctrinae propagationem. Videbant Cyrum et multos alios ad veram pietatem conversos esse, & gaudebant se habere faventem regem, qui imperio & virtute tunc omnibus regibus antecellebat. Haec gaudia seni Danieli etiam exilium leniebant, qui tunc abfuerat à patria annos 86.Nam abductus est ante Ieconiam, ut testatur initium primi capitis.

Sed vide lector imaginem ecclesiae. Haec tanta gaudia, post triennium sequitur tristissimus luctus. Discedit ex regno Cyrus, prohibetur novo edicto aedificatio templi. Aliquanto pòst, ipse Cyrus interficitur. In aula fit rerum omnium conversio, excluduntur, urgentur odiis, opprimuntur pii, qui antea autoritate apud Cyrum valuerant. Renovantur ethnicae superstitiones.Principes impii, inimici verae doctrinae vocantur in aulam. Hic Daniel in quanto luctu fuerit,nemo satis explicare potest,dolebat regis amici casum, dolebat contumelia adfici nomen dei, praecipuè verò excruciabatur Iudaeorum tum imbecillitate, tum perversitatibus, quorum multi iam nullam spem esse restituendae urbis, & frustra sibi verba dari à prophetis,dicebant. Alii, etsi non prorsus desperatione frangebantur, tamen dehortatores erant suis, ne properarent redire in patriam, sed iubebant expectari tempora tranquilliora, ridebant stultitiam eorum,qui incerta spe moti,statim ex Chaldaea discesserant.

Ut igitur initia fuerant laeticiae plena,ita nunc boni sunt in luctu, squalore, tristitia. Econtra verò pars Iudaeorum,quae suum ocium religioni anteferebat, fremit indignatione, distrahit voluntates suorum,perturbat bonos.

Habes ecclesiae imaginem, quam deus vult & exerceri adflictionibus, & fide expectare liberationes. Et cum liberat,tamen eventus non respondent nostris coniecturis. Cum Cyri beneficium impeditum esset, postea magis conspici potuit,à deo gubernari hanc liberationě, cum tot impedimenta incidissent,quae humanis consiliis tolli non poterant.

Nova igitur visio exhibetur, iam Danieli, non solum ut ipse & caeteri pii in hoc praesenti periculo confirmentur, sed etiam ut posteritas praemoneatur de praecipuis mutationibus imperiorum, & de iis calamitatibus, quae Iudaeae impendebant. Saepe autem iam dictum est, quare utile sit praemoneri pios. Discunt vitare caussas calamitatum,videlicet ea scelera, quib.

caeteri accersunt poenas,praeparare animos, ne in ipsa calamitate adsentiantur impiis, expectare liberationem, praesertim cum intelligunt, clara voce dei promissos esse laetos exitus horum certaminum. Sed de his utilitatibus infrà rursus dicendum erit. Nunc breviter huius capitis praecipuos locos percurram.

Angelus pius narrat Danieli, se dimicasse cum principe Persarum, id est, cum diabolo moliente dissipationes regni Persici. Et si enim ignoramus, quomodo pugnent inter se boni & mali spiritus,tamen certamina esse non dubium est,sive disputatione fiunt,sive aliis modis. Ait ergo bonus angelus,repressum à se esse malum spiritum, qui Cambysen ivuenem, & aulicos impios incitabat, vel ad delendam gentem Iudaicam,vel ad interficiendum Danielem, vel ad alias res malas tentandas, quae novos motus in regno allaturae erant, sicut interdum leve erratum, quod initio contemnitur, postea ingentem ruinam trahit.

Alii aliter de sententia huius capitis disputant, sed iudico hanc esse simplicissimam enarrationem. Adfuit autem bono angelo dux Michael,quem cum hîc & infrà vocet ducem populi dei, intelligo esse ipsum filium dei,λόγον,ut à Ioanne nominatur. Hunc ducem & suprà scribit interesse colloquio in capite octavo,ubi angelus ab illo domino petit interpretationem visionis.

Adesse eum & in hoc colloquio,ac Danielis labra attingere,& consternatum recreare, adparet. Semper enim adfuisse filium dei ecclesiae, eamque defendisse contra furorem diaboli, certum est. Ideo Ioannes inquit, Omnia per ipsum facta sunt. Loquitur enim non tantum de conditione rerum, sed etiam de gloriosis liberationibus ecclesiae. Texit populum in mari rubro,& in deserto,Defendit Iosue,Gedeonem,SamuelemDavidem,Eliam,Elisaeum, & alios fideles gubernatores sui populi.

Ideo inquit Iacob, Benedicat his pueris angelus, qui me eripuit ex omnibus malis. Haec verba conveniunt ad filium dei, qui verè liberat ab omnibus malis, videlicet à peccato, ab ira dei, à morte aeterna, ab insidiis diaboli. Non enim sine caussa nominatim dicitur,ab omnibus malis. Haec gloria non potest tribui ministris angelis, qui etiamsi protegunt corpora piorum,tamen nec peccatum nec aeternam mortem tollere possunt.

Discamus igitur admoniti his locis, filium deiλόγονadfuisse ecclesiae inde usque ab initio. Erimus enim melius muniti adversus Samosateni furores, cum cogitabimus haec testimonia de filio dei,etiam antequam se in carne ostendit, opitulato patribus, & cum eis collocuto. Deinde & statuemus, nunc quoque adesse eum ecclesiae, opitulari piis, depellere diabolum, & sicut in Epistola Ioannis dicitur, destruere opera diaboli.Accendamur ergo ut ab eo petamus & expectemus auxilium adversus diabolum undique grassantem & conantem dissipare & delere ecclesiam armis Turcicis, regum & pontificum negligentia in fovendis ecclesiis, pravis ingeniis inflammandis, ut serant aut confirment falsas opiniones.Sed recensere omnes artes diaboli nemo potest.

Exempla potius cogitemus. Universa mundi historia ostendit, quàm sit acer hostis dei & generis humani. Vides ex quanto fastigio Saulem detraxerit, quale bellum Davidi intulerit, ut ipsum unà raperet in exitium,cum alios tam multos perdidisset,filium Absolonem, Ahitophel, & haud dubiè multos sapientia & virtutè antecellentes.

Et opinor vix ullum esse hominem, cui non serpens ille insidiator, insigne aliquod vulnus in animo faciat, ut languefaciat fidem. Quoties David sedato iam tumultu, cogitavit de suo lapsu, de coniugibus vi stupratis,deque aliis scandalis,non solum moesticia renovata est,sed etiam pavore de dubitatione concussa & languefacta est fides.

Haec cogitent pii, & sint in statione & in excubiis adversus tam dirum hostem,& à filio dei petant defensionem & gubernationem. Vocatur Emanuel filius dei, & verè nobiscum adèst,sed agnosci vult & invocari. Vult etiam nos non per ignaviam ruere quò hostis impellit, sed vigilantes cavere insidias,repugnare illecebris. Si peccant oculi, non indulgeas, Si blanditur impia opinio,revoca animum, fuge consuetudinem, colloquia impiorum. Collige animo vera & firma testimonia rectae sententiae, & simul hosti repugnes, & ducem tuum filium dei accersas, ut te adivuet, ut destruat opera diaboli.

Hic praecipuus est locus huius capitis,quod clarè nos admonet de his certaminibus,significat praeliari bonos & malos spiritus inter se de ecclesia & de imperiis. Ut tunc diaboli irritabant adolescentem regem Cambysen & plaerosque principes, ut populum Iudaeorum vel opprimerent, vel delerent, ita nunc irritant Turcicum tyrannum, ut nomen Christi funditus deleat, incendunt pontifices & reges, ut defendant idola, extinguant lucem evangelii. Et ut tunc diabolus regni Persici dissipationes efficiebat, impellebat Babylonios & Assyrios ad movendas seditiones, ita nunc domesticis bellis implicat principes, ut etiam hac ratione impediat Evangelii propagationem.

Haec dum geruntur, dimicant inter se angeli boni & mali. Regit autem & adivuat bonos dux noster filius dei, & protegit ecclesiam, & reprimit impetus malorum spirituum. Haec etsi non cernimus oculis, & homines prophani fabulosa esse ducunt, tamen revera ita geri, multa illustria testimonia in historiis, & dictis propheticis & apostolicis ostendunt.

Notae sunt historiae Iacob, & defensionis populi in mari rubro & in deserto. Postea cum Iosue castrametatus esset ad Iericho, manedum situm regionis speculatur, videt quendam stricto gladio coram astantem, qui interrogatus quis sit, respondit se esse ducem exercitus dei, missum videlicet, ut multitudini Israeliticae opem ferret. Arbitror autem & hunc fuisse ipsum filium dei. Huc adde David, Eliae & Elisaei historias. Et David inquitPsalm. 91.Quoniam angelis suis mandavit de te, ut custodiant te in omnibus viis tuis. Et 34. Psalmo, Circumvallat angelus domini timentes eum, & eripit eos.

Cum igitur certum sit, deum ecclesiae suae angelos addidisse custodes, pericula corporum in obeunda vocatione minus formidemus. Haec mihi scribenti, quaedam nostri temporis exempla in mentem venerunt. Et quanquam historias plures, certas et veras de honestis hominibus, & fide dignis, qui adhuc vivunt, narrare possem, tamen unicam recitabo de Simone Gryneo, qui ad excellentem eruditionem & virtutem adiunxerat veram dei reverentiam, & maximè Philosophiam illorum detestabatur, qui putant eo se plus videre caeteris, quòd vel contemnere religiones audent, vel de omnibus sententiis Pyrrhonio more in utramque partem disputare solent.

IN conventu Spirensi, qui fuit anno 1529.

sortè ad me ex Academia Edelbergensi expatiatus erat. Ibi cum Fabrum Viennensem Episcopum audisset in concione quosdam tetros errores defendere, sequitur egredientem ex templo, ac reverenter salutat. Postea narrat, se non petulantia aliqua, sed bono studio moveri, ut quaedam dicat. Non detrectat Faber colloquium. Ibi Gryneus, dolere se ait, quòd vir instructus doctrina, & antecellens autoritate, confirmet errores palàm contumeliosos adversus deum, & quos constet manifestis testimoniis antiquitatis ecclesiasticae refutari posse. Scribit Irenaeus Polycarpum solitum aures obstruere audientem portentosos errores fanaticorum hominum. Quo te animo, putas, audisset iam Polycarpus, disserentem, quid muscomedat, arrodens panem consecratum? Quis non deploret ecclesiae tenebras? Interpellat Faber plura additurum, ac interrogat nomen. Nihil dissimulat hic, sed placidè fatetur, se Gryneum esse.

Erat omnino Faber, ut multi norunt, flagitiosè timidus in congressibus doctorum, vel potius erat, ut veteri proverbio dicebatur, Nullus, magnus, malus, piscis, id est pavidus, procerus, insidiosus, & infans. Quare cum Grynei eruditionem, eloquentiam, copiam, ardorem animi, praesertim in tali caussa metueret, simulat se à rege vocatum esse, nec esse nunc ocium ad disput ationem de re omnium maxima pertexendam. Simulat se maxime cupidum esse & amicitiae Grynei & colloquii longioris, & cum sua causa, tum propter rempublicam orare incipit, ut cras ad se redeat, monstrat hospitium & horam constituit. Gryneus serio haec dici putans, non gravatim promittit.

Relicto igitur Fabro, ad nos recta accedit. Vix adsederat mensae, iam enim cenae tempus erat, & recitaverat partem disputationis mihi et aliis, cum ego adsidens ad mensam, subito ex conclavi evocor. Hîc me, nescio quis senex, singularem gravit atem vultu, oratione, & vestitu prae se ferens, nec quis fuerit, unquam resciscere potui, alloquitur, ac narrat, mox adfuturos esse in hospitio nostro lictores, missos iussu regis, apud quem Faber Gryneum accusasset, ad abducendum Gryneum in carcerem. Iubet, ut statim ex urbe discedat, ac hortatur ne contemur. Ita dicta salute, senex abit.

Ego ad sodales redeo, iubeo ut surgant, de narro quid senex nunciarit. Mox per publicam plateam deducimus Gryneum, acceptum in medium agmen, & ad Rhenum pergimus, ubi nos in ripa citeriore tantisper restitimus, donec in alteram ripam Gryneus cum suo comite in parva cymba transvectus est. Postea reversi in hospitium, intelligimus statim adfuisse lictores, cum nos exiguo spacio ab ea domo progressi essemus.

Quanto autem in periculo futurus fuerit Gryneus, si in carcerem abreptus esset, ex Fabri saevi tiafacile aestimari poterat. Quare iudicabamus consilium illud crudele elusum esse divinitus. Et ut adfirmare non possum, quis fuerit ille senexμηνυτὴς,ita lictorum ea fuit festinatio, ut nisi ab angelis tectus fuisset Gryneus, evadere non potuisset.

Rem autem ita gestam esse, ut narravi, certum est, & norunt quidam boni viri adhuc superstites, qui adfuerunt. Agamus igitur gratias deo, quòd nobis custodes angelos addit, & eo tranquillioribus animis vocationis offitia obeamus.

Nunc ad textum redeo. In fine rursus, inquit angelus, se cum rege Persico militaturum esse, adversus Graecos, & malos spiritus eorum duces.

Nominat autem Graecos Iavan, seu, ut ipsi loquuntur, Iones. Nec dubium est, in prophetis ubique Ionas intelligi Graecam gentem, quae & littus in Asia tenuit, & sedes habuit in vicinis insulis, & in Europae parte, quae, etsi saepe mutatae sunt appellationes, tamen tandem communi nomine Graecia dicta est.

Fuit autem Ion seu Iavan filius Iapheth, ut testatur catalogus in Genesi capite decimo, à quo propagatos esse Ionas, hac quoque coniectura ratiocinari possumus, quia apud Graecos utriusque nominis memoria mansit, Iapeti & Ionis. Euripides poeta fingit Ionem deucalionis pronepotem. Sed tota illa narratio Euripidis, de rebus recentioribus loquitur, quàm quae tribui possint filio Iapheth. Nam Graecis magna ex parte ignotae sunt res veteres ante Pelopem in Graecia gestae. Et arbitror, ut significat Thucydides, primos illos Ionum qui in Europam traiecerunt, diu incertis sedibus vagatos, non reliquisse certa monumenta suarum rerum. Ita et prima doctrima de deo apud posteros extincta est, & oblivio secuta rerum veterum omnium.

Porrò à diluvio Nohae, usque ab bellum Troianum, anni sunt circiter mille aut paulo plus. Horum si historiam cogites, non dubium est mansisse ecclesiam dei in familiis duabus Sem et Iapheth, dum vixit pater Noha, & dum ipsi superstites fuerunt. Vixit autem Noha post diluvium, annos trecentos & quinquaginta. Sem verò etiam post Abraham vixit. Consentaneum est & Iapheth diu vixisse. Horum igitur aetate fulsit adhuc inter gentes caelestis doctrina, de promissione venturi liberatoris, & de misericordia dei propter hunc promissa, item de bonis & castis moribus. Et fortassis ex hac patrum doctrina reliqui fuerunt versiculi, qui de mundi conflagratione, de aeterna vita, de iudicio, & de placatore dei vaticinantur, qui extant apud Lactantium & alios.

Postea secuta est aetas deterior & extincta luce doctrinae caelestis, ortae sunt quotidie novaeἐιδωλομανίαι,receptae flagitiosae libidines, quas deus ingentibus bellis, & excidiis urbium & gentium punivit. Hae confusiones rerum humanarum, magis etiam deleverunt memoriam antiquitatis. Ac multas magnas et exitiales mutationes gentium accidisse intra annos quingentos post Iapheth, credibile est. Quare non est miradum, Graecis non satis notam esse suam originem.

Caeterum singulari consilio deus in ecclesia extare voluit, primam imò perpetuam historiam, usque ad Messiae adventum, ut sciremus, primam hanc esse & perpetuò consentientem revelationem, in qua deus se patefecit. Ac bona mens valde confirmatur in hac professione, cum intelligit unam hanc esse primam & perpetuò sibi consentientem doctrinam de deo. Necesse est enim initio aliquem fuisse coetum, qui deum rectè invocaret.

Cum autem in hac prima doctrina patrum series, certa fide tradita sit, nomina saepe indicant gentium origines, Ut ab Assur, Assyrii, à Canaan, Cananaei, Ab Elam, Elamitae, quorum in Persia nomen mansisse etiam Graeci historici testantur. Ita genti Ionicae nomen suum pater Ion attribuit, à qua cum Graecae gentes aliquae propagatae sint, constat hac appellatione Graecos in prophetis ubique significari, ut suprà Daniel nominat Alexandrum regem Iavan, seu Ionum. Esaias eum educit ex Cithim. Et in libro Machabaeorum, Alexander vocatur rex Cethim. Haec conveniunt, Nam liber Genesis docet Cithim esse filium Iavan. Est igitur aliquanto generalius nomen Iavan, quo Graecae gentes significantur, Cithim angustius, à quo non dubium est, factum esse nomenμακέτ-ρ,quod est Macedo. Nam vetustior appellatio estμακέτης.Constat autem Macedonicam gentem Graecae cognatam fuisse. Reges certè Graeci fuerunt orti ab Hercule. Et Alexandri mater Olympias fuit orta à Molosso nepote Achillis. Aeacum verò, à quo Achilles ortus est, fuisse Graecum, & tenuisse Aeginam certum est. Praeterea Alexander non solum rex erat Macedonum, sed etiam communi decreto civitatum Graeciae appellatus est dux Graecorum. Et magnam partem tenebat, Thessaliam, Aetoliam, Phocidem. Denique res eius gestae sunt initio magna ex parte pecunia, machinis & copiis Graecarum gentium. Quare & ipse modestissimè in Trophaeo scripsit,ἀλἐξανδρος καὶ ἕλληνες πλὴν λακεδαιμονίων..Ac de nomine Iavam seu Ionum haec dixisse satis sit

Illud quaeri potest in hac angeli narratione, de quibus Ioniae motibus dicatur, excitatis circa extrema Cyri tempora, aut regnante iam Cambyse absente patre. Angelus breviter indicat, gentem Ionicam à Malis spiritibus incitari ad movendas seditiones. Breviter enim res peregrinas aliorum regnorum attingunt prophetae. Nec ego adfirmare possum, de quibus motibus haec dicantur. Sed consentaneum est, reliquias belli quod gessit Harpagus in Ionia, cum ibi à Cyro relictus esset, magnas habuisse difficultates.

Gens Ionica, quae omnium maximè floruerat ingeniis et opibus, bis domita est Cyro regnante. Rebellârat enim cum Cyrus Babylonem peteret. Et fuisse longas urbium oppugnationes, Phoceae exemplum ostendit, quae tanta pertinacia adversus Harpagum defensa est, ut tandem universa multitudo civium in ipsa obsidione naves conscenderit, & procul alias sedes quaesiverit. Hi exules urbem Massyliam ad Rhodani ostium condiderunt, & in eam Galliae oram transvexerunt Graecas literas & disciplinam plenam humanitatis. Potestque ex eius urbis humanitate existimari, quàm civiliter antea in Asia gens Ionica vixerit.

Sed luxus sine modo creverat & pepererat libidines & alia vitia. Ideo secutae sunt ingentes calamitates & urbium excidia. Etsi enim in Cyro clementia et bonitas erat insignis, tamen quia deus constituit et mutat imperia, utεἰδωλομανίας,tyrannides, & libidines puniat, mutationes omnes aliquid habent saevitiae, magnis igitur domita cladibus Ionia, vix tandem in servitutem redacta est, quam adeo tulit impatienter, ut crebras seditiones contra duces Persicos moverit. Et erant ducum imperia superba & crudelia. Ne ipsi quidem satrapae vicini inter sese diu concordes fuerunt. Nam Oraetes, qui post Harpagum Ioniam rexit, Mitrobaten vicinum & eius filium Cambyse regnante interfecit. Haec exempla ostendunt satis inquietam fuisse provinciam. Fuerunt igitur & angelorum certamina, qui malos spiritus seditionum & discordiarum inflammatores depellebant.

## 11:1 Argument
CHAPTER XI.

But I, from the first year of Darius the Mede, was standing, that he might be comforted and strengthened. And now I will announce the truth to you. Behold, still Three kings shall stand in Persia, and the fourth shall be enriched with exceeding great riches above all: and when he shall have grown strong by his riches, he shall stir up all against the kingdom of Greece. But a strong king shall arise, and shall rule with great power: and he shall do what he pleases. And when he shall have stood, his kingdom shall be broken, and shall be divided into the four winds of heaven: but not to his posterity, nor according to his power with which he ruled: for his kingdom shall be torn even to outsiders, except for these. And the king of the South shall be strengthened, and one of his princes shall prevail over him, and shall rule with dominion: for his dominion shall be great. And after the end of years they shall form an alliance: and the daughter of the king of the South shall come to the king of the North to make friendship, and she shall not obtain the strength of the arm, nor shall his seed stand: and she shall be delivered up, and they who brought her, her young men, and they who strengthened it in the times. And a planting from the sprout of his roots shall stand: and he shall come with an army, and shall enter the province of the king of the North: and he shall abuse them, and shall prevail. Moreover, their gods also, and their graven images, and the precious vessels of gold and silver, he shall lead captive into Egypt: he himself shall prevail against the king of the North. And the king of the South shall enter into the kingdom, and shall return to his own land. But his sons shall be provoked, and shall gather a multitude of very many armies: and he shall come hastening and overflowing: and he shall return and be stirred up, and shall join battle with his strength. And the king of the South, being provoked, shall go forth, and shall fight against the king of the North, and shall prepare an exceeding great multitude, and the multitude shall be given into his hands. And he shall take the multitude, and his heart shall be exalted, and he shall cast down many thousands, but he shall not prevail. He shall turn for the king of the North, and he will prepare a multitude much greater than before: and in the end of times and years he will come hastening with a great army and excessive riches. In those times many will rise up against the king of the South: also the sons of the transgressors of your people will be lifted up to fulfill the vision, and they will fall. And the king of the North will come, and will heap up a mound, and will take the most fortified cities: and the arms of the South will not withstand, and his chosen ones will rise up to resist, and there will be no strength. And he who comes upon him will do according to his pleasure, and there will be no one who stands against his face: and he will stand in the glorious land, and it will be consumed in his hand. And he will set his face, to come to hold his entire kingdom, and he will do upright things with him, and he will give him the daughter of women, to overthrow it: and it will not stand, nor will it be his. And he will turn his face toward the islands, and shall take many: and he shall cause the prince of his reproach to cease, and his reproach shall be turned back upon him. And he shall turn his face toward the empire of his own land, and he shall stumble, and fall, and shall not be found. And there shall stand in his place one most vile, and unworthy of the kingly honor: and in a few days he shall be destroyed, not in rage nor in battle. And there shall stand in his place one despised, and the kingly honor shall not be given him: and he shall come privately, and shall obtain the kingdom by fraud. And the arms of the fighter shall be overcome before his face, and shall be broken: moreover also the prince of the covenant. And after friendships, he shall deal deceitfully with him: and he shall go up, and shall overcome with a small people. And he shall enter into abundant and rich cities: and he shall do that which his fathers have not done, and the fathers of his fathers: he shall dissipate their spoils and prey and riches, and against the most firm thoughts it will enter: and this even until the time. And his strength and his heart will be stirred up against the king of the South with a great army: and the king of the South will be provoked to war with many and very strong auxiliaries: and they will not stand, because they will devise plans against him. And those eating bread with him will crush him, and his army will be oppressed: and very many will fall slain. The heart also of the two kings will be to do evil, and at one table they will speak a lie, and they will not succeed: because as yet the end is for another time. And he will return into his land with many riches: and his heart will be against the holy covenant, and he will act, and will return into his land. At the appointed time he will return, and will come to the South: and the latter will not be like the former. And galleys will come upon him, and the Romans, and he will be struck, and will return, and will be indignant against the testament of the sanctuary, and he shall act: and he shall return, and shall think against them that have forsaken the testament of the sanctuary. And arms from him shall stand, and they shall defile the sanctuary of strength, and shall take away the continual sacrifice, and they shall place the abomination unto desolation. And the wicked against the testament shall deal deceitfully: but the people that know their God shall prevail and act. And the learned in the people shall teach many: and they shall fall by the sword, and by flame, and by captivity, and by spoil of days. And when they shall have fallen, they shall be lifted up with a little help: and many shall be joined to them deceitfully. And some of the learned shall fall, that they may be refined, and chosen, and made white even to the time appointed: because as yet there shall be another time. And the king shall do according to his will, and he shall be lifted up and shall be magnified against every god: and against the God of gods he shall speak magnificent things, and it shall be directed, until the indignation is accomplished: for the determination is made. And he shall not regard the God of his fathers: and he shall be in the desires of women, and he shall not regard any of the gods, for he shall rise up against all things: but he shall worship the god Maozim in his place: and a god whom his fathers knew not, he shall worship with gold, and silver, and precious stone, and precious things. And he shall do to fortify Maozim with a strange god whom he hath not known, and he shall multiply glory, and shall give them power in many things, and shall divide the land for nothing. And at the time appointed, the king of the South shall fight against him, and the king of the North shall come against him like a tempest, with chariots, and with horsemen, and with a great navy: and he shall enter into the lands, and shall destroy, and pass through. And he shall enter into the glorious land, and many shall fall: but these only shall be saved from his hand, Edom and Moab, and the leaders of the sons of Ammon. And he shall send forth his hand into the lands: and the land of Egypt shall not escape. And he shall have power over the treasures of gold, and of silver, and in all the precious things of Egypt: and he shall pass through Libya also and Ethiopia. And rumors shall disturb him from the East and from the North: and he shall come in a great multitude, to destroy and kill many. And he shall fix his tabernacle in Apadno between the two seas, upon the glorious and holy mountain: and he shall come even to the top thereof, and no man shall help him.

Now he begins the prophecies concerning the kingdoms, so that he may at once confirm Daniel, and forewarn posterity concerning the impending calamities. And the prediction is an illustrious testimony, which shows that the doctrine of the prophets was delivered by God. And because he promises liberation, he signifies that this people, who embrace the prophetic doctrine, is a care to God. Therefore the pious are confirmed, so that they may not fall away from God, nor cast aside the profession of this doctrine.

Moreover, the final part of this long discourse pertains also to this last age of the world, and to the miseries of the church, which it has already borne for many centuries, while elsewhere the Mahometan madness attempts to blot out entirely the name of the Son of God, elsewhere Bishops reign in a heathen manner, and neglect the studies of ecclesiastical doctrine, allow the light of the Gospel concerning the true invocation in faith or trust in the Son of God to be extinguished, defend idols and lusts, and unjustly kill innocent men on account of the profession of true doctrine.

Meanwhile, many other evils also approach, even in those places where the doctrine has been amended. Princes, hindered, negligently protect discipline; few are munificent in nourishing the ministers of the Gospel and fostering the studies of letters. Therefore, ἀναρχία confirms the petulance of the wicked, and the neglect of letters threatens new darkness and a new barbarism.

Let the pious consider these evils, so that first they may ask of God that He Himself may preserve, rule, foster, and increase His church. Then, if any are able to heal some wounds, let them strive according to their calling, Lest Christ in the final judgment accuse them of having failed in their duty, saying, I was hungry, and you did not feed me, etc. Let courts and royal councils govern discipline, let them cherish those who teach. Let the learned not disdain the works or the doctrine of the church. For it is most worthy of a philosopher man to seek the true doctrine concerning God, and to celebrate God. Let them therefore assist the studies of the Gospel, and let them maintain the harmony of true doctrine with a certain moderation and philosophy necessary for pious governors. But these things will have to be said again below in their own place. Now I return to the histories, which are recited here.

First, let the testimony concerning the watch of the angels be observed. The angel narrates that he had been present from the very beginning with Darius the Mede, in order to assist and establish his kingdom. Perhaps he was a gentle old man, despised by the princes and neighbors, as is accustomed to happen. Yet a pious angel defended him, assisted him in battles, and governed dangerous events. Moreover, prosperous successes, especially when there have been great dangers, are signs of divine aid, therefore they strike fear into enemies or the envious, and among the good they increase the reverence for princes. As the people obeyed Hezekiah and Isaiah when the city was besieged, even though the situation was in the utmost desperation.

But good men judged the counsels and affairs of these princes to be divinely ordained. Let us therefore also learn both to seek the protections of the angels, and to retain them by piety.

Wondrous arts are now practiced in courts. And insidious counsels have been punished καὶ πλεονεκτικὰ, They think this to be political wisdom. But let pious governors learn that empires cannot be retained without the help of God. Wherefore let them obey Him, perform necessary duties, and expect defense from God, as it is written, WHO GIVES SALVATION TO KINGS Ps. 144:10. Nor let them seek faithless protections, nor lean upon the most uncertain and most fallacious wills of men, nor devise πλεονεκτήματα, nor entangle themselves in unnecessary matters.

AFTERWARDS the angel begins to prophesy concerning the Persian kingdom. There shall stand, he says, yet three kings in Persia. Here the Jews hallucinate remarkably, who think there were only three kings of the Persians, and they lose years from the series of times, more than a hundred. They can, however, be clearly refuted by writers who were contemporary with the individual Persian kings. But the angel says expressly, There shall stand three kings.

There is a singular emphasis in the word to stand among the prophets. For a kingdom to stand signifies to flourish, to retain power not yet inclined.

The customary enumeration is, after Cyrus, Cambyses, and Darius, to recount Xerxes as the fourth. For to insert the Magi as supposititious kings is absurd. This Monarchy flourished in virtue, victories, and power, especially under those first three kings. But Xerxes brought war upon the Greeks in Europe, where, when he had been defeated, the Monarchy began to be shaken and, some time later, to collapse. And the return of Xerxes to Asia was distinguished not only by the slaughter of the army, but also by the foulness of his morals, by incestuous lust, and by the cruelty of Xerxes. For immediately after his flight, he began to love his brother's wife. Afterwards, when he had given his brother's daughter to his own son, he burned with love for this young girl as well. After this incestuous practice, not only did other acts of parricide follow, but also the murder of his brother. Finally, Xerxes himself was also killed by Artabanus.

I recite these examples, First, so that punishments may warn us, so that we may fear the judgment and wrath of God, who severely punishes lusts. Next, so that we may be moved by the thought of human weakness, so that Let us ask of God, who through his son promised help, that he himself may govern us in all the counsels of life, and in the directing of our morals.

Born Xerxes in a Heroic family, from parents whose virtue was excellent, he had had a distinguished character, and had been diligently instructed in virtue by domestic discipline. But since in such great power and luxury he does not strive to curb his desires, he is finally rendered utterly savage. And punishments undoubtedly follow such cruelty.

These examples should exhort all men, and especially the powerful, to modesty and the fear of God. Let us remember also this, which the son of God says, Without me you can do nothing. And what the church sings concerning the holy spirit, Without your divinity, there is nothing in man, nothing is harmless. Wherefore let us ask that we be bent toward virtue, and ruled by the holy spirit of God.

BUT I return to the text. I have recited the customary enumeration of the kings, which if anyone does not approve, let him show a more suitable one. For I too would prefer to understand the prophecy concerning three, Cyrus, Darius the son of Hystaspes, and Artaxerxes Longimanus, even though Xerxes is interjected, who evidently at the same time wages war in Europe, Where Longimanus reigns at home, who, since he excelled in virtue and piety, was nevertheless still propping up the shaken Monarchy. Nor were many things taken away from the Persians outside of Europe while he was reigning. Afterwards there followed defections of the provinces, of Egypt and others. There followed also a civil war between two brothers, Cyrus the Younger and Artaxerxes Mnemon.

Nor was any other more beneficent toward the people of God than Longimanus. For while he was reigning, the decree concerning the restoration of the temple was renewed. And it appears that his chief governors were Ezra and Nehemiah. Therefore fortune responded to his virtue. God protected and helped the modest, pious king, who was beneficent to the church, in the government and defense of the kingdom; He also gave him a pleasing outcome. Therefore, his modesty and piety are to be imitated.

Thus far the angel has preached concerning the Persians, briefly indeed, but nevertheless signifying that there would still be certain flourishing kings, but that afterwards the Greeks would tear the Monarchy to pieces. Therefore he begins to speak concerning the Greeks.

Latin:

CAPUT XI.

Ego autem ab anno primo Darii Medi stabam, ut confortaretur et roboraretur. Et nunc veritatem annunciabo tibi. Ecce, adhuc tres reges stabunt in Perside, et quartus ditabitur opibus nimiis super omnes: & cum invaluerit divitiis suis, concitabit omnes adversum regnum Graeciae. Surget verò rex fortis, & dominabitur potestate multa: & faciet quod placuerit ei. Et cum steterit, conteretur regnum eius, & dividetur in quatuor ventos caeli:sed non in posteros eius, neque secundum potentiam illius qua dominatus est: lacerabitur enim regnum eius etiam in externos, exceptis his. Et confortabitur rex Austri, & de principibus eius, praevalebit super eum, & dominabitur ditione: multa enim dominatio eius. Et post finem annorum foederabuntur: filiaque regis Austri veniet ad regem Aquilonis facere amicitiam, & non obtinebit fortitudinem brachii, nec stabit semen eius: & tradetur ipsa, & qui adduxerunt eam, adolescentes eius, & qui confortabant eam in temporibus. Et stabit de germine radicum eius plantatio: & veniet cum exercitu, & ingredietur provinciam regis Aquilonis: & abutetur eis, & obtinebit. Insuper & deos eorum, & sculptilia, vasa quoqùe preciosa auri & argenti, captiva ducet in Aegyptum:ipse praevalebit adversus regem Aquilonis. Et intrabit in regnum rex Austri, & revertetur ad terram suam. Filii autem eius provocabuntur, & congregabunt multitudinem exercituum plurimorum: & veniet properans & inundans: & revertetur & concitabitur, & congredietur cum robore eius. Et provocatus rex Austri egredietur, & pugnabit adversus regem Aquilonis, & praeparabit multitudinem nimiam, & dabitur multitudo in manus eius. Et capiet multitudinem, & exaltabitur cor eius, & deiiciet multa millia, sed non praevalebit. Convertetur enim rex Aquilonis, & praeparabit multitudinem multo maiorem quàm prius: & in fine temporum annorumque veniet properans cum exercitu magno & opibus nimiis. In temporibus illis multi consurgent adversus regem Austri: filii quoque praevaricatorum populi tui extollentur ut impleant visionem, et corruent. Et veniet rex Aquilonis, & comportabit aggerem, & capiet urbes munitissimas: & brachia Austri non sustinebunt, & consurgent electi eius ad resistendum, & non erit fortitudo. Et faciet veniens super eum iuxta placitum suum, & non erit qui stet contra faciem eius: & stabit in terra inclyta, & consumetur in manu eius. Et ponet faciem suam, ut veniat ad tenendum universum regnum eius, & recta faciet cum eo, & filiam feminarum dabit ei, ut evertat illud: & non stabit, nec illius erit. Et convertet faciem suam ad insulas, & capiet multas: & cessare faciet principem opprobrii sui, & opprobrium eius convertetur in eum. Et convertet faciem suam ad imperium terrae suae, & impinget, & corruet, & non invenietur. Et stabit in loco eius vilissimus, & indignus decore regio: & in paucis diebus conteretur, non in furore nec in praelio. Et stabit in loco eius despectus, & non tribuetur ei honor regius: & veniet clàm, & obtinebit regnum in fraudulentia. Et brachia pugnantis expugnabuntur a facie eius, & conterentur: insuper et dux foederis. Et post amicitias, cum eo faciet dolum: & ascendet, & superabit in modico populo. Et abundantes & uberes urbes ingredietur: & faciet quae non fecerunt patres eius, & patres patrum eius: rapinas et praedam et divitias eorum dissipabit, & contrà firmissimas cogitationes inibit: & hoc usque ad tempus. Et concitabitur fortitudo eius, et cor eius adversum regem Austri in exercitu magno: & rex Austri provocabitur ad bellum multis auxiliis & fortibus nimis: & non stabunt, quia inibunt adversus eum consilia. Et comedentes panem cum eo, conterent illum, exercitusque eius opprimetur: & cadent interfecti plurimi. Duorum quoque regum cor erit ut malefaciant, & ad mensam unam mendacium loquentur, & non proficient: quia adhuc finis in aliud tempus. Et revertetur in terram suam cum opibus multis: & cor eius adversum testamentum sanctum, & faciet, & revertetur in terram suam. Statuto tempore revertetur, & veniet ad Austrum: & non erit priori simile novissimum. Et venient super eum trieres, & Romani, & percutietur, & revertetur, & indignabitur contra testamentum sanctuarii, & faciet: reverteturque, & cogitabit adversum eos qui dereliquerunt testamentum sanctuarii. Et brachia ex eo stabunt, & polluent sanctuarium fortitudinis, & auferent iuge sacrificium, & dabunt abominationem in desolationem. Et impii in testamentum simulabunt fraudulenter: populus autem sciens deum suum, obtinebit & faciet. Et docti in populo docebunt plurimos: & ruent in gladio & in flamma, & in captivitate & in rapina dierum. Cumque corruerint, sublevabuntur auxilio parvulo: & applicabuntur eis plurimi fraudulenter. Et de eruditis ruent, ut conflentur, & eligantur & dealbentur usque ad tempus praefinitum: quia adhuc aliud tempus erit. Et faciet iuxta voluntatem suam rex, & elevabitur & magnifica bitur adversus omnem deum: & adversus Deum deorum loquetur magnifica, & dirigetur, donec compleatur iracundia: perpetrata quippe est definitio. Et deum patrum suorum non reputabit: & erit in concupiscentiis feminarum, nec quemquam deorum curabit, quia adversum universa consurget: deum autem Maozim in loco suo venerabitur: & deum quem ignoraverunt patres eius, colet auro & argento, et lapide precioso, rebusque pretiosis. Et faciet ut muniat Maozim cum deo alieno quem non cognovit, & multiplicabit gloriam, & dabit eis potestatem in multis, & terram dividet gratuito. Et in tempore praefinito, praeliabitur adversus eum rex Austri, & quasi tempestas veniet contra illum rex Aquilonis in curribus & in equitibus & in classe magna: & ingredietur terras, & conteret & pertransiet. Et introibit in terram gloriosam, et multi corruent: hae autem solae salvabun tur de manu eius, Edom & Moab, & principes filiorum Amnon. Et mittet manum suam in terras: & terra Aegypti non effugiet. Et dominabitur thesaurorum auri, & argenti, & in omnibus preciosis Aegypti: per Lybiam quoque et Aethiopiam transibit. Et fama turbabit eum ab Oriente & ab Aquilone: & veniet in multitudine magna, ut conterat et interficiat plurimos. Et figet tabernaculum suum Apadno inter duo maria, super montem inclytum & sanctum: & veniet usque ad summitatem eius, & nemo auxiliabitur ei.

Iam vaticinia de regnis inchoat, ut simul Danielem confirmet, & posteros praemoneat de impendentibus calamitatibus. Estque praedictio testimonium illustre, quod à deo traditam esse prophetarum doctrinam ostendit. Et quia pollicetur liberationem, significat deo curae esse hunc populum, qui doctrinam propheticam amplectitur. Confirmantur ergo pii, ne à deo deficiant, nec abiiciant huius doctrinae professionem.

Pertinet autem postrema pars huius longae concionis, etiam ad hanc ultimam mundi aetatem, et ad ecclesiae aerumnas, quas tulit iam multis seculis, dum alibi Mahometica rabies conatur prorsus delere nomen filii dei, alibi regnant Episcopi ethnico more, & studia doctrinae ecclesiasticae negligunt, sinunt extingui lucem Evangelii de vera invocatione in fide seu fiducia filii dei, propugnant idola et libidines, iniustè occidunt homines innocentes propter verae doctrinae professionem.

Accedunt interim & alia multa mala, etiam in iis locis ubi est emendata doctrina. Principes impediti, negligenter tuentur disciplinam, pauci sunt munifici in alendis Evangelii ministris, & fovendis studiis literarum. Confirmat igitur ἀναρχία petulantiam malorum, & neglectio literarum novas tenebras & novam barbariem minatur.

Haec mala pii considerent, ut primum à deo petant, ut ipse suam ecclesiam servet, regat, foveat, et augeat. Deinde si qui possunt aliquibus vulneribus mederi, annitantur pro sua vocationc, ne defuisse eos officio suo Christus in ultimo iudicio accuset, Esurivi, & non pavistis me, &c. Aulae, Curiae regant disciplinam, foveant docentes. Eruditi non fastidiant operas aut doctrinam ecclesiae. Nam maximè dignum est homine philosopho, quaerere veram de deo doctrinam, & celebrare deum. Adivuent igitur studia Evangelii, & concordiam doctrinae verae quadam moderatione & philosophia necessaria piis gubernatoribus retineant. Sed haec infra suo loco iterum dicenda erunt. Nunc ad historias redeo, quae hîc recitantur.

Primum observetur testimonium de angelorum excubiis. Narrat angelus se inde usque ab initio adfuisse Dario Medo, ut adivuaret & stabiliret eius regnum. Fortassis placidus senex fuit, spretus à principibus ac vicinis, ut fieri solet. Hunc tamen defendit pius angelus, adiuvit in praeliis, gubernavit periculosos eventus. Successus autem prosperi, praesertim cum magna fuerunt pericula, signa sunt divini auxiolii, ideo metum incutiunt hostibus aut invidis, & apud bonos augent principum reverentiam. Ut Ezechiae & Esaiae populus obtemperabat obsessa urbe, etsi res erat in summa desperatione.

Sed boni iudicabant regi divinitus consilia & negotia horum principum. Discamus igitur & nos angelorum praesidia & petere, & pietate retinere.

Mirae artes nunc exercentur in aulis. Et multata sunt consilia insidiosaκαὶ πλεονεκτικὰ,Hanc putant esse politicam sapientiam. Sed discant pii gubernatores, non posse retineri imperia sine auxilio dei. Quare huic obtemperent, faciant officia necessaria, & à deo expectent defensionem, sicut scriptum est, QUI DAT SALUTEM REGIBUS. Nec quaerant infida praesidia, nec incertissimis & fallacissimis hominum voluntatibus nitantur, nec molianturπλεονεκτήματα,nec implicent se non necessariis rebus.

POSTEA incipit vaticinari angelus de regno Persico. Stabunt, inquit, adhuc tres reges in Persia. Hîc insigniter hallucinantur Iudaei, qui putant tantum tres fuisse reges Persarum, & amittunt annos ex serie temporum, plures quàm centum. Possunt autem perspicue refutari per scriptores, qui singulis regibus Persicis coaetanei fuerunt. Sed angelus disertè inquit, Stabunt tres reges.

Singularis est emphasis in verbo stare apud prophetas. Nam stare regnum, significat florere, retinere potentiam nondum inclinatam.

Usitata enumeratio est post Cyrum, Camyysen, Darium, recensere quartum Xerxen. Nam Magos inferere supposititios reges, absurdum est. Floruit autem haec Monarchia virtute, victoriis & potentia, maximè sub tribus illis primis regibus. Xerxes verò bellum intulit Graecis in Europa, ubi cum victus esset, quassari Monarchia, & aliquanto pòst ruere coepit. Ac reditus Xerxis in Asiam non solum clade exercitus, sed etiam foeditate morum incesta libidine, & crudelitate Xerxis insignis fuit. Statim enim post fugam amare coepit fratris coniugem. Postea cum filiam fratris suo filio dedisset, exarsit & huius ivuenculae amore. Post hanc incestam consuetudinem, cum alia parricidia secuta sunt, tum verò etiam caedes fratris. Tandem & ipse Xerxes ab Artabano interfectus est.

Haec exempla eo recito, Primum, ut poenae nos admoneant, ut iudicium & iram dei timeamus, qui libidines severe punit. Deinde, ut cogitatione humanae imbecillitatis moveamur, ut petamus à deo, qui per filium suum promisit auxilium, ut ipse nos in omnibus vitae consiliis, & regendis moribus gubernet.

Natus Xerxes in familia Heroica, ex paren tibus quorum fuit excellens virtus, habuerat indolem praeclaram, & domestica disciplina diligenter ad virtutem institutus fuerat. Sed cum in tanta potentia et luxu non studet frenare cupiditates, tandem prorsus efferatur. Et immanitatem sequuntur haud dubiè poenae.

Haec exempla cum omnes homines tum praecipuè potentes ad modestiam & timorem dei hortentur. Meminerimus & hoc, quod inquit filius dei, Sine me nihil potestis facere. Et quod ecclesia canit de spiritu sancto, Sine tuo numine, nihil est in homine, nihil est innoxium. Quare nos flecti ad virtutem, & regi à spiritu sancto dei petamus.

SED redeo ad textum. Recitavi enumerationem regum usitatam, quam si quis non probat, monstret aptiorem. Nam & ego malim vaticinium de tribus intelligere, Cyro, Dario filio Hystaspis, & Artaxerxe Longimano, etiamsi interiectus est Xerxes, qui videlicet eodem tempore bellum gerit in Europa, quo domi regnat Longimanus, qui cum virtute & pietate excelleret, Monarchiam quassatam tamen adhuc fulciebat. Nec extra Europam multa Persis hoc regnante, adempta sunt. Postea secutae sunt defectiones provinciarum, Acgypti & aliarum. Secutum & bellum civile inter duos fratres, Cyrum iuniorem & Artaxerxen Mnemonem.

Nec alius magis beneficus fuit erga populum dei quàm Longimanus. Nam hoc regnante decretum de instauratione templi renovatum est. Et adparet eius praecipuos gubernatores fuisse Esdram & Nehemiam. Respondit igitur virtuti fortuna. Deus regem modestum, pium, benefacientem ecclesiae, protexit ac ivuit in gubernatione & defensione regni, dedit etiam ei placitum exitum. Huius ergo modestia & pietas imitanda est.

Hactenus de Persis concionatus est angelus, breviter quidem, sed tamen significans quosdam adhuc florentes reges fore, sed postea Graecos dilaceraturos esse Monarchiam. Incipit igitur de Graecis dicere.

## 11:1 Concerning Alexander
CONCERNING ALEXANDER.

AND a mighty king shall stand, and shall rule with great dominion great, and he shall do according to his will. And when he shall stand up, his kingdom shall be broken, and shall be divided toward the four winds of heaven; and not to his posterity, nor according to his dominion which he ruled: for his kingdom shall be plucked up, even for others beside those.

These words prophesy concerning Alexander, whom he also said above would obtain the Persian Monarchy. But he adds that it will come to pass that the Monarchy will be torn apart little by little, because many leaders are to succeed Alexander, who will divide the provinces among themselves, and will fight among themselves for the empire. Therefore he says that the kingdom will not come to his posterity, that is, not to any one heir born of him, but to strangers, namely to the leaders who had campaigned with him. He signifies, however, that there will be great calamities for the world, because the varied wars of the successors were going to cause frequent changes of affairs, which do not happen gently.

Most elegantly and most wisely had Demades said that, with Alexander dead, his army was like a Cyclops with his eye gouged out. For just as that great mass, having lost the light of its eye, had wandering and useless impulses in various directions, so the army without a leader rushed about in various ways, and had no certain kingdom could establish. Daily new treaties, new divisions of allies were being made. Those who a little before had been joined among themselves by most sacred treaties, afterwards having become enemies, were perishing by mutual slaughter, and were declaring that the voice which exists in a certain tragedy of Seneca is too true.

Faith never enters a royal threshold.

But let us say a few things about Alexander, so that the reader may rouse himself to the fear of God by the thought of these examples and of divine judgment.

FIRST, consider with how great gifts Alexander was adorned by God. It was a great honor that he was preferred by God to all other kings, and was endowed with the empire of the whole world, with riches, and with many glorious victories. All people look up to, admire, and highly value these things. And it is true that the benefits of God are to be highly valued. But I wish for other, far greater divine ornaments also to be looked at, which the common people do not admire to the same extent.

There was in Alexander an admirable force of nature; he surpassed the wisest old men in counsel, on account of the sagacity of his intellect, by which he was able both to judge the best things and to foresee outcomes. Then, how great was the loftiness of his spirit joined with a love of justice and moderation, despising dangers and labors, how great was his benevolence and kindness toward his own. Finally, of all the kings of the nations who are known to us from histories, there was no virtue as great as that of Alexander, while he was still sound, that is, before Babylon was captured. His character, therefore, was a far greater honor than the possession of the empire itself.

Endowed with these such great gifts, he did not acknowledge God as the author, and with his former intention of mind relaxed, he gave himself over to obscene pleasures, he gorged himself, he killed his friends while drunk, he raged against faithful and most deserving leaders, he was defiled by lusts. To these shameful acts he added manifest insults against God. He wished to be believed to have something of divine power above the condition of mortals, he did not acknowledge that he had performed such great things with the help of the eternal God, but he attributed this glory to his own wisdom and his own virtues. He would sometimes go out horned, like Jupiter Ammon, sometimes like Diana. Finally, he played with intolerable insolence.

Therefore, most sad punishments followed. He himself, with the kingdom not yet established, dies, when he had drunk too much wine at the banquet of Amasius. By this ruin, the entire house was crushed at the same time, somewhat Afterwards, the mother Olympias, the sister, the two wives, and the two small sons were killed by his own fellow soldiers.

What example can be commemorated more illustriously of the inconstancy of human affairs? The family and posterity of this such great king in the whole world had no faithful friend from those very ones to whom he himself had given provinces and whom he had elevated.

Cassander, the son of Antipater, who as a youth had been educated at the court of Alexander, killed first Olympias, the mother of Alexander, afterwards Roxane and her son Alexander, and besides this one, another son, Hercules, born from Barsine. This is the gratitude the fosterling returned to the prince who had deserved well of him. Pausanias narrates these things in the Boeotica concerning Cassander, ὁ κάσανδρος ὀλυμπιάδα γε παρέβαλε καταλεῦσαι τοῖς ἐπʼ ἀυτὴν μακεδόνων παρωξυσμένοις, καὶ τοὺς παῖδας ἀλεξάνδρου τὸν τὲ ἐκ βαρσίνης ἡρακλέα, καὶ ἀλέξανδρον τὸν ἐκ ρωξάνης ἀπέκτεινεν ὑπὸ φαρμάκων.. but the prefect of Sardis killed the sister, Cleopatra, so that he might gratify Antigonus, although he denied that she had been killed by his order, for he was a brother, the bastard of Philip.

I have reviewed the calamities of the family of Alexander, which, since they did not happen by chance, surely warn us of the wrath of God. God punishes many crimes in this life, and let us consider that a judgment remains after this life, in which all who do not amend their life will be cast into eternal punishments. Moreover, let the brevity of this life remind us that God does not propose rewards and punishments only in this life, but much more in that future eternity. For whatever will at some time be finished must be considered brief; the life of man is certainly a short course. Let us know, therefore, that a judgment remains concerning eternal rewards and punishments.

Latin:

DE ALEXANDRO.

ET stabit rex fortis, & dominabitur dominatione magna, & faciet secundum voluntatem suam. Et cum steterit, conteretur regnum eius, & dispergetur in quatuor ventos caeli, & non ad posteros suos, nec erit tanta potentia succes sorum, quanta ipsius fuit, quia extirpabitur regnum eius, & ad alienos transferetur.

Haec verba de Alexandro vaticinantur, quem & suprà dixit, Monarchia Persica potiturum esse. Sed addit fore, ut paulo paulst dilaceretur Monarchia, quia Alexandro multi duces successuri sint, partituri inter se provincias, & dimicaturi inter se de imperio. Ideo inquit, perventurum regnum non ad posteros, id est, non ad unum aliquem haeredem ex ipso natum, sed ad alienos, videlicet ad duces qui cum ipso militaverant. Significat autem magnas fore ea lamitates orbis terrarum, quia bella tam varia successorum factura erant crebras rerum mutationes, quae non fiunt molliter.

Venustissimè & sapientissime dixerat Demades, Alexandro mortuo, exercitum eius similem esse Cyclopi effosso oculo. Ut enim illa magna moles, amissa luce oculi, variè errantes impetus & inutiles habebat, ita exercitus sine duce variè ruebat, & nullum certum regnum constituere poterat. Quotidie nova foedera, novae distractiones sociorum fiebant. Qui paulò antè, inter se sanctissimis foederibus coniuncti fuerant, postea facti hostes, mutua laniena peribant, ac declarabant nimis veram esse vocem, quae extat in quadam tragoedia Senecae.

Non intrat unquam regium limen fides.

Sed pauca de Alexandro dicamus, ut lector cogitatione horum exemplorum & iudicii divini se ad timorem dei exuscitet.

PRIMUM, quantis donis à deo ornatus sit Alexander, considera. Magnum decus fuit, quòd caeteris regibus omnibus à deo praelatus est, & donatus orbis terrarum imperio, opibus, multis gloriosis victoriis. Haec suspiciunt, admirantur, magnifaciunt omnes. Et esse dei beneficia magnifacienda, verum est. Sed volo alia divina ornamenta longe maiora etiam aspici, quae vulgus non perinde miratur.

Erat in Alexandro admiranda naturae vis, sapientissimos senes consilio vincebat, propter ingenii sagacitatem, qua & diiudicare optima poterat, & prospicere eventus. Deinde quanta celsitudo animi coniuncta cum amore iustitiae & moderationis, despiciens pericula & labores, quanta erga suos benevolentia & comitas. Denique nullius regum ethnieorum omnium qui nobis noti sunt ex historiis, tanta virtus fuit quanta fuit Alexandri, cum adhuc sanus esset, hoc est, ante captam Babylonem. Erat igitur ingenium eius, longè maius decus, quàm ipsa imperii possessio.

His tantis muneribus donatus, non agnovit deum autorem, & remissa priore animi intentione, dedidit se obscoenis voluptatibus, helluatus est, interfecit ebrius amicos, saeviit in duces fideles & optimè meritos, pollutus est libidinibus. His flagitiis addidit manifestas contumelias adversus deum. Credi voluit se supra mortalium conditionem, quiddam divini numinis habere, non agnoscebat se res tantas deo aeterno ivuante gessisse, sed suae sapientiae & suis virtutibus hanc gloriam tribuebat. Prodibat interdum cornutus, ut Iupiter Hammon, interdum ut Diana. Denique intolerabili petulantia ludebat.

Secutae sunt igitur tristissimae poenae. Ipse nondum constituto regno, moritur, cum nimium hausisset vini in convivio Amasii. Hac ruina simul oppressa est universa domus, aliquanto post, mater Olympias, soror, uxores duae, duo filii parvi interfecti sunt ab ipsius commilitonibus.

Quod exemplum commemorari potest illustrius inconstantiae rerum humanarum? Huius tanti regis familia & posteritas toto orbe terrarum, nullum fidum amicum habuit ex illis ipsis, quibus ipse provincias dederat, & quos evexerat.

Cassander Antipairi filius, qui adolescens apud Alexandrum educatus erat, interfecit primum Olympiadem Alexandri matrem, postea Roxanen & eius filium Alexandrum, & praeter hunc alterum filium Herculem ex Barsine natum. Hanc gratiam alumnus principi bene merito reddidit. Narrat haec Pausanias in Boeoticis de Cassandro,ὁ κάσανδρος ὀλυμπιάδα γε παρέβαλε καταλεῦσαι τοῖς ἐπʼ ἀυτὴν μακεδόνων παρωξυσμένοις, καὶ τοὺς παῖδας ἀλεξάνδρου τὸν τὲ ἐκ βαρσίνης ἡρακλέα, καὶ ἀλέξανδρον τὸν ἐκ ρωξάνης ἀπέκτεινεν ὑπὸ φαρμάκων..Sororem verò Cleopatram praefectus Sardium interfecit, ut Antigono gratificaretur, etsi is negavit suo iussu interfectam esse, fuit enim frater, Philippi nothus.

Recensui calamitates familiae Alexandri, quae cum non acciderint casu, profecto nos de ira dei admonent. Multa deus flagitia in hac vita punit, & cogitemus restare iudicium post hanc vitam, in quo in aeternas poenas coniicientur omnes qui vitam non emendant. Brevitas autem huius vitae commonefaciat nos, deum non tantum praemia et poenas in hac vita proponere, sed multo magis in illa futura aeternitate. Quic quid enim aliquando finietur, breve ducendum est, vita certè hominis breve curriculum est. Sciamus igitur restare iudicium de aeternis praemiis & poenis.

## 11:1 Concerning the Successors of Alexander
CONCERNING THE SUCCESSORS of Alexander.

WHEN after the death of Alexander his brother Aridaeus held the royal name, under whom the supreme governor was Perdiccas, the provinces were distributed among the leaders, in such a way that all were ordered to obey King Aridaeus and Perdiccas. Perdiccas therefore seeks, because he was stronger in authority than the others, the marriage of Alexander's sister Cleopatra. And in order to remove from the midst those whom he judged to be opposing his plans, he orders Meleager to be killed, he constructs traps for Antigonus, Who when he had fled to Antipater in Macedonia, Eumenes was sent to wage war against Antipater and Antigonus, with whom Ptolemy also had joined himself. Perdiccas therefore leads an army into Egypt, to expel Ptolemy from there. But deserted by his own army, Perdiccas is killed by his own horsemen, in the third year after the death of Alexander. Thus the first author of the sedition paid the penalty, which were widely spread. For soon also the brother of Perdiccas, Alcetas, was killed, and his sister.

Elsewhere indeed, when Eumenes had fought with the army of Antipater, Craterus and Neoptolemus perished. These were the beginnings of huge disturbances, for with so many leaders killed, hatreds had now flared up, and new treaties were being made, and individuals were preparing themselves for new struggles. There were, however, two most fierce of all, Antigonus and Eumenes. These fighting among themselves, drew the rest into parties. Finally, Antigonus the victor captured Eumenes by treachery, surrendered by the soldiers, and killed him.

Eumenes had been, however, a scribe under Philip and Alexander, and in these tumults he showed that he did not serve his own utility, but to defend heirs, Alexander's mother, sister, wife, and children. And because the appearance of duty, and especially of piety toward orphans, is of great value in conciliating and retaining the wills of ordinary men, by this title he joined and bound the veteran Macedonians to himself.

But far greater and more difficult wars followed. Arrhidaeus having died, the individual leaders attempted to claim for themselves the opportune provinces. Cassander, the son of Antipater, in order to retain Macedonia, killed the two sons of Alexander the Great, as I narrated above. Antigonus held Asia with his son Demetrius, Ptolemy held Egypt, Seleucus held Syria and the neighboring provinces as far as Persia. But the furies of Cassander provided the cause of a new war among the others, who, in order to pay the penalty for his crimes, moved war against Antigonus, intending to snatch from him certain provinces in Asia. He had, however, made an alliance with Lysimachus, Ptolemy, and Seleucus, who feared the growing power of Antigonus.

Although Antigonus saw how great a mass of war was impending, yet he was not broken in spirit. And the first successes against Cassander confirmed him. Antigonus judged this man to be an enemy, because of the murdered Olympias, and he forced him to surrender the cities and armies in Asia.

Afterwards he attacked Seleucus and Ptolemy. That was a long and difficult war. Demetrius, the son of Antigonus, was first defeated in battle by Ptolemy at Tyre, where, when Ptolemy had captured the tent of Demetrius, which was furnished with royal equipment, he restored the entire equipment to him along with the captives, signifying that he was contending for glory, not for wealth.

Some time later Demetrius, when he had suddenly overwhelmed Cilles, the general of Ptolemy, and eight thousand soldiers, so that a surrender was made without a struggle, mindful of the kindness he had received, he returned the favor in full measure. For he sent back both the general unharmed and the army to Ptolemy, having ordered him to depart from Syria. Thus those princes contended among themselves, not only in virtue on the battlefield, but also in humanity and moderation.

At last, the supreme issue was fought over by all the kings, not far from Ephesus. In one camp were Seleucus, his son Antiochus, Lysimachus, and the auxiliaries of Ptolemy. In the other camp were Antigonus and his son Demetrius. On that night which preceded the battle, there was seen Through a dream Alexander the Great told Antigonus that he was now crossing over to the enemy. Antigonus, thinking that the outcome of the following battle was signified by that dream, declared his son his successor before the army. Afterwards in the battle, while Antigonus was pursuing Antiochus, the son of Seleucus, too recklessly, he was surrounded by the cavalry of Seleucus and killed, when he had already reached his eightieth year. The army having been scattered, his son Demetrius fled to Athens, and some time later he renewed the war, but having been captured by Seleucus, he did not survive his father for long.

NOW there remained of the leaders of Alexander only two, who until now had been allies, Seleucus and Lysimachus, holding Thrace and a part of Macedonia. This Lysimachus, when he was an attendant of Alexander, having been more familiar with Callisthenes, was thrown to a lion. But when he had remained unharmed after the lion was killed, he was afterwards dearer to Alexander on account of his virtue.

But to omit the rest of the history of Lysimachus, I shall add only this. Strabo writes that while he was waging war with the neighboring Getae, he was captured in battle and led into the Getic land, where the Getic king, when he had shown the daily custom of his life and nation, which living most harshly, and

excelling in strength, he easily surpassed other nations in military matters, and he exhorted Lysimachus to prefer to have such neighbors as friends rather than enemies, and that he would have them as friends if he did not bring arms against them. Then, having gifted him honorably, he dismissed him, having agreed upon nothing except peace. Pausanias relates that his son was captured, but whichever one it was, the humanity is worthy of praise, and it signifies that in that people, which it is certain was Gothic, there was an understanding and pursuit of virtue.

But this Lysimachus also deformed his virtues with lust; he married two sisters and fathered children by both. But one killed the son of the other. Afterwards, the mother, a fugitive, sought hiding places among related kings.

Hence the cause of the war arose between Seleucus and Lysimachus, which Pausanias recounts. Therefore, Lysimachus perished, defeated by Seleucus, who, now holding the greatest part of the Monarchy, alone of the leaders of Alexander survives. And so, elated by such great successes, he hopes that he will also gain possession of the empire of Macedonia and Greece. And when he transfers the war into Europe, in the seventh month after the death of Lysimachus, he is killed by Ptolemy κεραυνῷ, who, since he was the brother of Ptolemy Philadel who, already reigning in Egypt, had sailed into Europe, in order to occupy Macedonia. Nor long after, this κεραυνὸς was killed in battle by Brennus, the Celtic leader, who was ravaging Macedonia.

I AM ALMOST wearied by reciting the slaughter of these gladiators, in which, if anyone should look at the confusion by human judgment, he might think the constitutions and changes of kingdoms to be fortuitous. But the pious judge otherwise. Let them know that the crimes of both princes and peoples are punished by God, and yet afterwards the political order is restored, sometimes more happily, sometimes more sadly.

BUT FINALLY after so many huge wars, God divided these kingdoms in this way. The posterity of Ptolemy retained Egypt, up to the times of Julius Caesar. Antiochus, the son of Seleucus, possessed Asia and Syria. But his descendants lost Asia, occupied partly by Eumenes of Bithynia, partly by the Galatians. There was a struggle for a long time over the possession of Macedonia. But in the end, the posterity of Demetrius, who was the son of Antigonus, having been captured by Seleucus, as I said, held Macedonia, until the Romans occupied it. The adolescent sons of Cassander and Lysimachus perished through most foul parricides.

Ptolemy κεραυνὸς, having taken his sister Arsinoe and afterwards killed her, he himself had also invaded Macedonia. But having been killed by Brennus, he paid the penalty for his incestuous habit with his sister, and for his acts of parricide.

Afterwards Antigonus, son of Demetrius, a most moderate king, born of a most praiseworthy mother, Phila, daughter of Antipater, took possession of Macedonia, which, although he lost it several times, he nevertheless recovered afterwards. He expelled the Gauls from Greece, and afterwards waged wars with Pyrrhus. And when the head of the slain Pyrrhus had been brought to him, and his son Helenus had been surrendered, he sent him back to his paternal kingdom, and gave him his father's head and bones, to be buried in royal fashion. Diodorus Siculus praises his mother Phila in these words, αὐτὴ δὲ ἡ γυνὴ συνέσει ἐδόκει διενηνοχέναι, τούς τε γὰρ ταραχώδεις ἐν τῷ στρατοπέδῳ κατέπαυσεν, τάς τε ἀδελφὰς καὶ θυγατέρας τῶν ἀπόρων ἐξεδίδου ἴδίοις δαπανήμασι, πολλοὺς καὶ τῶν διαβολαὶς περιπιπτόντων μὴ δικαίως, ἀπηλλαξε κινδύνων,, etc.

We have woven a sufficiently long catalog of kings, in whose history it is useful to compare the end of each one to morals, so that, being admonished concerning the judgment of God, Let us govern our morals more diligently.

Furthermore, Daniel, having omitted the other kingdoms, now prophesies only concerning two, the Syrian and the Egyptian, because Judea, situated in the midst of these, was harshly shaken for nearly three hundred years. Meanwhile, the other kingdoms had no business with Judea. Wherefore, having passed over the others, he predicts the principal movements of these two. And because the prophecy is almost a history of several centuries, it is useful to add catalogs of the Syrian and Egyptian kings, so that the narrative may be aptly accommodated to the names of the kings.

KINGS OF SYRIA.

Seleucus. Antiochus Soter. Antiochus Theos. Seleucus Callinicus, and his brother Antiochus Hierax.

Antiochus the Great, born to his father Seleucus, had a brother Seleucus, older by birth, who nevertheless was killed immediately after he had begun to reign.

Seleucus and his brothers Antiochus Epiphanes and Demetrius. φιλοπάτωρ,

Antiochus born to his father Epiphanes. εὐπάτωρ

Demetrius the younger and his brother Antiochus, sons of Demetrius.

Antiochus Grypus, son of Demetrius the younger. Antiochus Cyzicenus, son of Sidetes. With the posterity of Seleucus extinguished, not long after the Romans occupied Syria.

SELEUCUS was born in Macedonia in a middling station. He had for a father a certain one of the leaders of Philip. They say that Seleucus and his posterity were marked on the thigh by a natural anchor. But when, after the death of Perdiccas, he was prefect of Babylon, a dissension having arisen between Antigonus and the others, he joined a part of Syria, and defended it with great struggles against Antigonus, whom he finally overcame in a huge battle, and afterwards he also captured the son of Antigonus, Demetrius. Then, having conquered in battle, he killed Lysimachus. Finally, surrounded by treachery, he was killed by Ptolemy κεραυνῶ brother of Philadelphus.

He left to his sons a most ample kingdom, Babylon, Persis, the Medes, Bactra, Parthia, Asia, and a part of Syria. For Ptolemy held a part, who had been an ally against Antigonus.

Josephus writes that he was beneficent toward the Jews, whom he permitted to use their own laws in their own cities. Pausanias also praises his justice and clemency.

Antiochus σωτὴρ was present at the battle of his father against Antigonus, nor did he reign long after his father.

Antiochus θεὸς had at the beginning as a wife Laodice, from whom he received two sons, Seleucus Callinicus, and Antiochus to whom was the surname ἱέραξ.

Afterwards Ptolemy Philadelphus, so that by that occasion he might recover the cities lost in Syria, gave to Antiochus θεῷ his daughter Berenice. After these second nuptials, the husband was not long surviving. And this marriage provided the occasion for new movements and discords of the kings. Laodice, having been repudiated, exhorted her sons, that they should not allow the kingdom to be snatched from them. Wherefore Seleucus, the elder by birth, surrounded with a siege the city in which was Berenice with her small son. And when he had persuaded her to make a surrender, he killed her, having been surrendered, with her son. These were the auspices of the reign of Seleucus, who afterwards paid the penalties for his parricide.

Seleucus Callinicus, after he killed his stepmother, by this crime summoned a huge war upon himself, For Ptolemy Euergetes, so that for his sister he might take vengeance, he brought war upon Seleucus, and having routed and put him to flight, he took away a great part of Syria.

There was, however, in the other part of the kingdom, namely in Asia, his brother Antiochus ἱέραξ. From him Seleucus sought aid. Hierax eagerly undertook the expedition, hoping that he alone would gain possession of the whole kingdom. He brought up mercenary troops of Galatians, who had already been drawn into Asia by pay. Seleucus, because he distrusted his brother's will, made peace with Euergetes. Wherefore, angered, ἱέραξ, he brought war upon his brother, whom, having defeated in battle and driven from Syria, new disturbances arose in Asia, which Eumenes the Bithynian was occupying while empty, and the Galatians were wandering through it everywhere. And the Egyptian king had invaded Cilicia.

When, returning into Asia, he had met these disturbances, ἱέραξ having received great disasters, he fled to his father-in-law in Cappadocia, from whom, sensing that snares were being laid for him, shortly after, he sought other hiding places, and betook himself to his enemy in Egypt. Distrusting him also, while he was wandering in out-of-the-way places in flight, he was killed. His brother Seleucus, at almost the same time, having been thrown from his horse, was extinguished. This was the end of the two impious brothers, who reigned for no more than three years.

Daniel recounts the cause of these wars, saying, The daughter of the king of the South, namely Berenice, daughter of the Egyptian king, shall come to the king of the North, that is, the Syrian, Antiochus θεόν, to make friendship. And his arm shall not be strong, and his seed shall not stand, that is, Berenice shall be killed with her son. Then he adds that the king of Egypt, namely Euergetes, shall come and shall occupy a part of the Syrian kingdom.

Thus, the causes having been briefly set forth, and mention of the wars having been made, he signifies that there would be great calamities in those regions, in which also neighboring Judea was badly afflicted, as it is said, Something of evil because of an evil neighbor.

Antiochus the Great had a brother, Seleucus, older by birth, who, immediately after he had begun to reign, was killed by his own men in the crossing of the Taurus, as Polybius narrates, in whom the beginnings of the deeds of Antiochus the Great are extant. The rest are missing, which if we had, much light would be added to the prophecy of Daniel, and the calculation, about which it was spoken above, would become more illustrious.

Antiochus as a youth waged his first war with the leader Molon, who had defected from him. Him, finally conquered in battle and captured, he drove to the cross.

Afterwards he recovered the cities of Syria, which the Egyptians had occupied in the former war, partly by force, and partly he invited them to surrender by certain agreements.

This was the cause of a new and great war between Antiochus the Great and Ptolemy Philopator, which is here predicted by Daniel. But the preparations and the battle of the kings are described by Polybius. Ptolemy led an army into Syria, seventy thousand infantry, and five thousand cavalry. The forces of Antiochus were sixty thousand infantry, and six thousand cavalry. These armies clashed in Syria, and Ptolemy was the victor, and he recovered the lost cities. To Antiochus, who was asking, peace was granted. For Ptolemy, although he could have advanced and snatched more parts of the kingdom from Antiochus, nevertheless, because of his sloth, he rejoiced to be freed from the labor of warfare. Just as Polybius also narrates, συνεχώρησε σπονδὰς ἐνιαυσίους ὀυκ ἀλλότριος ὢν τῆς ἡσυχίας ἀλλὰ ἐἰπερ τὸ δέον οἰκείως ἑλκόμενος, ὑπὸ τῆς συνήθους ῥαθυμίας καὶ καχεξίας.

When, however, after a few years Ptolemy Philopator had died at home, leaving behind his underage son Ptolemy Epiphanes, Antiochus again stirs up war. For because of the infancy of the enemy, He saw that he would more easily incline the wills of the states to defection.

The Egyptians, however, sent ambassadors to Rome, and requested that the Roman senate should undertake the guardianship of the royal boy. When this had been done, the senate ordered Antiochus to abstain from Egypt. Taking this ill, and having as a guest Hannibal, who was an encourager that the other kings should join themselves together to repress the Romans, he made an alliance with Philip, king of the Macedonians. These were the seeds of the war with the Romans, which is described by Livy. At length, however, Antiochus, being defeated, ceded to the Romans all of Asia on this side of Mount Taurus. He was also compelled to give as hostages his two sons, Antiochus Epiphanes and Demetrius. Some time later, intending to despoil a certain temple in Syria, he was killed by his own men.

It is an easy conjecture, however, that Judea was gravely vexed in these Syrian wars, now by Syrian, now by Egyptian leaders. Ptolemy Philopator came to Jerusalem as a victor, and since, with the curiosity customary to kings, he was about to see the most celebrated temple, and had already entered into the vestibule, the priests prayed that he would not proceed, nor profane the sanctuary. And when a sudden having been divinely restrained by consternation, and led out of the temple, he began to be vehemently angry at the Jews, and afterwards he raged against them atrociously even in Egypt. These things are recounted in the third book of Maccabees.

Afterwards Scopas, a general sent by the Egyptian Epiphanes, occupied certain towns of Judea. But when he had been defeated by Antiochus the Great at the Jordan, the Jews surrendered themselves to the victor, who, although he did not yet rule them cruelly, nevertheless imposed tributes, and assigned a part to himself, and a part to his son-in-law Ptolemy Epiphanes. These were the beginnings of the Jewish servitude under these Syrian kings. Therefore it is said here in Daniel, He shall stand in the desirable land, namely Antiochus the Great. Then he shall turn his face to the islands, and shall take many, that is, he shall depart from there, about to wage war with the Romans in Greece and in Asia.

Antiochus the Great left three sons, Seleucus Philopator, Antiochus Epiphanes, and Demetrius. Seleucus reigned for a short time after his father. Describing this Seleucus, the angel says, There shall stand in his place, a vile or slothful one, a quaestor of royal honor. He calls him vile or slothful, a king negligent of his duties, nor diligently exercising judgments at home, neither gravely maintaining discipline, nor consulting the utilities of the people, nor driving away enemies from abroad, but given over to pleasures, and squandering public and private monies on useless things, pomps, banquets, and Lucullan buildings. And because no wealth suffices for these follies, exhausting the resources of private individuals with infinite plunderings. Therefore he calls him a Quaestor, but with royal honor, that is, ruling with a royal title. Nor without cause does the angel depict this Quaestor. For he signifies that in the last times there will be such empires of kings and princes, to which the church will be subject.

IT IS however of a good mind to consider human affairs, and to be affected by common miseries. Therefore, reader, I exhort you that you now compare the empires of Europe to this image.
