Onias contra mandatum dei templum extruit, sacrificia alieno loco facit, & ne deesset praetextus, citatEsaiam, qui cap. 19.inquit, in die illa erit altare domini in medio terrae Aegypti, &c. Hoc cum dicatur de vocatione gentium & de Evangelio, quod, ut caeteras gentes, ita & Aegyptios ad agnitionem filii dei, & vitae aeternae vocaturum erat, Onias detorsit ad suum consilium, Ita impostores, aut fascinati pravis opinionibus, omnibus temporibus abutuntur voce dei, & eam à genuina sententia, ad confirmanda sua deliria, transferunt.

Damnat autem angelus hoc loco templi illius Aegyptii aedificationem, & vocat id consilium praevaricationem seu violationem legis divinae & audaciam. Hoc iudicium omnibus aetatibus observent, & qui dogmata servut contra verbum dei, & qui cultus dei instituunt sine mandato divino, doctrinae corruptelas esse, praevaricationem, ac violationem primi & secundi mandati, nihil dubium est.

Deinde horribilis audacia est, cum certissima extent testimonia, deum nullo scelere magis offendi, quàm corruptelis doctrinae, contemptis omnibus minis & exemplis, tamen ludere falsis opinionibus & contumeliosis adversus deum. Quanta fuit audacia Ierobeam, cum contra apertum mandatum dei, suum quoddam templum condit, & instituit novum sacerdotium? Quantus furor in ethnicis, cum excogitârunt portentosos cultus, Priapi & alios? Quanta audacia fuit Mahometi, qui prorsus abolet universam doctrinam prophetarum & apostolorum? Sed haec audacia non est humand, singulares furiae sunt, immissae in pectus Mahometo à diabolo. Nec est exigua audacia pontificum et monachorum, qui manifesta idola, ut invocationem mortuorum & suas missas defendunt. Quare timere deum discamus, &

nos intra metas divinitus praescriptas cohibeamus, frenemus etiam ingeniorum atque animorum impetus, ne ambitione, aemulatione,Φιλονεικίαveras sententias conturbemus.

ET veniet rex Aquilonis, & vallo cinget urbes, & capiet eas, &c. Hîc redit angelus ad Antiochi magni historiam, quem ait multas urbes occupaturum esse, regnante Ptolemaeo Epiphane, sed adhuc impubere.

Breviter autem inserit particulam de Iudaea, quae in istis bellis utrinque vexata est. Et stabit in terra desiderabili, & deficiet manus eius, id est, Antiochus magnus veniet in Iudaeam, & victo Scopa duce Aegyptio, Iudaeis tributum imponet. Quorum manus deficient, id est, non repugnabunt, quia sine ullo certamine imperata fecerunt, dederunt tributum hac pactione, ut retinerent suas leges et cultus divinitus traditos.

Iterum igitur in servitute est ecclesia, & tamen deus ita mitigat calamitatem, ut maneat libertas docendae & audiendae legis divinae nec mutetur sacerdotium, nec abolcantur pii cultus. Nec sine caussa toties hic populus circa finem in servitute est, etsi promissiones habuit de libertate & conservatione suae politiae. Sed imago est ista servitus miseriarum ecclesiae in ultima senecta mundi.

Et veniet ut amicitiam cum eo faciat, & dabit ei filiam, ut eum perdat, id est, Antiochus magnus dabit filiam Aegyptio regi, ut ea conditione & pacem faciat, & Syriae urbes retineat, & augeat sui regni opes. Quae res tamen eum fefellit, quia post Antiochum magnum secuta est insignis inclinatio regni Syriaci.

Porrò in foedere facto cum Aegyptiis hoc tempore ab Antiocho magno, fuit & haec conditio, partem tributorum addixit Antiochus regno Syriae, partem genero Ptolemaeo Epiphani. Ita reges inter se partiebantur opes ecclesiae, ut nunc reges suis purpuratis tradunt titulos & reditus episcoporum.

Et convertet faciem suam ad insulas, & capiet multas, id est, navigabit in Graeciam, ubi se alique Graecae civitates ipsi adiungent, bellum moventi contra Romanos. Sed princeps quidam faciet eum cessare cum dedecore, inquit angelus, id est, consul Romanus vincet eum, et coget eum pacem petere, & conditiones duras accipere, vide icet, ut tota Asia cedat, citra Taurum montem.

Postea, inquit, rediturum ad suas urbes & interiturum. Nam cum redisset in Syriam Antiochus magnus, & propter inopiam templum quoddam spoliaturus esset, ibi interfectus est, cum vulgus ad templi defensionem concurrisset. Hic fuit exitus regis Antiochi, quem vel inquieta natura ingenii, vel ambitio incenderat, ut multa bella moveret, cum tamen ferè semper adversam fortunam experiretur. Pernicies est autem reipublicae, bella non necessaria movere, praesertim adversante fortuna.

## 11:1 Concerning Seleucus the Son of Antiochus the Great
CONCERNING SELEUCUS THE SON of Antiochus the Great.

AND there shall stand in his place a vile quaestor in royal honor, and he shall perish quickly, not by strength, nor in war.

These are the words in the text, by which Seleucus is described, who reigned for a short time after Antiochus the Great, when his brothers were hostages in Rome. This description should be diligently observed, because it is an image of government in Europe, for the most part.

He calls this Seleucus a vile or slothful quaestor or plunderer. This is the true interpretation of the words, and it signifies that he was idle a prince, not defending the fatherland abroad with arms, nor governing judgments, discipline, and morals at home, but exhausting the resources of his own people with great extortions, so that funds might be available for pleasures and other vain things, upon which courts generally pour out money without measure. Furthermore, this Seleucus also orders the temple in the city of Jerusalem to be plundered.

If you compare this image to our own age, you will understand that the angel did not preach to that time alone, but signified the calamities and servitude of the church of the last time, so that we may consider our sins to be punished by slothful and harsh governments, and yet that the church will meanwhile be a care to God.

Kings and princes ought to defend the churches of God with a common spirit and joined forces against the Turkish barbarity, which attempts to destroy the name of Christ, letters, and honest discipline from the foundation. At home they ought to govern judgments and morals. How many people care for these proper duties? Most are idle and indulge in pleasures, and can with difficulty tolerate expenses. Therefore, new extortions are devised without measure. That voice of John is despised, Be content your wages. The example of Ahab is despised, whom God testified that He would punish on account of the vineyard snatched from the citizen Naboth. The resources of the citizens ought to have been spared, so that they might be able to educate their children in virtue and honest arts, and so that in a time of necessity, they might be able to contribute larger sums of money against external enemies.

But as I have said, let us acknowledge these public miseries to be the punishment for our sins, and let us rouse ourselves to piety, and commend the church to God, and pray that He may mitigate public evils, and grant governors who are wise, just, strong, happy, looking out for the utility of their citizens, and desiring to adorn the churches and illustrate the glory of God.

What now are empires for the most part, if not sufficiently bitter exactions, that is, plunderings, to which recourse is had so that useless profusions may be tolerated? Judgments do not serve discipline or morals, but exactions. What wars are waged that are necessary and pious?

And the courts of bishops surpass other courts in sloth, profusions, and βουλιμία For nowhere do bishops and colleges perform ecclesiastical duties; everywhere they squander revenues, having defrauded to pastors and scholars, to whom expenses for studies ought to be provided from the wealth that has been donated to the churches. But let us pray to God the eternal Father of our deliverer Jesus Christ, that He may mitigate these evils, and not allow His church, and pious studies, to perish utterly.

Latin:

DE SELEUCO FILIO Antiochi magni.

ET stabit loco illius vilis quaestor honore regio, & citò peribit, non strenuitate, nec in bello.

Haec sunt verba in textu, quibus describitur Seleucus, qui exiguo tempore post Antiochum magnum regnavit, cum fratres Romae obsides essent. Diligenter autem haec descriptio observetur, quia imago est gubernationis in Europa, magna ex parte.

Vocat hunc Seleucum, vilem seu ignavum quaestorem seu expilatorem. Haec vera est verborun interpretatio, & significat fuisse ociosum principem, non foris propugnantem armis patriam, nec domi regentem iudicia, disciplinam, mores, sed magnis expilationibus exhaurientem facultates suorum, ut sumptus suppeterent ad voluptates & alias res inanes, in quas aulae plerunque pecuniam sine modo profundunt. Porrò hic Seleucus etiam templum in urbe Ierosolyma spoliari iubet.

Si conferas hanc imaginem ad nostram aetatem, intelliges angelum non illi tantum tempori concionatum esse, sed significasse calamitates & servitutem ecclesiae postremi temporis, ut cogitemus puniri peccata nostra ignavis & acerbis imperiis, & tamen deo interim curae fore ecclesiam.

Reges & principes communi animo & coniunctis copiis defendere ecclesias dei adversus Turcicam barbariem debebant, quae nomen Christi, literas, & honestam disciplinam funditus delere conatur. Domi debebant regere iudicia, & mores. Haec propria munera quotus quisque curat? Plerique ociosi indulgent voluptatibus, & difficulter sumptus tolerare possunt. Ideo excogitantur novae expilationes sine modo. Vox illa Ioannis contemnitur, Estote contenti stipendiis vestris. Contemnitur exemplum Achab, quem deus testatus est se punire propter vineam ereptam civi Naboth. Parcendum erat facultatibus civium, ut educare liberos ad virtutem & honestas artes possent, & ut necessario tempore, adversus externos hostes conferre pecunias ampliores possent.

Sed ut dixi, agnoscamus has miserias publicas, poenam esse nostrorum peccatorum, et nos ipsos ad pietatem excitemus, & deo ecclesiam commendemus, ac oremus, ut mitiget mala publica, det gubernatores sapientes, iustos, fortes, felices, prospicientes utilitati suorum civium, cupientes ornare ecclesias, & illustrare gloriam dei.

Quid nunc sunt imperia magna ex parte, nisi satis acerbae quaesturae, hoc est, expilationes, ad quas decurritur, ut tolerari profusiones inutiles possint? Iudicia non disciplinae, non moribus serviunt, sed quaesturae. Bella necessaria & pia quae geruntur?

Et aulae episcoporum vincunt alias aulas ignavia, profusionibus, &βουλιμίαNusquam enim episcopi & collegia faciunt offitia ecclesiastica, ubique profundunt reditus, defraudatis pastoribus & scholasticis, quibus ad studia sumptus praeberi debebant ex opibus quae donatae sunt ecclesiis. Deum autem aeternum patrem liberatoris nostri Iesu Christi oremus, ut haec mala mitiget, nec sinat funditus interire suam ecclesiam, & pia studia.

## 11:1 Concerning Antiochus Epiphanes
CONCERNING ANTIOCHUS EPIPHANES.

AND there shall stand in his place a despised person, and the honor of the kingdom shall not be given to him, he shall come peaceably, and shall obtain the kingdom by flatteries.

Here begins the description of Antiochus Epiphanes, whom he calls despised. There are singular emphases in the prophets, which do not speak of common or small matters. For someone might wonder why he calls Antiochus despised, since he was a legitimate heir, ingenious, and later even waged many wars. But I judge he is called despised because he was not divinely adorned with authority. A singular gift of God in a prince is authority or majesty, which consists of three things: virtue, good fortune, and the inclination of the minds of the people.

As in Scipio, although he was a youth, yet there was authority, or majesty. First, exceptional virtue shone in him, and it appeared that he was helped divinely. But the minds of men are inflamed by the admiration of virtue, and the opinion of the presence of God, or of divine aid. Although these things contribute much to preparing and maintaining authority, yet a third gift of God must also be added, namely, the inclinations of minds divinely produced in the people themselves.

To this pertains that memorable sentence of Solomon, That the eye may see, and the ear may hear, God makes both, that is, that a governor may be strong in counsel and virtue, and then that the people may obey, and not cast off those who advise rightly, both are the work and gift of God. That Themistocles might be strong in counsel, and that the Greeks might not spurn his counsels, both were happening with God governing their minds. That Alexander might be strong in virtue, and the army might willingly follow such a young man, was happening divinely.

These things do not happen to all governors. Antiochus lacked everything. Although he was cunning, yet without justice and modesty, a crafty or sycophantic cunning is equipped for destruction, and is in hatred, like the cunning of Sinon. And for the most part in great affairs it is unfortunate, because God hates the confidence of human wisdom, As it is written, Do not lean on your own wisdom.

Then the reputation of lusts greatly deforms all men, both the highest and the lowest, and it is truly considered that the heavenly divinity is not lightly offended, and that debaucheries, rapes, and similar crimes are punished by public disasters. Therefore, Julian had these verses of Bacchylides in his mouth, concerning the praise of modesty. As, unless the face is expressed in living color,

A trunk has no beauty in a painted body, Thus the character of a man, if despised, will remain without honor,

Unless they are adorned by the praise of modesty.

Moreover, to the other vices of Antiochus, which were atrocious, were added obscene lusts. Wherefore, since he was adorned neither by virtue nor by happiness, authority or majesty was lacking to him, nor was he judged worthy of royal honor, because many nobles of Syria had conspired against him, in order to hand over the kingdom to the son of Seleucus, with Antiochus excluded on account of his very bad character. But the crafty man also had his own faction, which he attached to himself by his own artifices and largesses. This is the meaning of the words in the text.

And the arms shall overflow before him, and they shall be broken, and also the leader of the covenant, that is, many Syrian princes will oppose Antiochus, and the leaders of the Egyptian armies, who the cities in they held Phoenicia. And the leader of the covenant himself, that is, Ptolemy Philometor, born of the sister of Antiochus Epiphanes, who had sent auxiliaries against him. These, he says, that is, the Egyptian and Syrian leaders, Antiochus will defeat. Then three expeditions are described in order, the first undertaken into Phoenicia, the two remaining into Egypt.

And he will deceitfully make a covenant with him, he will ascend and lead a small army, and he will come into an opulent province, which he will plunder, that is, although he had already made a covenant with his sister's son, nevertheless he invaded Phoenicia, and as he was rapacious by nature, and on account of his extravagances could not spare the property of others, he plundered the wealth of the rich everywhere, he carried off the treasures of the temples, he held all things for sale, he granted his army license to pillage and ravage, which was not at all consistent with military discipline. Such was his expedition, when he had come into the most opulent city of Tyre. The events carried out in Egypt follow.

And his strength will be stirred up against the king of the South, with a great army, that is, Antiochus Epiphanes having taken possession of Phoenicia, and elated by success, afterwards invaded Egypt. And having driven out the garrisons at Pelusium, when he knew of the greatest cities in that the assault would be difficult and long, he began to use his arts. He corrupted the powerful with bribes, and feigned that he would be the guardian of the young king. Under this pretext, Syrian garrisons were introduced into the Egyptian cities.

And eating bread together, they shall crush him, that is, the Egyptian nobles corrupted by Antiochus will deceive their king and will not defend him.

And the heart of the two kings shall be to do evil, and they shall speak lies at one table, and the counsels shall not succeed, that is, when Antiochus has crossed into Egypt with this simulation of friendship, and the youth, abandoned by his own, receives his uncle with hospitality, they will meet as friends, and yet each will perfidiously plot traps for the other, as it is established that the sincere friendships of princes are most rare.

And he shall return into his land with great riches, and his heart shall be against the holy covenant, that is, when Antiochus had introduced garrisons into the principal cities of Egypt, and had made plunderings in his own manner, he returned to Syria with good hope.

On this journey he first came to Jerusalem, received by Jason. And although he does not yet place an idol in the temple, nevertheless he carries away the treasures, And he plundered many ornaments, and after a great slaughter of the citizens, he seized the wealth of the rich. These things are both recited above, and are narrated in the first book of Maccabees. There follows the second journey, which was much more deadly.

At the appointed time he will return to the South, and the latter expedition will not be like the former. And ships from Kittim will come against him, and he will be grieved, and will return, that is, after two years, when Philometor, having discovered the treachery of his uncle, had expelled the Syrian garrisons, and was recovering his cities in Egypt, and was seeking aid from the Romans, Antiochus openly prepares for war, and again leads an army to the borders of Egypt.

But this expedition was not like the former, that is, the attempts of Antiochus were vain and empty, because Antiochus was compelled by the edict of the Roman legate Popilius to depart with his business unfinished. Therefore he says, Ships from Kittim will come against him, that is, the Roman fleet will come, bringing the legate Popilius, who will promise aid to the Egyptian, and will force Antiochus to do what is commanded. Nor did Popilius obtain without great contention that Antiochus should not wage war against Egypt.

And then I judge that celebrated agreement that it was made with Antiochus, concerning half of the ships, when the Roman legate ordered all the ships that had been brought across to be cut up in one place, and assigned half of the heap of cut-up timbers to Antiochus, and half to the Romans. Thus the Roman man played with the crafty Antiochus, so as to signify to him that the artifices were understood, which he himself had used against the Egyptians and the Romans.

Latin:

DE ANTIOCHO EPIPHANE.

ET stabit loco eius despectus, & non dabitur ei honos regni, veniet placide, & potietur regno per blandicias.

Hîc inchoatur descriptio Antiochi Epiphanis, quem vocat despectum. Singulares emphases sunt in prophetis, quae non de vulgaribus aut parvis rebus loquuntur. Miretur enim aliquis, cur vocet despectum Antiochum, cum fuerit legitimus haeres, ingeniosus, postea etiam multa bella gesserit. Sed iudico despectum dici, quia divinitus non fuit autoritate ornatus. Singulare dei donum est in principe, autoritas seu maiestas, quae tribus rebus constat, virtute, felicitate, & inclinatione animorum in populo.

Ut in Scipione quanquam adolescente, tamen erat autoritas, seu maiestas. Primum lucebat in eo eximia virtus, & adparebat eum iuvari divinitus. Incenduntur autem animi hominum admiratione virtutis, & opinione praesentiae dei, seu auxilii divini. Haec quanquam multum conducunt ad autoritatem parandam & tuendam, tamen et tertium munus dei accedere oportet, videlicet, inclinationes animorum divinitus factas in ipso populo.

Huc pertinet illa memorabilis sententia Solomonis, Ut oculus videat, & auris audiat, deus facit utrumque, id est, ut valeat gubernator consilio & virtute, deinde ut populus obtemperet, non excutiat recta monentes, utrumque dei opus & donum est. Ut valeret consilio Themistocles, & ne eius consilia aspernarentur Graeci, utrumque fiebat deo gubernante animos. Ut Alexander virtute valeret, & exercitus libenter talem ivuenem sequeretur, divinitus fiebat.

Haec non omnibus gubernatoribus contingunt. Antiocho defuerunt omnia. Etsi erat astutus, tamen sine iustitia & modestia, instructa est ad perniciem veteratoria seu sycophantica astutia, & in odio est, ut Sinonis astutia. Et plerumque in magnis negociis est infortunata, quia deus odit confidentiam humanae sapientiae, Sicut scriptum est, Ne innitaris sapientiae tuae.

Deinde omnes homines summos & infimos magnopere deformat fama libidinum, & verè existimatur, non leviter offendi numen caeleste, & publicis cladibus puniri stupra, raptus, & similia scelera. Ideo Iulianus hos versiculos Bacchylidis in ore habuit, de laude pudicitiae. Ut, nisi sit vivo facies expressa colore,

Non decus in picto corpore truncus habet, Sic hominis mores spreti sine honore manebunt,

Ni sint ornati laude pudicitiae.

Accedebant autem ad caetera vitia Antiochi, quae erant atrocia, obscoenae libidines. Quare cum nec virtute, nec felicitate ornatus esset, deerat ei autoritas seu maiestas, nec iudicabatur regio honore dignus, quia multi proceres Syriae adversus eum conspiraverant, ut regnum traderent Seleuci filio, excluso Antiocho propter pessimos mores. Sed habuit & suam factionem homo vafer, quam sibi suis artificiis & largitionibus adiunxit. Haec est sententia verborum in textu.

Et brachia inundabunt coram eo, & conterentur & ipse dux foederis, id est, multi Syriaci principes adversabuntur Antiocho, & duces exercituum Aegyptiacorum, qui urbes in phoenice tenebant. Et ipse dux foederis, id est, Ptolemaeus Philometor, natus ex sorore Alltiochi Epiphanis, qui contra cum auxilia miserat. Hos, inquit, id est Aegyptios & Syriacos duces, vincet Antiochus. Deine tres expeditiones ordine describuntur, prima suscepta in Phoenicen, duae reliquae in Aegyptum.

Et dolosè foedus faciet cum illo, ascendet & ducet exercitum exiguum, & in opulentam provinciam veniet, quam spoliabit, id est, etsi cum sororis filio iam foedus fecerat, tamen Phoenicen invasit, & ut natura rapax erat, ac propter profusiones alieno parcere non poterat, diriput passim facultates locupletum, sustulit templorum thesauros, omnia venalia habuit, exercitui grassandi & populandi licentiam concessit, minime convenientem disciplinae militari. Talis fuit eius expeditio, cum in urben opulentissimam Tyrum venisset. Sequuntur res in Aegypto gestae.

Et excitabitur fortitudo eius contra regem Austri, cum exercitu magno, id est, Antiochus Epiphanes potitus Phoenice, & successu elatus, postea Aegyptum invasit. Et pulsis praesidiis ad Pelusium, cum sciret maximarum urbium in aditu difficilem, & longam fore oppugnationem, coepit suis artibus uti. Corrupit potentes largitionibus, & simulavit se tutorem fore regis adolescentis. Hoc praetextu Syriaca praesidia in urbes Aegyptias introducuntur.

Et comedentes unà panem, conterent eum, id est, proceres Aegyptii corrupti ab Antiocho, suum regem fallent, & non defendent.

Et duorum regum cor erit ut malefaciant, & ad unam mensam mendacium loquentur, & non succedent consilia, id est, Cum hac simulatione amicitiae transierit Antiochus in Aegyptum, & adolescens à suis destitutus excipiet hospitio avunculum, convenient tanquam amici, & tamenu perfidiosè uterque struet insidias alteri, ut constat rarissimas esse principum sinceras amicitias.

Et revertetur in terram suam, cum magnis opibus, & cor eius contra foedus sanctuarii, id est, Cum praesidia in urbes Aegypti praecipuas introduxisset Antiochus, et suo more direptiones fecisset, bona cum spe rediit in Syriam.

In hoc itinere primùm venit Ierosolymam exceptus à Iasone. Et quanquam nondum in templo idolum collocat, tamen dufert thesauros, & multa ornamenta, & facta magna civium caede, locupletum facultates diripuit. Haec & suprà recitata sunt, & narrantur in primo libro Machabaeorum. Sequitur alterum iter, quod fuit multo funestius.

Certo tempore revertetur in Austrum, & non erit posterior expeditio priori similis. Et venient contra eum naves ex Cithim, & dolebit, & revertetur, id est, post biennium, cum Philometor deprehensa fraude avunculi, expulisset Syriaca praesidia, & urbes suas in Aegypto reciperet, & à Romanis auxilia peteret, Antiochus palàm molitur bellum, ac rursus exercitum ad Acgypti fines adducit.

Sed haec expeditio non fuit similis priori, id est, irriti & inanes conatus Antiochi fuerunt, quia Antiochus edicto legati Romani Popilii coactus est, infectis rebus, discedere. Ideo inquit, Venient contra eum naves ex Cithim, id est, Romana classis veniet, legatum adducens Popilium, qui auxilia Aegyptio promittet, & coget Antiochum imperata facere. Nec sine magna contentione obtinuit Popilius, ne Antiochus bellum Aegypto inferret.

Ac tunc arbitror illam celebratam pactionem cum Antiocho factam esse, de dimidio navium, cum legatus Romanus traductas naves omnes in unum locum dissecari iussit, & dissectarum trabium acervum dimidium attribuit Antiocho, dimidium Romanis. Ita Romanus homo cum veteratore Antiocho lusit, ut ei significaret, intelligi artificia, quibus ipse adversus Aegyptios & Romanos usus esset.

## 11:1 On the Term Cithim
ON THE TERM Cithim.

ON THE term Cithim it has been spoken above. Although, however, the Jews here trifle in their own custom, nevertheless it is sufficiently established from many testimonies of the sacred books, what the force of the term is. The prophets are accustomed to distinguish regions and nations by the appellations of the stocks or first parents. As when they understand the Greeks or the Ionian nation, it is named Ion or Javan, the father of the Ionian nation.

Ion or Javan had, however, sons, from whom the Greek nations arose and were named. Elishah, from whom it appears the Aeolians were named. Dodanim from whom are the Dodonaeans, the ancient Pelasgians in Europe. Cithim from whom there was a nation neighboring to the Dodonaeans. For Isaiah adds to Alexander an army of the nations of Dodanim and Cithim, that is, Greek and

The Macedonian, and the old name μακέτης, which antiquity used for the word Macedonian, was undoubtedly made from Cithim, so that it is μακέτης, sprung from Cithim. For the force of the letter placed before is known to those who are in any way skilled in the Hebrew language. And that the Ceteans were in places neighboring Macedonia is manifest from Homer, who says that the Ceteans obeyed Telephus, king of the Mysians, and his son Eurypylus, who was killed at Troy by Pyrrhus. Now Mysia was in Europe, which today is a part of Servia, neighboring Macedonia. The verses of Homer's Odyssey 11. are these.

ἀλλʼ οἷον τε τηλεφίδης κατενήρατο χαλκῶ̣. ἥρὠ εὐρύπυλος, πολλοὶ δʼἀμφ'̓ αὐτ' ἑταῖροι κήτειοι κτείνοντο, γυναίων εἵνεκα δώρων..

Neoptolemus himself pierced with a sharp weapon, the great-hearted Eurypylus, whom Telephus once begat,

And he laid low the Ceteans beside him in arms, whom they said were drawn by a promised marriage.

Therefore both the proximity of the Ceteans, and the affinity of the word indicate that the Macedonians were sprung from Cithim. Wherefore the book of the Maccabees calls King Alexander Cithim, and that the Ausonians and Latins were sprung from the Greek nations, there is no doubt. Indeed Suidas writes that Latinus was the son of Telephus, and that he led the Cetei into Italy, and gave his name to Latium.

Since therefore Cithim signifies the nations in Europe, sprung from Cithim, it embraces both the Macedonians and the Italians. Wherefore also in Numbers ch. 24. Balaam prophesies that fleets are to come from Cithim, which shall destroy the Assyrians and the Hebrews. Here he understands the kingdoms in Europe, and embraces Alexander and the Romans. For Alexander did not destroy the Hebrews.

Let us know therefore that Cithim are the nations in Europe, sprung from Iavan's son Cithim, both in the places neighboring Greece, and in Italy. Thence from the European empire he says a fleet shall come, which shall repress Antiochus. If Suidas's annotation concerning Latinus the son of Telephus is true, the name Cithim fits Latium most aptly, since Latinus led the Cetei into that shore of Italy. Furthermore, it is certain that colonies were led into Italy by the Greeks in the beginning.

And he shall think thoughts with those who forsake the law of the holy covenant. And arms from him shall stand, and they shall profane the temple, and shall take away the continual sacrifice, and he shall place the abomination of desolation, and he shall deceive the transgressors of the covenant with flatteries.

That is, After Antiochus Epiphanes was deterred by the Roman legate from waging war against Egypt, he returned to Syria raging and angry. Furthermore, in this second return he exercised much greater cruelty and impiety than before, because he now placed the idol of Olympian Jupiter in the temple, prohibited circumcision by edict, burned the sacred books, saw to it that the Bacchanalia were celebrated in Jerusalem, killed the pious men who did not wish to worship idols, and left a garrison for the defense of the idols and for the hindering of true worships. Finally, the whole history described in the books of the Maccabees shows how great the dissipation of the entire Jewish polity and true religion was.

NOR is it difficult to think of the causes why God predicted these disasters. The causes of these punishments are shown, so that the pious, being warned, might be more cautious and firmer. For it is clearly predicted that the beginnings of these evils arise from the Jews themselves, as the text says, He will devise plans with the transgressors. The high priests themselves summon Antiochus, they agree that the idol should be placed in the temple, they assist the cruelty, and finally they are partners in such a great crime. And so God reminds the pious, that the society let them flee these crimes. It signifies also from where the calamities of the church usually arise, namely, from the very governors of the church.

Then the pious were greatly strengthened by this consolation, because they foresaw the outcome and end of these tumults. Although they also knew that the cruelty of the impious would rage for some years, yet they knew for certain that they were not fighting in vain, but would at length recover their country, temple, and liberty. This hope strengthened brave men, so that they did not hesitate to take up arms. Therefore both the time is predicted, and the events correspond. Within three years Maccabaeus recovered the temple. This victory added even more spirit to the pious, so that they might press the war.

Lastly, the prediction confirms the pious against scandal, that is, against the clamors of the impious, who shout that one must obey the ordinary power, and must think with the greater part. Thus there were undoubtedly many then, who used this excuse to cloak their own impiety and cowardice, that it is the part of a good citizen to obey the magistrates and high priests, and not to dissent from the greater part of the citizens. That all separations from the ordinary power, or from the common consent of the greater part, are dishonest and seditious.

Against these plausible speeches God consoles the pious, and condemns the greater part falling away from the law, and affirms that those few who are about to defend the divine law are pleasing to Him. We ourselves now have need of this very consolation, in the reproof of the idols which the pontiffs defend.

It is customary for men to fight over wealth and honors. Most bishops, canons, monks, and their friends fight for these in reality, when they attempt to protect their rites and their idols, and they use the name of the church as a pretext, and attribute that to their own assembly, which dominates. Then they contend that one must not depart from the ordinary authority, which even if it sometimes has some slight errors, yet has never received impious opinions, or idols. It is of a good mind to take in good part certain inconveniences of government in such great infirmity of the human race, and to soften them with a moderate interpretation.

How many times now in these twenty years have I heard these sermons? How many times has that voice been cast at me, κακὸν εὖ κέιμενον μὴ κινητέον, and that just as the delusions of parents, so certain errors of the church must be tolerated and overlooked, lest by doctoring them more evil be stirred up. For when errors are moved, discord is inflamed, great ruins follow, Great lacerations, which beget many errors and greater evils, as Homer truly says concerning Strife.

Small at first through fear, soon it raises itself into the breezes, and walks upon the ground, and hides its head among the clouds.

Finally, the books of this philosophy are full concerning this moderation, which forbids moving and disturbing the forms of governments, even if they have some not light vices.

These are those blandishments, concerning which he says in the text, that many were invited by blandishments to assent to the king and the pontiff. But there are certain limits to this philosophy. Let us bear and tolerate civil burdens, civil servitude by art, let us forgive governments their manifold negligence in judgments, in protecting the advantages and bodies of the people.

But let us prefer the glory of God and of his son Jesus Christ to all human things, to governments, to public concord, to our honors, to our life. As Christ said, He who will deny me before men, I also will deny him before my heavenly father. And the apostles command us to prefer the commandment of God to the concord of their republic, when they say, It is necessary to obey God rather than men.

And Paul says, If anyone shall teach another Gospel, let him be anathema, that is, depart from the defenders of impious worship, as from those condemned by God.

To these sayings, let us add the judgment of God here proposed in the prophecy, which approves the smaller part dissenting from the king and from the pontiff, and condemns the princes defending impious worship. Nor are the errors of the pontifical party, which we tax, light. For although certain crafty men seek reconciliations of religions, and mix all, ethnic, Jewish, and Evangelical, yet these deceptions are by no means to be admitted into the church, in which light ought to shine, which the eternal God has handed down to us through the voice of the prophets, Christ, and the apostles.

How great the darkness was, the frivolous questions in the books of sentences still show, whether God can command that he be held in hatred? What does a mouse eat, gnawing consecrated bread? But these say, these were the ineptitudes of a few and idle men, nor for that reason should the church, that is, the assembly of bishops and of all prudent governors, be accused. Nay, we rebuke the sinews of idol-manias, which the highest ones especially defend, which are not light errors, nor can they be mitigated by painted excuses.

The true invocation of God has been overwhelmed in two ways: First, because they defend the invocation of the dead, or rather of statues, such as Mary, James, and similar examples, which differ in no way from ethnic rites, from the invocation of Hercules, of the Tyndaridae, of Romulus.

Then they command one to invoke God with doubt concerning reconciliation. This opinion is also entirely ethnic. But this error concerning doubt does not strike the eyes, nor can it be judged, except by the pious. The mind ought to invoke in this way, joining itself to the high priest Christ.

Almighty, eternal, and living God, Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, creator and preserver of all things, wise, good, merciful, judge, and strong, on account of Jesus Christ your Son, whom you willed to be a victim for us by a wonderful and ineffable counsel, have mercy on me, and by your Holy Spirit govern and sanctify me.

And on account of the high priest Christ we ought to determine that we are truly received and heard.

The contrary opinion, which the adversaries defend, is ethnic, as the Homeric priest prays, Phoebe, if my sacrifices have pleased you, help me.

What shall I say about the mass? Let them invent illusions as they wish, yet the ancient and purer church cries out against it, and by manifest testimonies refutes the manifold profanation with which they pollute the Lord's supper. And since it is most manifest that no ceremonies outside the instituted use retain the nature of a divine sacrament, why do they command the bread to be adored, and indeed entirely outside the nature of the sacrament? This is no light evil.

Then the law concerning celibacy is an infinite ruin of souls, nor does the length of time excuse these evils. The church in this world is for the most part in sad and harsh servitude. But yet there have always been some pious and prudent men who lamented these errors and desired an amendment. But later it will be necessary to speak of the struggles of these times. Now the history of Antiochus must be woven through.

He calls the leaders and garrisons which Antiochus left in Judea arms, so that the pious might be killed.

The designation of the temple is significant, when he says, They shall profane the sanctuary of strength, that is, of the temple. He calls the temple strength or a citadel, because on account of this temple, the people were defended by God. This temple, this worship of God and doctrine was the citadel and fortification of this people. Just as for us the Gospel is a citadel, that is, we hope that the church will not perish entirely, and it will remain there, where the voice of the Gospel sounds. Neither the Turks nor any tyrants will be able to destroy this.

There was, however, a single temple for the Jewish people, just as there was a single doctrine and a single worship. Therefore this temple is called in the singular number, the strength of Maoz. But below he will call the idol the god Maozim, that is, of dissimilar temples.

At Rome in the Capitol Jupiter was worshipped, in the Attic temple, Pallas. In the Eleusinian, Ceres. In the Argive temple, Juno. In the Delphic, Phoebus. In the Theban, Bacchus. In the Ephesian, Diana. In the Cyprian, Venus. In the Lampsacene, Priapus. In the Egyptian, the Ox. Finally, opinions and worships were dissimilar, idols were dissimilar, in the whole world.

Nor did men know how God wishes to be invoked, and to hear. Therefore those were dissimilar temples, not dedicated to one eternal God, but to many empty titles, which men ignorant of God, either in perpetual doubt, or in impious confidence they invoked with confidence, when there was no reality underlying those little letters.

He therefore calls them the gods of Maozim, that is, the fictitious gods of dissimilar temples, and of opinions straying from the one doctrine of the divine voice. But concerning this word, it will have to be spoken of again below.

The rest which the text adds are matters known from the history described in the book of Maccabees. Pious sacrifices were hindered by arms, and an idol of Jupiter Olympius was placed in the temple, which he calls the abomination of desolation. For the Hebrews customarily call all idols abominations or βδελύγματα.

Moreover, the epithet is added, the desolating idol, that is, one destroying true doctrine and true worship, for as long as it was to remain. For here he does not speak of the perpetual destruction of the people, as above in cap. 9. where many words are added which expressly predict the ruin of the entire polity after Christ has been exhibited. But here the angel signifies that he is speaking of the calamity of this people, which is to be before the coming of Christ. Wherefore he also promises liberation to the people. And he expressly adds that another time remains, namely, that this the polity, until the Messiah shall have been exhibited.

Understand therefore in this place the idol, devastating the doctrine and worship of God, for so long until, with the garrisons of Antiochus driven out and the temple recovered, the idol is removed. Moreover, the idol was placed in the temple in the year 145 after the death of Alexander, on the 15th day of November. Then indeed, after three years, the temple having been recovered, the idol was again removed and burned, in the year 148 after the death of Alexander, on the 25th day of November, on which day afterwards the Dedication was celebrated, of which mention is made in John.

And the people knowing their God shall be strengthened and shall act, and the learned among the people shall teach many, and they shall fall by the sword and flame, etc.

This is the promise of liberation, in which the angel testifies that there will be some knowing their God who will be opposing the fury of Antiochus. And although many in this very number will be killed and will be afflicted with cruel punishments, yet at length, with the enemies driven out, they will recover their country and liberty.

But it is especially to be observed in this place what God judges concerning each party, which He approves, which He condemns, the High Priests and princes with The greater part of the people will embrace ethnic rites, having cast off the law of Moses. Although this part is greater, and has with it the ordinary power, as they call it, that is, the pontiffs, nevertheless it is condemned above, when it names prevaricators.

The lesser part was that which had not defected from its God, and specifically its own, he says, that is, who revealed himself in this very word, which he delivered to his people. He approves of this lesser part. Therefore, let not the pious be deterred from the profession of true doctrine, when adversaries oppose the judgments of pontiffs and the greater part, as we are now chiefly attacked by this argument.

You shall remember also this, that he says, The learned shall teach many. He signifies that in that very struggle it will come to pass that doctrine is purged and more illuminated. As now in the most bitter disputes of these times, by the benefit of God, much light has been added to the doctrine of the Gospel, which before was buried by the filth of human traditions and many frivolous and impious questions of the Sententiaries, finally when the church was thought to be a pontifical kingdom, the true church was not recognized. Now battles have shown the distinctions.

But many shall fall, he says, by the sword, and flame.

Nor are examples lacking in our own age. I remember a most excellent man, endowed with outstanding talent and learning, Henry of Zutphen, whom the ministers of the Bishop of Bremen caused to be cruelly killed, because he had taught the Gospel purely in the church of Bremen, when he had indeed performed his duty most modestly. And I could recount the histories of many others.

And when they shall fall, they shall be helped with a small help, And many shall join themselves to them deceitfully. He declares the manner of liberation, and as it were sounds the trumpet to those who repressed Antiochus. For these words moved the pious to take up arms. They shall be helped with a small help, that is, the pious party will be without ordinary leaders, nor will it have great armies, but there will be small forces of pious and brave men, who without ordinary command will run together of their own accord for the common defense. Just as the text also says in the book of Maccabees; naming this assembly, volunteers, that is, those who of their own accord, without ordinary leaders, ran together.

After the return from Babylon, up to this war, leaders from the lineage of David presided over the people without a royal title, but nevertheless with moderate authority, Although in the end the high priests began to excel in power and pomp. And the names of those leaders exist in the genealogy of Christ, and a little before this war there was John the leader, who was besieged by Antiochus Epiphanes in the castle of Jericho, and was killed there. Afterwards the people were without a leader, and the high priests contended among themselves with ambition and every kind of wickedness.

The true church, therefore, being destitute of ordinary governors, gathers at the exhortation of Mattathias the priest, whom the pious revered and followed on account of his piety, not on account of power or wealth, just as now the true church is helped by private efforts, while kings, princes, and bishops attack it. However, the example is to be observed, so that it may be opposed to the clamors about ordinary power and about the majority.

There is a useful admonition also in the other part, Many shall join themselves to them deceitfully. Alcimus the high priest pretended that he was a friend to the army of the pious, and by that simulation he perfidiously oppressed the deceived citizens, and killed many. Nor is there any doubt that there were frequent changes of wills and defections, as happens in civil discords, in which fickle and evil men follow fortune are, or indulge private hatreds.

In this age of ours we see the defections of many powerful men, likewise of others who have taught publicly in our churches, Witzel, Campanus. And there still seem to be those in our own camps who feign benevolence toward us, so that they may bend our people to receive errors, and to restore idols insulting to God.

Then many in all civil discords on account of αναρχίαν take for themselves a license which would be granted to no one in a tranquil republic and established discipline. Wherefore also the bad morals of many harm us, who deform a good cause with scandals. These domestic wounds greatly hinder both good things and the pursuits of good men.

Nor, however, is the defense of the Gospel to be cast aside, and let the true church acknowledge its miseries, and the snares of the devil, who, in order to scatter the church and to afflict God and his son Jesus Christ with insults, attacks the church from every side, incites Epicurean men and hypocrites against it, abroad, and in the very camps of the church. The church of the first family felt this calamity in the beginning, when Cain killed his brother.

The following words repeat the description of the calamity and they exaggerate it, but yet they add again a consolation concerning liberation. The learned shall fall, and shall be tested, but even to the time, because another time remains, that is, Although the atheists will think that this people is now going to perish utterly, yet let the pious know that they will struggle through, and with this hope they will contend with the enemies. For the time of the coming of the Messiah remains, whom it was necessary to appear while this polity was still enduring.

THE FOLLOWING verses many, having omitted the history of Antiochus entirely, understand concerning the Antichrist alone. But although there is no doubt that this prophecy embraces the calamities of the church which it bears in these last times of the world after the Apostles, and a little before the resurrection of the dead, yet I will not tear this part away from the history of Antiochus. For in the end he speaks concerning Edom, and the neighbors. These things seem to be historical. Wherefore in the following exposition we will first accommodate the words to Antiochus, afterwards also to the last empires of the world, which the name of Antichrist embraces. For the history of Antiochus itself also is an image and a prophecy concerning the Antichrist.

I said however above, that the prophets and apostles prophesy concerning two kingdoms that will oppress the church of God. The other is the Mahometan, which openly rejects the doctrine of the prophets and apostles, then openly attempts to erase the name of the son of God. And although it retains a portion of the law, concerning civil morals, like other nations, yet it utterly rejects the gospel concerning the son of God. It denies that he is the son of God, born of the substance of the eternal father, it denies that he is a victim for the human race. It removes everything that the Gospel teaches concerning the remission of sins and concerning faith receiving reconciliation on account of a mediator, it only plays with ethnic imaginations concerning God.

But that pious minds may be fortified against these furies, let them consider these arguments, which are most certain.

It is impossible for a doctrine to be true which rejects the prophets and apostles, or which does not retain the word of God, handed down through the prophets and apostles. But it is certain that the writings of the prophets and apostles are not heard at all in the whole sect of Mahomet. And it is certain that the Mahometan opinion was born recently a long time after the apostles, and that it fights against the prophets and apostles. Wherefore it is manifest that it is a fiction of the devil, condemned by God.

All religions are to be execrated and fled from, which are ignorant of the Son of God, and of the promises concerning Him handed down through the prophets and apostles. For God wishes to be acknowledged in such a way, as He has revealed Himself by His own voice. Furthermore, the human mind cannot affirm that God is appeased, or that He hears those calling upon Him propitiously, unless the promises concerning the Son of God are known. Moreover, a mind doubting concerning appeasement flees from God, and does not expect help from Him.

The heathens raged, feigning that there were many gods, and attributing different gifts to different ones: to Jupiter the governance of storms, to Mars wars, to Venus procreation, to Ceres the guardianship of crops, to Bacchus the protection of vines, and finally to others other things.

These ravings of the common people were not approved of even then by the wise, but they acknowledged that there is one eternal mind, the cause of good in nature, as Plato most learnedly said. And these did not blame the customary cults for this reason, because they thought that by different statues and names various powers of one immense nature were signified, because that immensity could not be shown by one name and one sign, which was called Pallas, insofar as it was the cause of order in nature and of intelligence in the human mind, Jupiter, Ceres, or Bacchus, insofar as he might make the earth fruitful every year by a wonderful law.

These things, although the ancients argued thus, acknowledging that there is one eternal mind, the creator and preserver of this whole mass of things, nevertheless they did not yet rightly acknowledge God. For they did not know the promises concerning propitiation and concerning the mediator handed down to the fathers.

Although therefore the Mahometans greatly exalt and boast of their religion, because they retain the article concerning the unity of God, because they do not adore idols, nor do they invent many gods, nevertheless this wisdom of theirs is nothing, except a certain common Philosophy of human reason, in which the doctrine concerning the propitiation of God does not yet shine.

I know that many, fascinated by human persuasions, detest the quarrels of religions. To what end, they say, is there need for such great dissensions to be stirred up, when religions agree in substance, even if there are and have been differences in appellations and signs?

Against these darts of the devil minds must be fortified, and it must be considered how God wishes to be acknowledged and invoked, likewise how he wished to be propitiated. Plato knew that there is one eternal the mind as the cause of good in nature, whether that mind were called Jupiter or Pallas. Mahomet teaches just as much. But the same Plato knew that God is a just mind, angry at baseness. Wherefore, thinking about his own vices, he did not judge that he would be forgiven, nor did he call upon God, but rather fled from God.

It is necessary, therefore, that the article concerning propitiation also be held. And how much this matters, only the church of God understands clearly, which acknowledges sin, and the wrath of God, and in turn acknowledges that it is received on account of the mediator. It knows also that this article was delivered immediately from the beginning to the first fathers in the promise, it understands that by this confidence the hearts of the fathers were confirmed, and that they performed true worship and true invocation.

Hypocrites think that they call upon God, but in true terror all this invocation is shaken out of them. But only the church of God understands and performs true invocation. These things concerning the mediator must be diligently considered, so that against the πιθανολογίας, of Mahomet, minds may be confirmed, and all religions may be execrated which are ignorant of the propitiation which was made through the Son of God. For they both overwhelm the wrath of God against sin, and the mercy promised on account of the Son, and they destroy the highest benefit of God.

Therefore John the Baptist, testifying concerning the Son of God, says, He who believes in the Son has eternal life. He who does not believe in the Son of God shall not see life, but the wrath of God remains upon him. If a pious mind considers these things, it will be sufficiently fortified against the furies of Mahomet, who spurns the whole Gospel concerning the Son of God.

Tear it apart, and that part which is properly called the law, because it pretends that nothing is a sin except external deeds against the law. It also removes the true rights of marriage, it grants the right to take and to cast away as many as each one wishes, without any cause and investigation. This is to remove the bond of marriage entirely, and to grant infinite fornication, as we shall say below.

It is evident, however, that for almost nine hundred years the churches of Christ have been horribly both devastated and oppressed by Mahometan tyranny. Wherefore Mahomet is to be included under the name of Antichrist, and is to be execrated and shunned among the enemies of the Son of God.

And above, in the empires concerning the Mahometan kingdom, I said that the divine premonition must be diligently held, which clearly says that this kingdom will speak blasphemies against God and will kill the saints. And since it speaks of the kingdom, when it is placed with the fourth Monarchy, it is most certain that mention is made of the Mahometan tyranny. The pious, however, understand that there are many grave and great reasons why God wished to forewarn the church, which they should weigh for themselves.

BUT what is the other kingdom by which the church of God is oppressed? Paul clearly says that the man of sin will rule in the church, exalting himself above the worship of God. It is certain, however, that in the church and under the title of the church, the pontiffs who defend idols hold sway.

It is the mark of a moderate mind to be moved by reverence for that power which is called ordinary. It is also the mark of a grateful one to consider the right deeds of ancestors. The Gospel was propagated through bishops, the disciples of the apostles. Furthermore, whatever sort of bishops they were, they nevertheless retained the name of Christ and some sort of shadow of the church. And it must be admitted that under their rule there were some who were pious, and some church of God. Why then, you will ask, is there no sparing of the bishops on account of that part? Especially since, in such great infirmity of the human race, there has never been, from the very beginning of the world, nor will there be, any government without vices and errors. Wherefore it is both customary and easy to criticize the vices of a government.

Often the Levitical high priests and the chief associates of the government were tyrannical, ignorant of true doctrine, rapacious, negligent in the administration of justice, or even unjust. Such were Hyrcanus, Annas, Caiaphas. Yet neither Simeon, Zechariah, and similar pious men, either condemned the order, or snatched from them the function of public office, or departed from them.

How many miseries there were in the government of the apostles, who, since they coerced no one with force, had many contumacious, fickle, arrogant disciples, who either spread false dogmas or deformed the Gospel with evil morals.

Finally, it is madness in this custom of weak men, indeed in this old age of the world, with human nature itself now raving, to imagine a sort of Platonic state, any polity without diseases, vices, and miseries.

And the examples of all ages show that, when attempts at amending polities were made, the following state generated new vices. Democracy at Athens had increased the license of the mob. Afterwards, Oligarchy brought forth tyrannies, which again stirred up discords and civil wars.

Since therefore many inconveniences must be pardoned in government, why (they say) do you reprehend the bishops so atrociously? Why do you not obey their laws? Why do you not acknowledge them to be the governors of the church? Nay, why do you join pontiffs to kingdoms openly impious and openly attempting to destroy the name of Christ?

I acknowledge the reprehension to be atrocious. But although many things can be said plausibly about the ancient merits of the order, about modesty and ἐπιεικεια in softening inconveniences and errors, nevertheless an immutable rule must be held, If anyone shall teach another gospel, let him be anathema. There are certain boundaries which warn which inconveniences are to be pardoned, and which indeed are to be taxed and shaken off. Simeon and Zacharias did not approve the opinions of Annas and Caiaphas, nor did they obey all laws and edicts, as Epiphanius narrates, that Zacharias the father of the Baptist was killed, taxing the errors of his college.

I know there are many great miseries, especially in the government of the church, the infirmity of morals in many who preside must be dissimulated and borne. In turn also good and wise governors, as wise fathers, tolerate with a certain probable moderation the great infirmity of the people. Honor and reverence are owed to those who preside, if they perform their office moderately.

But when these two things concur, the defense of idols, and in that defense pertinacity whether Epicurean or Judaic, it is necessary to resist.

I agree that the ancient bishops, the disciples of the apostles, were most deserving, and I venerate their memory, and I give thanks to God because he called these nations to the Gospel through their ministry. We shall also give thanks to them in that eternal fellowship. But the church is very different from other polities, nor is it bound to titles of dignities. Even if these recent bishops have succeeded into the place of the Apostles, yet with the doctrine changed, the function is new and unlike the apostolic one.

Japheth held the priesthood in his family, as he had received it from his father Noah, approved by God. His descendants, who celebrated orgies, succeeded into his place. Although therefore they alleged succession, yet the priesthood was entirely different.

But God with wondrous goodness preserves some assembly, which retains the doctrine of the prophets and of the apostles, nor does it allow the true church to perish entirely, even if it is often very small. But in that assembly it stirs up teachers from time to time, who may again kindle the light of the Gospel, as Ephes. 4 is written. Thus, although the whole priesthood of Jeroboam was condemned, yet God stirred up prophets for the people, who set forth the doctrine concerning true invocation. The hearers of these were the true church, as the text says, to Elijah lamenting the destruction of the church, I have left for myself seven thousand, who do not worship Baal, &c.

We see also that before our age there were learned men who rebuked and lamented public errors, indeed who endured exiles or punishments on account of a pious confession. Such were Wessel of Groningen, whose writings are extant, Tauler, John Hus, Jerome of Prague, and many before them, I number Bernard among these, and that there were many others, there is no doubt. Therefore the church of God remained, even if, as always, it was small. And at times there is more darkness, at times sharper struggles concerning doctrine arise, so that some restoration may take place, and more may be called to the light of the Gospel. There, the assembly which defends errors, and adds blasphemous obstinacy against God, is certainly not the church of God.

By these enemies of the Gospel we understand both Paul and Daniel, when they condemn the kingdom in the church defending idols, namely the bishops, who rule in a kingly manner, and lest their power or authority be shaken, they do not want any errors to be censured, and in order to defend their authority, they exercise cruelty against pious men, who do not approve of the errors. And although they could easily retain the wealth, authority, and form of the ancient ecclesiastical polity, with certain filth cast aside, yet raging with stubbornness and hatred, like Pharaoh or Antiochus, they judge that in no matter should they yield to their inferiors.

Nor is this stubbornness new, but for the most part when changes have been made, there has been a similar stubbornness of the impious, as that of Pharaoh, Saul, Zedekiah, and the Pharisees in the time of Christ. These defenders of idols, therefore, these blasphemers, stubborn men, and parricides we censure. If there are any bishops who by their judgment and will shrink from that defense of errors, if these declare their judgment by a pious confession, and take care that their churches are rightly taught, they are not members of the impious kingdom. And there are many citizens under the impious tyrants themselves, thinking rightly, rightly calling upon God, and his son We do not understand these to be condemned by Jesus Christ, but rather to be members of the true church, with which they are joined in judgment and will. Nor do we imagine an invisible church, or as a Platonic idea.

As God wills the confession of the Gospel, the use of the sacraments, and true invocation to be seen openly, so it is necessary that the church be a visible assembly. But it is unlike other polities; the pious are scattered everywhere, and they are not included in a certain worldly kingdom, or a certain place, or certain human ceremonies, or a certain region, but everywhere all who embrace the Gospel of God and avoid idols are members of the one church of God.

Therefore we do not wage war with the pious who live under tyrants, nor with those who separate themselves by judgment, will, and confession from the society of the enemies of the Gospel, but with those bishops and their henchmen who defend idols and declare their obstinacy by manifest examples and great cruelty.

We exhort, however, whomever we can for the sake of the glory of God, that they separate themselves by judgment and will from that impious faction. As Maccabeus exhorted many, that they should separate themselves from the society of the counsels They might separate the Antiochians. We also warn the learned and prudent, who either out of a desire for peace, as they profess, or out of a certain moroseness of their own, wish to appear to abhor new things and changes, that they should take care lest by their delay they strengthen the enemies of the Gospel. The matters are manifest, and can be easily judged by candid and non-calumnious persons.

Exaggerate, extol, amplify, Wicel, as much as you wish, the consensus of many centuries, the ordinary succession, the reverence owed to governors, yet if you open your innermost feelings and the recesses of your heart, you will say that you are displeased by the invocation of the dead, the profanation of the Lord's Supper, wandering lusts, the cruelty which the pontiffs have been exercising for so long, the neglect of sacred studies, the depravation of the doctrine concerning penance and grace, the superstitions of human rites, the royal domination of bishops which hinders the government of the church, the omission of synods and of the judgments of the church concerning doctrine and morals. Even if Epicurean wise men laugh at these things, yet honest minds, which judge that the Gospel is not fictitious, acknowledge that they must not be dissimulated.

BUT you stir up more evil by applying remedies, they say. You make ἀναρχίαν the authority of the highest ones weakened, you loosen the reins of the petulance and audacity of minds, the curious are moved by example and occasion to disturb true dogmas. We have seen Anabaptists, Servetuses, Campanuses, and other plagues arise. And just as when the heads of the hydra were cut off others were born, so with those impostors repressed in one place or another, similar ones arise elsewhere.

What do the powerful take upon themselves? By what discipline do they allow their morals to be ruled? How diligent are they in fostering pious studies? To where do they transfer the wealth of the churches? What of the fact that the distraction of princes is in itself an infinite evil? We see that they cannot conduct either great or small business with a common mind. Finally, if now the beginnings of dissensions have so much evil, what will happen to posterity, when there will be less erudition, and the audacity of the powerful will already be confirmed by length of time? Now in princes there is some zeal for pious doctrine, such will not be in posterity. Barbarism and inhumanity will therefore grow.

These things are objected to us when we tax errors, and these sad things are a great grief to us they bring spectacles. Civil discords beget infinite evils. But it is unjust to attribute the cause of discords to the Gospel. Idols and false opinions ought to have yielded to the divine voice. When these are defended, the obstinacy of those who oppose the Gospel inflames discords. And these are the customary punishments of kingdoms. I judge that there was no more beautiful kingdom afterwards than the kingdom of Egypt established by Joseph. Moses certainly grieved that so beautiful a polity was utterly overturned. Much more did Jeremiah grieve for the destruction of his own polity. Consider the times of Antiochus, about which the angel preaches here. You see the very image of the calamities of this time in the history of the Maccabees. The number of the pious was small. To these were joined many fickle, perfidious, and treacherous men; the princes were at variance. The principal leader dies before the end of the war, but nevertheless the better part conquered. What new confusions of the Jewish polity followed this victory?

Certainly Mattathias had not taken up arms so that his grandson Hyrcanus might foster the Sadducees, who openly spread Epicurean dogmas, not so that his great-grandsons might establish a kingdom through parricides and rage cruelly against the citizens, not so that his great-great-grandsons, Just as the Theban brothers, they would fight among themselves with impious arms, disturb religion, and lead the Romans to profane the temple. For it is certain that after the war of Antiochus Epiphanes, Judea was gravely afflicted by the domestic tyranny of the posterity of the Maccabees.

Thus new storms always overwhelm the church. Although therefore the state of our churches now seems mediocre, in which indeed there is no small amount of hardships, yet some time later some heat, or times will change the wills of princes and teachers, or will tear them apart, and there will be a harsher servitude, and the darkness of doctrines will follow, and perhaps other more serious changes of human affairs will also approach.

THEREFORE they say, if there is no hope of a better state, it were better to be silent. For to what purpose does it pertain, when things are stirred up, to accumulate miseries? There is one and the same answer. The command is immutable, Flee idolatry. By this voice let all who are not Epicureans understand themselves to be compelled to cast away false opinions, and to dissent from the defenders of idols. And although this assembly is small, as were the few, Mary, Joseph, Simeon, Zechariah, and others, who condemned the errors of the pontiffs of that time, nevertheless it ought to profess the true opinion.

Nor is that profession useless. Thus God from time to time kindles the flame of doctrine, and restores the church, and calls many to salvation, and wonderfully governs the outcomes of struggles, which it is not at all necessary for us to foresee. It is our duty to understand the command of God, and to obey it, and we know that these labors please God, are aided divinely, are salutary to the church, and although various offenses occur, nevertheless in the end they have a joyful καταστροφὴν.

Moses, leading out such a great multitude from Egypt, knew that the divine voice must be obeyed, he knew also that these labors would not be in vain, but he did not foresee all the events, and he was not at all thinking that such a great multitude would have to be led around for forty years among the rocks of the Arabs and in a vast wilderness without a fixed dwelling. Thus let us also obey the command of God, and let us not doubt that He Himself will govern the outcomes.

Let us also warn those censors and areopagites of ours, that they should consider that they are greatly mistaken if they think that by omitting the preaching of the Gospel they will obtain tranquility, and that there will not come that there are calamities by which God punishes the crimes of the world.

A civil war was arising in Greece, in which the principal cities were almost utterly destroyed, Plataea, Corcyra, Athens, Sparta. It was a fatal punishment for impiety and lusts, and for this very reason more sorrowful, because the voice of the Gospel was entirely unknown, which would have freed some from eternal punishments.

It is a benefit of divine mercy to kindle the light of the Gospel when changes of affairs are impending, as many examples show. Thus now there is no doubt that great changes of human affairs are impending. Wherefore, so that the remnants of the church may be sustained by true invocation, and punishments may be mitigated, God has willed the Gospel to be purified.

The voice of the Gospel did not draw the Turks into Pannonia, to speak of recent examples, but a huge long-time εἰδωλομανία, and many other crimes. What now has that wisdom of the Areopagites, which commands the Gospel to be silenced, profited the Pannonians? What consolation do those ignorant of the Gospel have? On the contrary, to the pious the light of the Gospel brings great consolation. These, although they see that they are oppressed by the most bitter servitude, yet they know that the church will not perish utterly. They will also mitigate these evils with their prayers, with true piety.

Our censors err if they think their opinions are the tablets of the Fates, from which not even God may depart. I will destroy, he says, the wisdom of the wise. Nor has this age seen few examples. Therefore, let us rather give thanks to God the eternal Father of our liberator Jesus Christ, because he has again kindled for us the light of the Gospel, and let us rouse ourselves to true invocation and piety, and let us not be deterred by the false judgments of the impious, let us embrace and retain the voice of the Gospel, let us love and venerate the true church, let us know that the pious are not called to slothful leisure, but to the most difficult warfare of all.

The enemies, therefore, must be known, and the signs of the enemies must be known. The devil lies in wait for all men in various ways; he entangles some in various crimes, lusts, murders, rapacity; he pours false opinions about religion over many. Against this enemy, every individual must fight assiduously, and the pious know what the method of battling is. The doctrine handed down by God must be held, and help must be sought by faith From God, as Christ commanded, Ask, and you shall receive, and minds must be strengthened, so that they do not yield to evil enticements.

The devil also has his instruments; Mahomet openly wages war against Christ, and it is easy to know the hostile signs. For because he rejects the entire doctrine, handed down through the prophets and apostles, there is no doubt that he is an enemy of God. Therefore, the doctrine of Mahomet must be fled and execrated.

The devil also has other instruments, the Epicureans, who bend all religions to their own utility, because they think they are fabricated.

Likewise, in the very assembly which retains the title of the church of God, the pontiffs and doctors who defend idols, and lead minds away from true invocation. That kingdom also must be included under the name of Antichrist, and avoided.

The venerable name of the church greatly moves some, which men are accustomed to attribute to place and order, therefore they do not think there are pernicious errors in that assembly. But the voice of Christ clearly warns us that in this very assembly, which professes itself to be the people of Christ, there will be dissensions concerning doctrine. Then if anyone says, Behold, here is Christ, or there, do not believe it. For many false Christs and false prophets will arise, and will perform signs, so as to seduce, if it were possible, even the elect. These things are said concerning those impostors who wish to be considered members of the church, not concerning Diocletian, or others who openly wage war with the name of Christ.

And2. Peter 2.Among you, he says, there will be those who will teach false things. There will therefore be errors, not only among external enemies, Diocletian, Mahomet, and the like, but also in that very assembly which is called the church of God.

Thus also Paul says inActs chap. 20.I know that after my departure ravenous wolves will penetrate to you, etc. He clearly says that in that assembly which is called the church of God, wolves will rage. Therefore there have been and will be great and pernicious errors among those who retain the name of the church. And yet there will be those who will resist these errors. It is necessary for errors to be judged, and for the signs of the enemies of God to be recognized. Nor is there any other norm for this judgment, except the Gospel itself. As Paul says, If anyone teaches another Gospel, let him be anathema.

Now if it is established that the pontiffs and their teachers defend errors against the Gospel, necessarily are to be refuted and avoided. As Christ says, Do not believe. These things must be opposed to those who wish to appear ἀναμάρτητοι, for the reason that they attribute to themselves the title of the church. Therefore, let each person set before himself clear articles, so that he may be able to judge truly concerning the church. For it is necessary for the pious to be certain which is the church of God.

ANTITHESES.

I THE TRUE church of God has always, from Adam onwards, called upon the one eternal God, the creator of all things, the father of the promised redeemer, who revealed himself in his word, and according to this word wishes to be called upon with trust in the redeemer, and by his holy spirit to render light and eternal life to those thus calling upon him. And he has forbidden himself to be worshipped otherwise than according to his word, and for other gods or mediators to be fashioned.

With this first article of the Gospel, these rites manifestly fight: to call upon dead men, to bind God or those dead men to certain statues, or to any thing to which God has not bound himself by his word. These things are similar to the madness of the heathens, and truly sprung from the devil, who strives to afflict the glory of God with insult, and to mock the weak nature of men.

II. THE CHURCH of God always from that time even from Adam, judged far otherwise concerning sin, and concerning righteousness, than human philosophy judges, namely.

The true Church always acknowledged doubt concerning God, and stubbornness born with us, to be truly an evil fighting against the law of God.

On the contrary, the doctors of the papal kingdom deny these to be evils fighting against the law of God.

THE CHURCH true always from that time even from Adam felt, that in conversion or repentance it is necessary that there exist in the souls of men, true terrors and sorrow arising from the recognition of the wrath of God against sin, and again that souls be raised up by faith, that is, by trust in the mercy shown in the promises on account of the mediator, and that men obtain the remission of sins, on account of the mediator by faith freely, not on account of the fulfillment of the law.

Against these the doctors of the papal kingdom contend, that it must always be doubted, whether we obtain remission, and if it happens to anyone, it is given on account of sufficient contrition, as they call it. And since you never know whether contrition is sufficient, they command you to always doubt. This is in reality to abolish the promise handed down in the Gospel.

THE TRUE church, always from that time even from Adam, has felt this weak nature of men, that it is by no means able to satisfy the law of God, but that those doing penance and raised up by faith, begin love and obedience, and have the righteousness of a good conscience, and yet are not righteous, that is, reconciled and accepted to eternal life, on account of the fulfillment of the law, but on account of the mediator by faith. By this faith or trust the true church has always called upon God.

Against these things the doctors of the papal kingdom have most manifestly written, that man is able to satisfy the law of God, and to be righteous by the fulfillment of the law. Then, so that they may overwhelm the doctrine concerning faith, they say that it must always be doubted whether we are in grace, and they deny that doubts, and other vicious inclinations, fight against the law of God.

In this whole article the adversaries transform the Gospel into philosophy. But we also know, and with the sharpest zeal we teach, that discipline is required divinely, and that the violation of discipline is punished. But yet the righteousness of faith is one thing, discipline or the governance of morals is another. Since the adversaries overwhelm the doctrine of faith, they bury Christ, they obscure the promise delivered in the Gospel, they corrupt the doctrine of true invocation, and there is so much darkness among them, that they contend that by the word of faith is not signified trust in mercy, but only knowledge of history, joined with doubt concerning grace.

Concerning canonical satisfaction, and indulgences, they teach mere trifles.

The Lord's Supper is horribly profaned by many errors. The adversaries invent transubstantiation, and falsely say that they offer the Son of God, and that this offering of theirs merits for others from the work performed. These profanations horribly provoke the wrath of God.

The true church of God has always, from Adam onwards, held that works commanded by God are worship of God, that is, duties pleasing to God, and by which God declares Himself to be honored, and that works devised without the command of God are not worship of God. For if these were worship, even the prodigious worship of the heathens could be excused.

By this error the adversaries invent monasticism and celibacy, to be an excellent worship of God, they prohibit marriage to the whole order of priests, and they tore spouses apart. And since the law of celibacy is unjust and impossible, it is a destruction to many, whose minds and bodies are polluted on account of this bond, and whose souls are hindered by this one law, so that they cannot rightly call upon God.
